Massless Maxwell fields on sub-extremal Kerr–Newman exteriors
Bobby Eka Gunara, Mulyanto, Emir Syahreza Fadhilla, Fiki Taufik Akbar

Abstract
We study the massless Maxwell system on fixed sub–extremal Kerr–Newman exteriors. In the weak Kerr–Newman regimes with 0 < | a | ≤ c a ​ M 0<|a|\leq c_{a}M , 0 < | Q | ≤ c Q ​ M 0<|Q|\leq c_{Q}M , and c a , c Q ∈ ( 0 , 1 ) c_{a},c_{Q}\in(0,1) , we prove non–degenerate boundedness, Morawetz / integrated local energy decay, the outgoing r p r^{p} hierarchy, radiation fields, polynomial decay, and two–sided scattering for the stationary–subtracted Maxwell field. In the axisymmetric relative slow–rotation regime 0 < | a | ≤ ε KN ​ | Q | < M 0<|a|\leq\varepsilon_{\mathrm{KN}}|Q|<M with ε KN ∈ ( 0 , 1 ) \varepsilon_{\mathrm{KN}}\in(0,1) , the same conclusions hold on the axisymmetric sector, with no perturbative restriction on | Q | / M |Q|/M beyond sub–extremality. For the full sub–extremal range a 2 + Q 2 < M 2 a^{2}+Q^{2}<M^{2} , by adding one order–zero radiative local–energy estimate for this master system, we could obtain similar results as the weak regimes. This separates the unconditional weak theory from the conditional full–range theory within one reconstruction/scattering framework.



1
Introduction and main results

1.1
Setting, stationary subtraction, and the radiative problem

We study the massless Maxwell system on fixed sub–extremal Kerr–Newman exteriors,

dF=0,\qquad\nabla^{\mu}F_{\mu\nu}=\mathcal{J}_{\nu},

with conserved source
\nabla^{\nu}\mathcal{J}_{\nu}=0
. The stationary obstruction to decay is the two–parameter electric–magnetic Kerr–Newman family determined by the asymptotic charges. Accordingly, every boundedness, decay, and scattering statement in the paper is formulated after the subtraction

F_{\mathrm{rad}}:=F-F_{\mathrm{stat}}^{\mathrm{KN}}(q_{E},q_{B}),

so that the analysis is carried out on the charge–free radiative sector.

In the present version the paper has two fixed–background layers. First, on the weak Kerr–Newman regimes currently covered by the available radiative theory, the final decay and scattering conclusions for the stationary–subtracted Maxwell field are unconditional. The principal weak theorem package concerns the general non–axisymmetric doubly small regime

0<|a|\leq c_{a}M,\qquad 0<|Q|\leq c_{Q}M,

with the smallness constants
c_{a},c_{Q}\in(0,1)
supplied later by Theorem
3.10
(2); as comparison material, the paper also records the axisymmetric relative slow–rotation regime
0<|a|\leq\varepsilon_{\mathrm{KN}}|Q|<M
. Second, on the full sub–extremal range

a^{2}+Q^{2}<M^{2},

we prove the structural short–range reduction together with a conditional reconstruction/scattering theory whose only external analytic hypothesis is a single order–zero radiative local–energy input for the master equation.

The paper has three objectives. The first is structural: construct gauge–invariant radiative variables for
F_{\mathrm{rad}}
that satisfy a horizon–regular short–range master system. The second is analytic: propagate the available weak Kerr–Newman radiative hierarchy through a physical–space argument that furnishes non–degenerate boundedness, Morawetz / integrated local energy decay, conformal control, and the outgoing
r^{p}
hierarchy at all commuted orders. The third is tensorial: reconstruct the full Maxwell tensor from the radiative variables without loss of top derivatives and upgrade the resulting master estimates to a decay and scattering theory for the stationary–subtracted Maxwell field itself.

Accordingly, the introduction first states the unconditional weak theorem package actually closed in the present paper, and then records the companion full sub–extremal conditional framework proved later in the body. We first fix the setting and notation, then position the paper within the existing literature, state the principal weak theorems, and finally give a concise proof roadmap. The broader full–range framework is kept as precise reference material, but it is not part of the unconditional weak theorem package.

1.2
Notation and conventions

For the reader’s convenience, we collect here the notation used in the introduction. The exact definitions of the energies, bulk terms, and scattering spaces are supplied later in the paper; in Section 1 they are used only as names for the canonical quantities furnished by the theorem package. When the master variable is realized as a finite collection rather than a single scalar, every norm below is understood componentwise and summed over that collection.

1.3
Relation to previous work

At the fixed–background spin-
1
level there are two fully understood boundary regimes. In the charged non–rotating case, Sterbenz and Tataru established local energy decay for Maxwell on generic spherically symmetric black–hole backgrounds, which in particular covers the full sub–extremal Reissner–Nordström family
[
1
]
. In the uncharged rotating case, Benomio and Teixeira da Costa proved a full physical–space decay and scattering theory for Maxwell on the entire sub–extremal Kerr family, with the stationary two–charge decomposition built into the theorem statement
[
2
]
. On Kerr–Newman, Giorgi derived the coupled Teukolsky and generalized Regge–Wheeler systems for electromagnetic–gravitational perturbations
[
3
]
, established Carter-type commutation identities in physical space
[
4
]
, Hollands and Toomani constructed source-compatible radiation-gauge potentials
[
5
]
, and Giorgi together with Wan obtained boundedness and decay in the small-parameter and axisymmetric regimes
[
6
,
7
]
.

The contribution of the present paper is complementary and modular. It proves the short–range radiative reduction and same–order reconstruction at the fixed–background Maxwell level, develops the physical–space reconstruction and scattering argument needed to transfer radiative bounds back to the full Maxwell tensor, and then combines that structure with the available weak Kerr–Newman radiative estimates to obtain unconditional tensorial decay and scattering. The broader full–range framework is retained only as reference material, because it clarifies how the same structural package would interact with stronger future radiative inputs.

1.4
Main conceptual contributions

The paper is organized around three structural contributions.
First, it proves a short–range radiative reduction for the charge–free Maxwell field on fixed Kerr–Newman backgrounds: after stationary subtraction, one passes to gauge–invariant radiative variables that satisfy a horizon–regular short–range master system and still determine the Maxwell field by same–order finite–time reconstruction.
Second, it develops a physical–space framework that turns radiative boundedness, Morawetz / integrated local energy decay, weighted
r^{p}
control, and conformal estimates for the master system into a canonical hierarchy of norms suited to tensorial reconstruction and scattering.
Third, it completes the transfer from radiative estimates to tensorial Maxwell estimates, including quantitative no–loss reconstruction, radiation fields on
\mathcal{I}^{\pm}\cup\mathcal{H}^{\pm}
, and two–sided scattering.

These ingredients are already enough to produce a clean unconditional theorem package on weak Kerr–Newman exteriors. The broader conditional template and its full–range Step 6 issue are relegated to a separate reference subsection and to Appendix
A
, so that the principal theorem package stated next remains sharp and purely weak–regime.

1.5
Main results

The principal unconditional fixed–background theorem package of the present version lies entirely in the weak regime. It consists of the structural short–range reduction theorem, an unconditional radiative estimate package for the resulting master system on weak Kerr–Newman exteriors, a tensorial decay theorem for the stationary–subtracted Maxwell field, and a separate sharp weak–regime scattering theorem. A later optional subsection records the companion full sub–extremal conditional framework proved in Section
4
; that reference material explains the full logical architecture but is not part of the unconditional weak theorem package.

The structural theorem is formulated so as not to depend on a particular realization of the radiative sector. Depending on the weak background under consideration, one may work with a single Fackerell–Ipser scalar or with any equivalent finite collection of Teukolsky, generalized Regge–Wheeler, or hidden–symmetry variables. For notational economy, the later radiative estimates are written in scalar form; for a finite collection, every norm and estimate is interpreted componentwise and summed over the collection.

Theorem 1.1

(Structural short–range radiative reduction and finite–time reconstruction)
.

Let
F
solve the sourced Maxwell system with conserved source
\mathcal{J}
on a fixed sub–extremal Kerr–Newman exterior, let
F_{\mathrm{stat}}^{\mathrm{KN}}(q_{E},q_{B})
be the stationary electric–magnetic Kerr–Newman family of Proposition
3.1
, and define

F_{\mathrm{rad}}:=F-F_{\mathrm{stat}}^{\mathrm{KN}}(q_{E},q_{B}).

Then there exists a finite collection

U_{M}=(U_{M}^{(1)},\dots,U_{M}^{(N)})

of gauge–invariant radiative variables, each obtained from the exact Kerr–Newman radiative variables by a background differential operator of order at most one with smooth stationary–axisymmetric coefficients, such that:

1.

U_{M}
vanishes identically on the stationary electric–magnetic family
F_{\mathrm{stat}}^{\mathrm{KN}}(q_{E},q_{B})
;

2.

F_{\mathrm{rad}}
, equivalently the exact radiative variables, can be recovered from
U_{M}
by a closed system of transport and angular elliptic equations with smooth background coefficients and with no loss of top derivatives; and

3.

U_{M}
satisfies a second–order short–range system of the form

\big(\Box_{g_{\mathrm{KN}}}I_{N}+\mathbb{A}^{a}\nabla_{a}+\mathbb{V}\big)U_{M}=\mathbb{S}(\mathcal{J}),

where
\mathbb{A}^{a}
and
\mathbb{V}
are smooth matrix–valued coefficients,
\mathbb{S}
is first order in
\mathcal{J}
, and in advanced coordinates all derivatives of
\mathbb{A}^{a}
and
\mathbb{V}
satisfy the short–range bounds

\displaystyle\partial^{\alpha}\mathbb{A}^{a}=O(r^{-2-|\alpha|}),

\displaystyle\partial^{\alpha}\mathbb{V}=O(r^{-3-|\alpha|})

\displaystyle(r\to\infty).

On the Kerr edge one may take the classical Fackerell–Ipser scalar, on the Reissner–Nordström edge the electric/magnetic Regge–Wheeler variables, and on general Kerr–Newman one may take a finite coupled Teukolsky/generalized Regge–Wheeler collection. In every case the short–range reduction and the same–order finite–time reconstruction belong to the rigorous theorem package.

Theorem 1.2

(Radiative package on weak Kerr–Newman exteriors)
.

There exist dimensionless constants
c_{a},c_{Q}\in(0,1)
such that the following holds. Let
(\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{KN}},g_{\mathrm{KN}})
be a fixed sub–extremal Kerr–Newman exterior satisfying

0<|a|\leq c_{a}M,\qquad 0<|Q|\leq c_{Q}M.

Let
u
be any admissible short–range master variable furnished by Theorem
1.1
for the stationary–subtracted Maxwell field. Then for every integer
k\geq 0
there exist non–degenerate energies
\mathcal{E}_{0}^{(k)}[u](\tau)
, Morawetz bulks
\mathcal{M}_{0}^{(k)}[u]([0,\tau])
, conformal/far–field quantities
\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{C},0}^{(k)}[u](\tau)
, and an outgoing
r^{p}
hierarchy such that every homogeneous solution of

(\Box_{g_{\mathrm{KN}}}+\mathcal{V}_{M})u=0

satisfies, for all
\tau\geq 0
,

\mathcal{E}_{0}^{(k)}[u](\tau)+\mathcal{M}_{0}^{(k)}[u]([0,\tau])\leq C_{k}\,\mathcal{E}_{0}^{(k)}[u](0)

and

\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{C},0}^{(k)}[u](\tau)\leq C_{k}(1+\tau)^{2}\Big(\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{C},0}^{(k)}[u](0)+\mathcal{E}_{0}^{(k)}[u](0)\Big).

Moreover, for each
0\leq p\leq 2
the associated outgoing
r^{p}
hierarchy controls the radiation field at null infinity. In particular, the full commuted non–degenerate boundedness, Morawetz / integrated local energy decay, and outgoing
r^{p}
hierarchy for the radiative master system hold unconditionally throughout the weak regime.

Theorem 1.3

(Maxwell decay and physical–space bounds on weak Kerr–Newman exteriors)
.

There exist dimensionless constants
c_{a},c_{Q}\in(0,1)
such that the following holds. Let
(\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{KN}},g_{\mathrm{KN}})
be a fixed sub–extremal Kerr–Newman exterior satisfying

0<|a|\leq c_{a}M,\qquad 0<|Q|\leq c_{Q}M.

Let
F
solve the sourceless Maxwell system

dF=0,\qquad\nabla^{\mu}F_{\mu\nu}=0,

and define the stationary–subtracted field

F_{\mathrm{rad}}:=F-F_{\mathrm{stat}}^{\mathrm{KN}}\big(q_{E}[F],q_{B}[F]\big).

Then for every
k\geq 6
and every
\tau\geq 0
,

\|F_{\mathrm{rad}}\|_{\mathcal{X}_{\mathrm{Max},0}^{(k)}([0,\tau])}^{2}\leq C_{k}\,\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{Max},0}^{(k)}[F_{\mathrm{rad}}](0).

Consequently the stationary–subtracted Maxwell field enjoys non–degenerate energy boundedness, Morawetz / integrated local energy decay, the outgoing
r^{p}
hierarchy and its radiation control at null infinity, and polynomial pointwise decay. The sharp weak–regime scattering statement is recorded separately in Theorem
1.4
.

If instead
F
solves the forced Maxwell system with conserved source
\mathcal{J}
and one performs a charge–compatible stationary subtraction, then for every
k\geq 6
and every
0\leq\tau\leq\tau_{*}
,

\displaystyle\|F_{\mathrm{rad}}\|_{\mathcal{X}_{\mathrm{Max},0}^{(k)}([0,\tau])}^{2}\leq C_{k}\Big(\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{Max},0}^{(k)}[F_{\mathrm{rad}}](0)+\int_{0}^{\tau}\!\!\|\mathcal{S}_{M}(\mathcal{J})\|_{\mathcal{N}_{0}^{*}}^{2}(\sigma)\,d\sigma\Big).

In particular, the same tensorial boundedness, integrated decay, and outgoing
r^{p}
statements hold with quantitative dependence on the forcing term.

Theorem 1.4

(Scattering on weak Kerr–Newman exteriors)
.

There exist dimensionless constants
c_{a},c_{Q}\in(0,1)
such that the following holds. Let
(\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{KN}},g_{\mathrm{KN}})
be a fixed sub–extremal Kerr–Newman exterior satisfying

0<|a|\leq c_{a}M,\qquad 0<|Q|\leq c_{Q}M.

Let
F
solve the sourceless Maxwell system and let

F_{\mathrm{rad}}:=F-F_{\mathrm{stat}}^{\mathrm{KN}}\big(q_{E}[F],q_{B}[F]\big).

Then for every
k\geq 6
the charge–free Maxwell radiation fields of
F_{\mathrm{rad}}
exist on
\mathcal{I}^{\pm}\cup\mathcal{H}^{\pm}
, the Cauchy–to–radiation maps extend to bounded isomorphisms

\mathscr{S}_{\mathrm{Max},0}^{\pm}:\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{Max},0}^{(k)}(\Sigma_{0})\xrightarrow{\ \sim\ }\mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{Max},\pm}^{(k)},

the wave operators

\displaystyle\mathscr{W}_{\mathrm{Max},0}^{\pm}:=(\mathscr{S}_{\mathrm{Max},0}^{\pm})^{-1}:\mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{Max},\pm}^{(k)}\xrightarrow{\ \sim\ }\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{Max},0}^{(k)}(\Sigma_{0})

are bounded, and the scattering operator

\displaystyle\mathscr{A}_{\mathrm{Max},0}:=\mathscr{S}_{\mathrm{Max},0}^{+}\circ(\mathscr{S}_{\mathrm{Max},0}^{-})^{-1}:\mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{Max},-}^{(k)}\xrightarrow{\ \sim\ }\mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{Max},+}^{(k)}

is a bounded isomorphism. Equivalently, the linear final–state problem on
\mathcal{I}^{\pm}\cup\mathcal{H}^{\pm}
is well posed and the stationary–subtracted Maxwell field admits a complete two–sided scattering theory throughout the weak Kerr–Newman regime.

Remark 1.5

(Scope of the main theorem package)
.

Theorems
1.1
,
1.2
,
1.3
, and
1.4
are the principal fixed–background results of the paper. They are unconditional and belong entirely to the weak non–axisymmetric Kerr–Newman regime
0<|a|\leq c_{a}M
,
0<|Q|\leq c_{Q}M
. Everything beyond that scope is recorded separately as comparison material.

1.6
Logical structure of the paper

The paper is written in two layers. The principal layer is the unconditional weak–Kerr–Newman theorem package consisting of Theorems
1.1
,
1.2
,
1.3
, and
1.4
. The secondary layer is the broader full sub–extremal conditional framework collected in Subsection
1.7
and proved later in Section
4
; Appendix
A
records the remaining comparison material.

For the principal theorem package there is only one external fixed–background analytic input: the weak radiative estimate package recorded later as Theorem
3.10
(2). Everything else needed for Theorems
1.2
,
1.3
, and
1.4
is proved in Sections
2
,
3
, and
4
. In particular, no unproved full–range trapping statement enters the proof of the main theorems in the weak regime.

A reader primarily interested in the unconditional weak result may therefore read the paper as follows: first the present introduction through Theorem
1.4
; then Section
2
; then Section
3
, which proves Theorem
1.1
and the weak Kerr–Newman consequences; then the transfer/scattering part of Section
4
; and finally Section
5
. The optional reference framework in Subsection
1.7
and Appendix
A
may be skipped on a first reading.

Remark 1.6

(Logical dependency of the principal weak–regime theorems)
.

Theorem
1.1
is the structural input: it constructs the short–range radiative master system and the same–order finite–time reconstruction of the charge–free Maxwell field. Theorem
1.2
combines that structural reduction with the weak–regime radiative verification supplied by Theorem
3.10
(2). Theorem
1.3
records the resulting tensorial physical–space decay consequences in the weak regime, while Theorem
1.4
isolates the corresponding two–sided scattering statement. Both weak tensorial theorems are proved in Section
3
by inserting the same weak radiative input into the conditional reconstruction and scattering theory established in Section
4
, together with the weak fixed–background consequence recorded later as Theorem
1.15
.

Remark 1.7

(Organization of the body of the paper)
.

Section
2
fixes the Kerr–Newman geometry, the source conventions, and the background differential operators used throughout. Section
3
proves the structural reduction and the weak Kerr–Newman consequences that form the principal theorem package. Section
4
develops the full sub–extremal conditional physical–space hierarchy, the no–loss reconstruction theory, and the scattering theory used by the weak package. Section
5
summarizes the present weak–Kerr–Newman result and places it in the broader fixed–background Kerr–Newman literature. The optional reference framework in Subsection
1.7
and Appendix
A
record the wider conditional architecture needed only for comparison.

1.7
Conditional results on sub-extremal Kerr-Newman exteriors

The next statements are retained only for reference because Sections
3
and
4
develop the argument in a modular form that can be transplanted to other parameter regimes. They are not part of the principal weak–regime theorem package stated above. Instead, they record the broader conditional template and the non-axisymmetric comparison theorem used to place the weak result within the wider Kerr–Newman program. In particular, we consider all sub-extremal Kerr–Newman with
a^{2}+Q^{2}<M^{2}
.

The radiative theorem below is stated in scalar notation, but by the previous theorem it may always be realized either by a single Fackerell–Ipser scalar or, more generally, by any equivalent finite collection of short–range master variables.

Theorem 1.8

(Conditional radiative template for the short–range master equation on Kerr–Newman)
.

Let
k\geq 0
. Suppose that the order–zero Step 6 estimate (
4.46
) for the sourced short–range master equation holds. Then for solutions of

\big(\Box_{g_{\mathrm{KN}}}+\mathcal{V}_{M}\big)u=0

and for their commutations by the scalar–master symmetry algebra generated by
T
,
\Phi
, and
\mathcal{O}
from Proposition
4.2
(or by any equivalent finite extension of that algebra) there exist:

•

a non–degenerate energy
\mathcal{E}_{0}^{(k)}[u](\tau)
;

•

a Morawetz bulk
\mathcal{M}_{0}^{(k)}[u]([0,\tau])
;

•

a conformal–type energy
\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{C},0}^{(k)}[u](\tau)
; and

•

a dual source norm
\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{N}_{0}^{*}}(\tau)
,

such that

\mathcal{E}_{0}^{(k)}[u](\tau)+\mathcal{M}_{0}^{(k)}[u]([0,\tau])\leq C_{0}\,\mathcal{E}_{0}^{(k)}[u](0)

(1.1)

and

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{C},0}^{(k)}[u](\tau)\leq C_{0}(1+\tau)^{2}\Big(\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{C},0}^{(k)}[u](0)+\mathcal{E}_{0}^{(k)}[u](0)\Big).

(1.2)

Moreover, for each
0\leq p\leq 2
the package contains an outgoing
r^{p}
hierarchy with weighted energies, spacetime bulks, and null fluxes sufficient to control the radiation field at null infinity.

Order of the seed versus order of the conclusion.
Theorem
1.8
yields estimates at arbitrary commuted order
k
, but its only external assumption is the order–zero estimate (
4.46
). Proposition
4.20
derives the one–level inhomogeneous redshift–Morawetz estimate from that seed, Proposition
4.27
supplies the one–level far–field weighted estimate, and Propositions
4.22
and
4.29
then generate the full commuted hierarchy. The lower bound
k\geq 4
first enters in Theorem
1.9
, where no–loss tensorial reconstruction and Sobolev estimates require additional derivatives. In particular, Theorem
1.8
is conditional only through (
4.46
); Theorem
3.10
later records the parameter regimes in which that same estimate is already available from the literature.

Theorem 1.9

(Conditional quantitative reconstruction and scattering for the charge–free Maxwell field)
.

Fix
k\geq 4
and assume the order–zero Step 6 estimate (
4.46
). Then the reconstruction and scattering stage can be carried out with no further external input. More precisely:

1.

Canonical master norm.
There exists a norm
\|u\|_{\mathcal{X}_{M,0}^{(k)}([0,\tau])}
equivalent to the non–degenerate energy, Morawetz bulk, the Step 10 conformal quantity
\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{C},0}^{(k)}
, and the outgoing Step 10
r^{p}
family, such that every solution of

(\Box_{g_{\mathrm{KN}}}+\mathcal{V}_{M})u=\mathcal{G}

satisfies

\displaystyle\|u\|_{\mathcal{X}_{M,0}^{(k)}([0,\tau])}^{2}\leq C_{k}\Big(\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{C},0}^{(k)}[u](0)+\int_{0}^{\tau}\!\!\|\mathcal{G}\|_{\mathcal{N}_{0}^{*}}^{2}(\sigma)\,d\sigma\Big).

(1.3)

2.

Quantitative no–loss reconstruction and tensorial coercivity.
There exist a non–degenerate order–
k
initial Maxwell energy
\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{Max},0}^{(k)}[G](0)
on the charge–free sector and a tensorial physical–space norm
\|G\|_{\mathcal{X}_{\mathrm{Max},0}^{(k)}([0,\tau])}
with the following property: for every smooth charge–free Maxwell field
G
with associated master variable
u_{G}
,

\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{C},0}^{(k)}[u_{G}](0)\leq C_{k}\,\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{Max},0}^{(k)}[G](0)

(1.4)

and

\displaystyle\|G\|_{\mathcal{X}_{\mathrm{Max},0}^{(k)}([0,\tau])}\leq C_{k}\Big(\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{Max},0}^{(k)}[G](0)^{1/2}+\|u_{G}\|_{\mathcal{X}_{M,0}^{(k)}([0,\tau])}\Big).

(1.5)

In particular, the finite–time reconstruction from
u_{G}
to
G
is quantitative and without top–order derivative loss. In the sourceless case,
\mathcal{X}_{\mathrm{Max},0}^{(k)}
contains enough local spacetime control to imply polynomial pointwise decay by Sobolev.

3.

Master scattering theory.
The Cauchy–to–radiation maps for the homogeneous master equation extend to isomorphisms

\mathscr{S}_{M,0}^{\pm}:\mathcal{H}_{M,0}^{(k)}(\Sigma_{0})\xrightarrow{\ \sim\ }\mathcal{R}_{M,\pm}^{(k)},

and the corresponding linear final–state problem on
\mathcal{I}^{\pm}\cup\mathcal{H}^{\pm}
is well posed.

4.

Asymptotic Maxwell reconstruction and scattering.
There exist bounded linear maps

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{\infty}^{\pm}:\mathcal{R}_{M,\pm}^{(k)}\longrightarrow\mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{Max},\pm}^{(k)},

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}_{\infty}^{\pm}:\mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{Max},\pm}^{(k)}\longrightarrow\mathcal{R}_{M,\pm}^{(k)},

inverse to one another on the charge–free sector and compatible with finite–time reconstruction. Consequently the charge–free Maxwell Cauchy–to–radiation maps

\mathscr{S}_{\mathrm{Max},0}^{\pm}:\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{Max},0}^{(k)}(\Sigma_{0})\xrightarrow{\ \sim\ }\mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{Max},\pm}^{(k)}

are bounded isomorphisms, and the corresponding Maxwell final–state problem on
\mathcal{I}^{\pm}\cup\mathcal{H}^{\pm}
is well posed.

Theorems
1.8
and
1.9
together yield a tensorial template relevant to the Maxwell field itself. Theorem
1.10
records that template on general sub–extremal Kerr–Newman exteriors, still conditional only on the Step 6 seed. The principal unconditional fixed–background consequence emphasized in the present version is its weak non–axisymmetric specialization, stated later as Theorem
1.15
.

Theorem 1.10

(Conditional tensorial physical–space decay and scattering template for charge–free Maxwell on Kerr–Newman)
.

Let
(\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{KN}},g_{\mathrm{KN}})
be a fixed sub–extremal Kerr–Newman exterior and let
F
solve the sourceless Maxwell system

dF=0,\qquad\nabla^{\mu}F_{\mu\nu}=0.

Let
q_{E}[F]
and
q_{B}[F]
denote the asymptotic electric and magnetic charges of
F
. Define the stationary–subtracted field

F_{\mathrm{rad}}:=F-F_{\mathrm{stat}}^{\mathrm{KN}}\big(q_{E}[F],q_{B}[F]\big),

where
F_{\mathrm{stat}}^{\mathrm{KN}}
is the stationary electric–magnetic Kerr–Newman family from Proposition
3.1
. Suppose that the order–zero Step 6 estimate (
4.46
) holds. Then for every
k\geq 6
and every
\tau\geq 0
,

\displaystyle\|F_{\mathrm{rad}}\|_{\mathcal{X}_{\mathrm{Max},0}^{(k)}([0,\tau])}^{2}\leq C_{\mathrm{Max}}\,

(1.6)

\displaystyle\qquad\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{Max},0}^{(k)}[F_{\mathrm{rad}}](0).

(1.7)

Equivalently, the tensorial radiative Maxwell field enjoys on the full sub–extremal Kerr–Newman exterior:

1.

non–degenerate energy boundedness;

2.

Morawetz / integrated local energy decay;

3.

the outgoing
r^{p}
hierarchy and the corresponding radiation control at null infinity; and

4.

polynomial pointwise decay for the field and its commuted derivatives, as supplied by Proposition
4.51
.

Moreover,
F_{\mathrm{rad}}
admits radiation fields on
\mathcal{I}^{\pm}\cup\mathcal{H}^{\pm}
, and the Cauchy–to–radiation maps extend to isomorphisms

\mathscr{S}^{\pm}_{\mathrm{Max},0}:\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{Max},0}^{(k)}(\Sigma_{0})\xrightarrow{\ \sim\ }\mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{Max},\pm}^{(k)},

where
\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{Max},0}^{(k)}(\Sigma_{0})
and
\mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{Max},\pm}^{(k)}
are the charge–free Maxwell Cauchy and asymptotic data spaces constructed in Definition
4.56
and Proposition
4.55
. The corresponding linear final–state problem on
\mathcal{I}^{\pm}\cup\mathcal{H}^{\pm}
is well posed. Consequently, every finite–energy sourceless Maxwell field on fixed sub–extremal Kerr–Newman decomposes uniquely as

F=F_{\mathrm{stat}}^{\mathrm{KN}}\big(q_{E}[F],q_{B}[F]\big)+F_{\mathrm{rad}},

where the radiative part satisfies non–degenerate boundedness, Morawetz/ILED, the outgoing
r^{p}
hierarchy, polynomial pointwise decay, and two–sided scattering.

Corollary 1.11

(Forced tensorial Maxwell estimates under charge–compatible forcing)
.

Let
(\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{KN}},g_{\mathrm{KN}})
be a fixed sub–extremal Kerr–Newman exterior and let
F
solve

\displaystyle dF=0,

\displaystyle\nabla^{\mu}F_{\mu\nu}=\mathcal{J}_{\nu},

\displaystyle\nabla^{\nu}\mathcal{J}_{\nu}=0

on the slab bounded by
\Sigma_{0}
and
\Sigma_{\tau_{*}}
. Fix
(q_{E},q_{B})\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
and define

F_{\mathrm{rad}}:=F-F_{\mathrm{stat}}^{\mathrm{KN}}(q_{E},q_{B}).

Assume the order–zero Step 6 estimate (
4.46
), and suppose that this subtraction places
F_{\mathrm{rad}}
in the charge–free sector on each slice
\Sigma_{\tau}
(for instance, because the chosen stationary field matches the asymptotic charges and the source carries no net asymptotic charge flux through the exterior). Then for every
k\geq 6
and every
0\leq\tau\leq\tau_{*}
,

\displaystyle\|F_{\mathrm{rad}}\|_{\mathcal{X}_{\mathrm{Max},0}^{(k)}([0,\tau])}^{2}\leq C_{\mathrm{Max}}\Big(\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{Max},0}^{(k)}[F_{\mathrm{rad}}](0)+\int_{0}^{\tau}\!\!\|\mathcal{S}_{M}(\mathcal{J})\|_{\mathcal{N}_{0}^{*}}^{2}(\sigma)\,d\sigma\Big).

(1.8)

In particular, the same tensorial non–degenerate boundedness, Morawetz/ILED, outgoing
r^{p}
, and Sobolev pointwise control hold with quantitative dependence on the forcing term. Whenever the source norm on the right–hand side is finite on
[0,\infty)
, these bounds extend globally in time.

Together, Theorem
1.10
and Corollary
1.11
provide the complete fixed–background massless transfer statement used later in the paper.

Comparison with other verified regimes.

The reference framework recorded above is designed to interface directly with the best currently rigorous Kerr–Newman inputs. For the purposes of the principal theorem package, we adopt the weak non–axisymmetric regime
0<|a|\leq c_{a}M
,
0<|Q|\leq c_{Q}M
as the fixed–background scope of the paper. We also retain the axisymmetric relative slow–rotation regime as a comparison result, since it remains the strongest currently rigorous rotating charged theorem beyond the doubly small setting.

Remark 1.12

(Relation to the current Kerr–Newman program)
.

Theorem
1.10
is phrased as a general conditional template in the same physical–space form as the recent full sub–extremal Kerr Maxwell result and is designed to absorb the current Kerr–Newman inputs based on hidden symmetries, Carter commutators, radiation–gauge reconstruction, and coupled Teukolsky–Regge–Wheeler structures
[
2
,
3
,
4
,
5
,
7
]
.

The preceding theorem is conditional only through the Step 6 local–energy input singled out in Theorem
1.8
. Theorem
3.10
records interior Kerr–Newman regimes where that remaining input is already available from the literature. In the present version we then adopt its weak non–axisymmetric part, which turns the template theorem into the unconditional weak–Kerr–Newman statement recorded next.

Theorem 1.13

(Axisymmetric Maxwell decay and scattering on slowly rotating strongly charged Kerr–Newman)
.

There exists a dimensionless constant
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{KN}}\in(0,1)
such that the following holds. Let
(\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{KN}},g_{\mathrm{KN}})
be a fixed sub–extremal Kerr–Newman exterior satisfying

0<|a|\leq\varepsilon_{\mathrm{KN}}|Q|<M.

Let
F
solve the sourceless Maxwell system

dF=0,\qquad\nabla^{\mu}F_{\mu\nu}=0,

and assume that
F
is axisymmetric, i.e.
\mathcal{L}_{\Phi}F=0
. Define

F_{\mathrm{rad}}:=F-F_{\mathrm{stat}}^{\mathrm{KN}}\big(q_{E}[F],q_{B}[F]\big).

Then the conclusion of Theorem
1.10
holds without any further radiative hypothesis. In particular, for every
k\geq 6
and every
\tau\geq 0
,

\displaystyle\|F_{\mathrm{rad}}\|_{\mathcal{X}_{\mathrm{Max},0}^{(k)}([0,\tau])}^{2}\leq C_{\mathrm{axi},k}\,

(1.9)

\displaystyle\qquad\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{Max},0}^{(k)}[F_{\mathrm{rad}}](0).

(1.10)

Moreover, the Cauchy–to–radiation maps of Theorem
1.10
restrict to bounded isomorphisms on the axisymmetric charge–free sector, so the corresponding axisymmetric final–state problem on
\mathcal{I}^{\pm}\cup\mathcal{H}^{\pm}
is well posed. More generally, if
F
solves the forced Maxwell system with an axisymmetric conserved source and one performs a charge–compatible stationary subtraction, then the estimate (
1.8
) holds with no additional radiative hypothesis. This is a genuinely relative slow–rotation result: no perturbative assumption is imposed on
|Q|/M
, so the theorem covers the whole strong–charge sector
|Q|=O(M)
subject only to sub–extremality and axisymmetry.

Remark 1.14

(Explicit strongly charged subcones)
.

Fix
q_{*}\in(0,1)
. If a fixed sub–extremal axisymmetric Kerr–Newman background satisfies

q_{*}M\leq|Q|<M,\qquad 0<|a|\leq\varepsilon_{\mathrm{KN}}q_{*}M,

then automatically
|a|\leq\varepsilon_{\mathrm{KN}}|Q|
, so Theorem
1.13
applies. Thus the unconditional theorem is available on every strongly charged subcone with
|Q|/M\geq q_{*}
.

Theorem 1.15

(Maxwell decay and scattering on weak Kerr–Newman exteriors)
.

There exist dimensionless constants
c_{a},c_{Q}\in(0,1)
with the following property. Let
(\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{KN}},g_{\mathrm{KN}})
be a fixed sub–extremal Kerr–Newman exterior satisfying

0<|a|\leq c_{a}M,\qquad 0<|Q|\leq c_{Q}M.

Then the conclusions of Theorem
1.10
and Corollary
1.11
hold unconditionally for general, not necessarily axisymmetric, Maxwell fields and charge–compatible conserved sources.

Remark 1.16

(Scope of the present version)
.

The principal theorem package of the paper is Theorems
1.2
,
1.3
, and
1.4
. The axisymmetric relative theorem and the broader conditional framework are retained only as comparison material and to document how the weak result sits inside the wider Kerr–Newman program.

Proof roadmap for the main theorems.
Theorem
1.1
is proved in Section
3
. Theorem
1.2
is then obtained there by combining the structural reduction with Theorem
3.10
(2). Theorem
1.3
is the resulting weak tensorial decay theorem, and Theorem
1.4
is the corresponding weak scattering corollary; both are proved in Section
3
after inserting the same weak radiative input into the full sub–extremal reconstruction and scattering package established in Section
4
. The optional reference framework above is mathematically precise but is not part of the main result package.

2
Geometry and notation

Sub–extremal Kerr–Newman geometry

Let
(\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{KN}},g_{\mathrm{KN}})
denote the domain of outer communication of a Kerr–Newman black hole with mass
M>0
, angular momentum parameter
a
, and background charge
Q
. In Boyer–Lindquist coordinates
(t,r,\theta,\varphi)
define

\Sigma:=r^{2}+a^{2}\cos^{2}\theta,\qquad\Delta:=r^{2}-2Mr+a^{2}+Q^{2},

and assume the sub–extremal condition

a^{2}+Q^{2}<M^{2},\qquad r_{\pm}:=M\pm\sqrt{M^{2}-a^{2}-Q^{2}}.

The exterior corresponds to
r\in(r_{+},\infty)
and the metric is

\displaystyle g_{\mathrm{KN}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\Delta-a^{2}\sin^{2}\theta}{\Sigma}\,dt^{2}+\frac{2a\sin^{2}\theta(2Mr-Q^{2})}{\Sigma}\,dt\,d\varphi-\frac{\Sigma}{\Delta}\,dr^{2}-\Sigma\,d\theta^{2}

(2.1)

\displaystyle\qquad-\frac{\sin^{2}\theta}{\Sigma}\Big((r^{2}+a^{2})^{2}-\Delta a^{2}\sin^{2}\theta\Big)d\varphi^{2}.

The stationary and axial Killing fields are
T=\partial_{t}
and
\Phi=\partial_{\varphi}
. We fix a regular spacelike foliation
\{\Sigma_{\tau}\}_{\tau\geq 0}
of the exterior and use
\tau
as the evolution parameter in all energies below.

Remark 2.1

(Standing conventions on constants and sectors)
.

Unless explicitly stated otherwise, every constant denoted by
C
,
C_{k}
, or
C_{k,\ell}
may depend on the fixed background parameters
(M,a,Q)
, on the chosen foliation
\{\Sigma_{\tau}\}
, and on the number of commutations, but never on the solution itself or on the time interval of integration. Whenever we speak of the
charge–free sector
, we mean the sector obtained after subtracting the stationary electric–magnetic Kerr–Newman field carrying the asymptotic electric and magnetic charges of the solution under consideration.

Spin-1 source conventions

For the Maxwell system the source must satisfy
\nabla^{\nu}\mathcal{J}_{\nu}=0
because applying
\nabla^{\nu}
to
\nabla^{\mu}F_{\mu\nu}=\mathcal{J}_{\nu}
and using antisymmetry of
F
yields the continuity equation.

3
Weak Kerr–Newman regimes

This section proves the weak fixed–background theorem package stated in Section 1. The first three subsections establish the structural reduction of Theorem
1.1
: stationary subtraction, gauge–invariant radiative variables, and the short–range master system with same–order reconstruction. The final subsection records the currently rigorous weak Kerr–Newman radiative inputs and then deduces Theorems
1.2
,
1.3
, and
1.4
, together with the axisymmetric and non–axisymmetric comparison results, by combining those inputs with the full conditional reconstruction/scattering framework proved later in Section
4
.

3.1
Stationary electric–magnetic sector and radiative splitting

For a finite–energy Maxwell field
F
we define the asymptotic electric and magnetic charges by

q_{E}[F]:=\frac{1}{4\pi}\lim_{R\to\infty}\int_{S_{R}}{}^{\star}F,\qquad q_{B}[F]:=\frac{1}{4\pi}\lim_{R\to\infty}\int_{S_{R}}F,

(3.1)

whenever the limits exist. Here
⋆
is the Hodge dual of
(\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{KN}},g_{\mathrm{KN}})
and
S_{R}
is a large coordinate sphere in a fixed asymptotically flat end. In the sourceless case these quantities are conserved.

A convenient representative of the stationary purely electric Kerr–Newman family is

A_{\mathrm{Coulomb}}(q):=-\frac{qr}{\Sigma}\big(dt-a\sin^{2}\theta\,d\varphi\big),

(3.2)

and we define

\displaystyle F_{E}^{\mathrm{KN}}(q)

\displaystyle:=dA_{\mathrm{Coulomb}}(q),

\displaystyle F_{B}^{\mathrm{KN}}(q)

\displaystyle:={}^{\star}F_{E}^{\mathrm{KN}}(q),

\displaystyle F_{\mathrm{stat}}^{\mathrm{KN}}(q_{E},q_{B})

\displaystyle:=F_{E}^{\mathrm{KN}}(q_{E})+F_{B}^{\mathrm{KN}}(q_{B}).

(3.3)

Proposition 3.1

(Two–charge stationary subtraction)
.

For each
(q_{E},q_{B})\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
, the two–form
F_{\mathrm{stat}}^{\mathrm{KN}}(q_{E},q_{B})
is a stationary sourceless Maxwell field on
(\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{KN}},g_{\mathrm{KN}})
and satisfies

q_{E}\big[F_{\mathrm{stat}}^{\mathrm{KN}}(q_{E},q_{B})\big]=q_{E},\qquad q_{B}\big[F_{\mathrm{stat}}^{\mathrm{KN}}(q_{E},q_{B})\big]=q_{B}.

Consequently, if
F
is any finite–energy sourceless Maxwell field, then the radiative field

F_{\mathrm{rad}}:=F-F_{\mathrm{stat}}^{\mathrm{KN}}\big(q_{E}[F],q_{B}[F]\big)

(3.4)

has vanishing electric and magnetic charges.

Proof.

Differentiating (
3.2
) gives the explicit stationary electric field

\displaystyle F_{E}^{\mathrm{KN}}(q)=q\frac{r^{2}-a^{2}\cos^{2}\theta}{\Sigma^{2}}\,dr\wedge\big(dt-a\sin^{2}\theta\,d\varphi\big)

(3.5)

\displaystyle\qquad+2qar\frac{\sin\theta\cos\theta}{\Sigma^{2}}\,d\theta\wedge\big((r^{2}+a^{2})d\varphi-a\,dt\big).

(3.6)

Since
F_{E}^{\mathrm{KN}}(q)=dA_{\mathrm{Coulomb}}(q)
, it is closed. The coefficients are independent of
t
, so the field is stationary. The identity
d{}^{\star}F_{E}^{\mathrm{KN}}(q)=0
is the standard sourceless Maxwell equation satisfied by the Kerr–Newman Coulomb field on the fixed Kerr–Newman background; one may verify it directly from (
3.5
) in Boyer–Lindquist coordinates, or observe that for
q=Q
this is exactly the background Einstein–Maxwell field and then use linearity in
q
.

To compute its charges, expand for large
r
:

A_{\mathrm{Coulomb}}(q)=-\frac{q}{r}\,dt+\frac{qa\sin^{2}\theta}{r}\,d\varphi+O(r^{-3}),

and therefore

F_{E}^{\mathrm{KN}}(q)=\frac{q}{r^{2}}\,dr\wedge dt+O(r^{-2})\,dt\wedge d\theta+O(r^{-2})\,dr\wedge d\varphi+O(r^{-1})\,d\theta\wedge d\varphi.

Since the Kerr–Newman metric is asymptotically flat, the pullback of
{}^{\star}F_{E}^{\mathrm{KN}}(q)
to a large coordinate sphere
S_{R}
is

\iota_{R}^{*}({}^{\star}F_{E}^{\mathrm{KN}}(q))=q\sin\theta\,d\theta\wedge d\varphi+O(R^{-1}),

so

q_{E}\big[F_{E}^{\mathrm{KN}}(q)\big]=\frac{1}{4\pi}\lim_{R\to\infty}\int_{S_{R}}{}^{\star}F_{E}^{\mathrm{KN}}(q)=q.

On the other hand, the pullback of
F_{E}^{\mathrm{KN}}(q)
to
S_{R}
is
O(R^{-1})\,d\theta\wedge d\varphi
, hence

q_{B}\big[F_{E}^{\mathrm{KN}}(q)\big]=\frac{1}{4\pi}\lim_{R\to\infty}\int_{S_{R}}F_{E}^{\mathrm{KN}}(q)=0.

By definition
F_{B}^{\mathrm{KN}}(q)={}^{\star}F_{E}^{\mathrm{KN}}(q)
, so Maxwell duality gives a second stationary sourceless solution with

q_{E}\big[F_{B}^{\mathrm{KN}}(q)\big]=0,\qquad q_{B}\big[F_{B}^{\mathrm{KN}}(q)\big]=q.

Linearity then yields

q_{E}\big[F_{\mathrm{stat}}^{\mathrm{KN}}(q_{E},q_{B})\big]=q_{E},\qquad q_{B}\big[F_{\mathrm{stat}}^{\mathrm{KN}}(q_{E},q_{B})\big]=q_{B}.

Finally, if
F
is sourceless and
F_{\mathrm{rad}}
is defined by (
3.4
), then the linearity of the charge functionals implies

q_{E}[F_{\mathrm{rad}}]=q_{E}[F]-q_{E}[F]=0,\qquad q_{B}[F_{\mathrm{rad}}]=q_{B}[F]-q_{B}[F]=0.

This is exactly the two–charge stationary subtraction required for the radiative analysis.
∎

Definition 3.2

(Charge–free Maxwell field)
.

A Maxwell field is called
charge–free
if both charges in (
3.1
) vanish.

Proposition 3.3

(Step 1 for Theorem
1.8
: stationary subtraction and projection to the charge–free class)
.

Let
F
be a smooth sourceless Maxwell field on a fixed sub–extremal Kerr–Newman exterior, and let
S\subset\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{KN}}
be any smooth embedded spacelike two–sphere homologous to the sphere at infinity. Define the electric and magnetic fluxes through
S
by

q_{E}[F;S]:=\frac{1}{4\pi}\int_{S}{}^{\star}F,\qquad q_{B}[F;S]:=\frac{1}{4\pi}\int_{S}F.

Then the following hold.

1.

The numbers
q_{E}[F;S]
and
q_{B}[F;S]
are independent of the choice of homologous sphere
S
.

2.

If

F_{\mathrm{rad}}:=F-F_{\mathrm{stat}}^{\mathrm{KN}}\big(q_{E}[F],q_{B}[F]\big),

then
F_{\mathrm{rad}}
is again a smooth sourceless Maxwell field and

q_{E}[F_{\mathrm{rad}};S]=0,\qquad q_{B}[F_{\mathrm{rad}};S]=0

for every such sphere
S
.

3.

Let
S_{\tau,r}
be one of the topological two–spheres induced by the fixed foliation, let
\iota_{\tau,r}:S_{\tau,r}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{KN}}
denote the inclusion map, let
\varepsilon_{\tau,r}
be the induced area form, and let
\Delta_{S_{\tau,r}}
be the Laplace–Beltrami operator on
(S_{\tau,r},\gamma_{\tau,r})
. Then there exist unique mean–zero scalars
\phi_{E}(\tau,r,\cdot)
and
\phi_{B}(\tau,r,\cdot)
on
S_{\tau,r}
such that

\displaystyle\iota_{\tau,r}^{*}({}^{\star}F_{\mathrm{rad}})=(\Delta_{S_{\tau,r}}\phi_{E})\,\varepsilon_{\tau,r},

(3.7)

\displaystyle\iota_{\tau,r}^{*}F_{\mathrm{rad}}=(\Delta_{S_{\tau,r}}\phi_{B})\,\varepsilon_{\tau,r}.

(3.8)

Equivalently, the electric and magnetic harmonic mean modes vanish on every
S_{\tau,r}
. In the exactly round case this is the familiar disappearance of the
\ell=0
sector.

Proof.

We divide the argument into three steps.

Step 1: conservation of the fluxes on homologous spheres.

Let
S_{1}
and
S_{2}
be smooth spacelike two–spheres homologous to infinity, and let
\Omega
be a smooth compact three–chain with oriented boundary

\partial\Omega=S_{2}\sqcup(-S_{1}).

Because
F
is sourceless, it satisfies

dF=0,\qquad\nabla^{\mu}F_{\mu\nu}=0.

In differential–form notation the second identity is
\delta F=0
. For a two–form in four dimensions this is equivalent to
d{}^{\star}F=0
. Stokes’ theorem therefore gives

\int_{S_{2}}F-\int_{S_{1}}F=\int_{\partial\Omega}F=\int_{\Omega}dF=0,

and similarly

\int_{S_{2}}{}^{\star}F-\int_{S_{1}}{}^{\star}F=\int_{\partial\Omega}{}^{\star}F=\int_{\Omega}d{}^{\star}F=0.

Thus
q_{B}[F;S]
and
q_{E}[F;S]
are independent of
S
. In particular they agree with the asymptotic charges defined in (
3.1
).

Step 2: exact removal of the stationary two–charge sector.

By Proposition
3.1
, the field

F_{\mathrm{stat}}^{\mathrm{KN}}\big(q_{E}[F],q_{B}[F]\big)

is a stationary sourceless Maxwell field whose electric and magnetic charges equal those of
F
. Hence the difference

F_{\mathrm{rad}}=F-F_{\mathrm{stat}}^{\mathrm{KN}}\big(q_{E}[F],q_{B}[F]\big)

is again sourceless. Using Step 1 for both
F
and
F_{\mathrm{stat}}^{\mathrm{KN}}
, we obtain for every homologous sphere
S

q_{E}[F_{\mathrm{rad}};S]=q_{E}[F;S]-q_{E}\big[F_{\mathrm{stat}}^{\mathrm{KN}}\big(q_{E}[F],q_{B}[F]\big);S\big]=q_{E}[F]-q_{E}[F]=0,

and likewise

q_{B}[F_{\mathrm{rad}};S]=q_{B}[F;S]-q_{B}\big[F_{\mathrm{stat}}^{\mathrm{KN}}\big(q_{E}[F],q_{B}[F]\big);S\big]=q_{B}[F]-q_{B}[F]=0.

This is the precise sense in which the stationary obstructions are removed.

Step 3: vanishing of the harmonic mean sector on each sphere.

Fix a sphere
S_{\tau,r}
. Since
S_{\tau,r}
is two–dimensional, every smooth two–form on it is a scalar multiple of the induced area form
\varepsilon_{\tau,r}
. Thus there exist unique smooth scalars
\rho_{\tau,r}
and
\sigma_{\tau,r}
such that

\displaystyle\iota_{\tau,r}^{*}({}^{\star}F_{\mathrm{rad}})=\rho_{\tau,r}\,\varepsilon_{\tau,r},

\displaystyle\iota_{\tau,r}^{*}F_{\mathrm{rad}}=\sigma_{\tau,r}\,\varepsilon_{\tau,r}.

Integrating and using Step 2 gives

\int_{S_{\tau,r}}\rho_{\tau,r}\,d\mu_{S_{\tau,r}}=\int_{S_{\tau,r}}{}^{\star}F_{\mathrm{rad}}=4\pi q_{E}[F_{\mathrm{rad}};S_{\tau,r}]=0,

and

\int_{S_{\tau,r}}\sigma_{\tau,r}\,d\mu_{S_{\tau,r}}=\int_{S_{\tau,r}}F_{\mathrm{rad}}=4\pi q_{B}[F_{\mathrm{rad}};S_{\tau,r}]=0.

Therefore both coefficients have zero average. On a smooth compact connected two–sphere, the Laplace–Beltrami operator

\displaystyle\Delta_{S_{\tau,r}}:\{f\in C^{\infty}(S_{\tau,r}):\textstyle\int_{S_{\tau,r}}f\,d\mu_{S_{\tau,r}}=0\}\longrightarrow\{g\in C^{\infty}(S_{\tau,r}):\textstyle\int_{S_{\tau,r}}g\,d\mu_{S_{\tau,r}}=0\}

is an isomorphism. Hence there exist unique mean–zero functions
\phi_{E}
and
\phi_{B}
with

\Delta_{S_{\tau,r}}\phi_{E}=\rho_{\tau,r},\qquad\Delta_{S_{\tau,r}}\phi_{B}=\sigma_{\tau,r},

which is equivalent to (
3.7
). This proves that after stationary subtraction the spherewise harmonic mean modes vanish identically. Those are exactly the topological obstructions that would otherwise prevent coercive angular elliptic estimates and Morawetz control.
∎

Use of Proposition
3.3
in Theorems 1.1–1.4.
Proposition
3.3
is used directly in the proof of Theorem
1.1
(Theorem 1.1): it provides the charge–free stationary subtraction and the vanishing of the spherewise harmonic mean modes, which are the inputs needed for item (1) and for the elliptic uniqueness part of item (2) of Theorem
1.1
. It is not invoked again as a separate new proposition in the proofs of Theorems
1.2
–
1.4
(Theorems 1.2–1.4); there it enters only through the already-proved structural theorem, Theorem
1.1
.

Remark 3.4
.

Proposition
3.3
is the rigorous content of Step 1 in Remark
4.5
. It is the only stage of the argument where topology enters. Once the two charge modes are removed, every remaining sphere component lies in the mean–zero sector, so the angular elliptic estimates used later have no zero–mode loss.

3.2
Exact gauge–invariant radiative variables and the closed GHP system

To execute Step 2 of Remark
4.5
we now fix an exact radiative system. Work in advanced Kerr–Newman coordinates
(u,r,\theta,\varphi_{*})
and set

\displaystyle\Gamma:=r+ia\cos\theta,

\displaystyle\Sigma:=|\Gamma|^{2}=r^{2}+a^{2}\cos^{2}\theta,

\displaystyle\Delta:=r^{2}-2Mr+a^{2}+Q^{2}.

Fix the principal Kinnersley tetrad

\displaystyle l=\partial_{r},

(3.9)

\displaystyle n=\frac{r^{2}+a^{2}}{\Sigma}\,\partial_{u}-\frac{\Delta}{2\Sigma}\,\partial_{r}+\frac{a}{\Sigma}\,\partial_{\varphi_{*}},

(3.10)

\displaystyle m=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}\,\Gamma}\Big(ia\sin\theta\,\partial_{u}+\partial_{\theta}+i\csc\theta\,\partial_{\varphi_{*}}\Big),

(3.11)

with
\bar{m}
the complex conjugate of
m
. Let
\text{\th},\text{\th}_{0},\eth,\eth_{0}
denote the associated Geroch–Held–Penrose (GHP) operators and let

\displaystyle\rho=-\frac{1}{\bar{\Gamma}},

(3.12)

\displaystyle\rho_{0}=\frac{\Delta}{2\Sigma\bar{\Gamma}},

(3.13)

\displaystyle\tau=-\frac{ia\sin\theta}{\sqrt{2}\,\Sigma},

(3.14)

\displaystyle\tau_{0}=-\frac{ia\sin\theta}{\sqrt{2}\,\bar{\Gamma}^{2}}

(3.15)

be the nonvanishing optical coefficients of this tetrad. For the charge–subtracted field
F_{\mathrm{rad}}
define the exact Newman–Penrose/GHP Maxwell scalars by

\displaystyle\phi_{0}:=F_{\mathrm{rad}}(l,m),

(3.16)

\displaystyle 2\phi_{1}:=F_{\mathrm{rad}}(l,n)+F_{\mathrm{rad}}(\bar{m},m),

(3.17)

\displaystyle\phi_{2}:=F_{\mathrm{rad}}(\bar{m},n),

(3.18)

and decompose the source current into tetrad components

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}_{l}:=\mathcal{J}_{a}l^{a},

(3.19)

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}_{n}:=\mathcal{J}_{a}n^{a},

(3.20)

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}_{m}:=\mathcal{J}_{a}m^{a},

(3.21)

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}_{\bar{m}}:=\mathcal{J}_{a}\bar{m}^{a}.

(3.22)

Because
F_{\mathrm{rad}}
is a two–form, the quantities in (
3.16
) are invariant under electromagnetic gauge transformations
A\mapsto A+d\chi
whenever a local vector potential is introduced.

Proposition 3.5

(Step 2 for Theorem
1.8
: exact radiative variables and their closed system)
.

Let
F
solve the sourced Maxwell system with conserved source
\mathcal{J}
, let
(q_{E},q_{B})\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
be fixed, and set

F_{\mathrm{rad}}:=F-F_{\mathrm{stat}}^{\mathrm{KN}}(q_{E},q_{B}).

Then the following hold.

1.

The stationary two–charge family is pure middle component in the principal tetrad. More precisely, after passing from Boyer–Lindquist to advanced Kerr–Newman coordinates and dropping the exact
dr
–term, one may represent the electric Coulomb field by the gauge potential

\displaystyle\widetilde{A}_{\mathrm{Coulomb}}(q):=-\frac{qr}{\Sigma}\big(du-a\sin^{2}\theta\,d\varphi_{*}\big),

(3.23)

\displaystyle d\widetilde{A}_{\mathrm{Coulomb}}(q)=F_{E}^{\mathrm{KN}}(q).

(3.24)

In the tetrad (
3.9
) one has

\displaystyle\widetilde{A}_{l}=0,

(3.25)

\displaystyle\widetilde{A}_{m}=0,

(3.26)

\displaystyle\widetilde{A}_{\bar{m}}=0,

(3.27)

\displaystyle\widetilde{A}_{n}=-\frac{qr}{\Sigma}.

(3.28)

Consequently,

\displaystyle\phi_{0}\big[F_{E}^{\mathrm{KN}}(q)\big]=0,

(3.29)

\displaystyle 2\phi_{1}\big[F_{E}^{\mathrm{KN}}(q)\big]=\frac{q}{\bar{\Gamma}^{2}},

(3.30)

\displaystyle\phi_{2}\big[F_{E}^{\mathrm{KN}}(q)\big]=0.

(3.31)

By Maxwell duality,

\displaystyle\phi_{0}\big[F_{B}^{\mathrm{KN}}(q)\big]=0,

(3.32)

\displaystyle 2\phi_{1}\big[F_{B}^{\mathrm{KN}}(q)\big]=i\frac{q}{\bar{\Gamma}^{2}},

(3.33)

\displaystyle\phi_{2}\big[F_{B}^{\mathrm{KN}}(q)\big]=0.

(3.34)

Hence for the full stationary family,

\displaystyle\phi_{0}\big[F_{\mathrm{stat}}^{\mathrm{KN}}(q_{E},q_{B})\big]=0,

(3.35)

\displaystyle 2\phi_{1}\big[F_{\mathrm{stat}}^{\mathrm{KN}}(q_{E},q_{B})\big]=\frac{q_{E}+iq_{B}}{\bar{\Gamma}^{2}},

(3.36)

\displaystyle\phi_{2}\big[F_{\mathrm{stat}}^{\mathrm{KN}}(q_{E},q_{B})\big]=0.

(3.37)

In particular,

\displaystyle\phi_{0}[F_{\mathrm{rad}}]=\phi_{0}[F],

(3.38)

\displaystyle\phi_{2}[F_{\mathrm{rad}}]=\phi_{2}[F],

(3.39)

\displaystyle 2\phi_{1}[F_{\mathrm{rad}}]=2\phi_{1}[F]-\frac{q_{E}+iq_{B}}{\bar{\Gamma}^{2}}.

(3.40)

2.

Since
F_{\mathrm{stat}}^{\mathrm{KN}}(q_{E},q_{B})
is sourceless, the radiative field satisfies the same sourced Maxwell system as
F
:

dF_{\mathrm{rad}}=0,\qquad\nabla^{\mu}(F_{\mathrm{rad}})_{\mu\nu}=\mathcal{J}_{\nu}.

(3.41)

In the GHP formalism associated with (
3.9
), the exact scalars (
3.16
) satisfy the closed first–order system

\displaystyle(\eth_{0}-\tau_{0})\phi_{0}-(\text{\th}-2\rho)\phi_{1}

\displaystyle=2\pi\mathcal{J}_{l},

(3.42)

\displaystyle(\text{\th}_{0}-2\rho_{0})\phi_{1}-(\eth-\tau)\phi_{2}

\displaystyle=2\pi\mathcal{J}_{n},

(3.43)

\displaystyle(\text{\th}_{0}-\rho_{0})\phi_{0}-(\eth-2\tau)\phi_{1}

\displaystyle=2\pi\mathcal{J}_{m},

(3.44)

\displaystyle(\eth_{0}-2\tau_{0})\phi_{1}-(\text{\th}-\rho)\phi_{2}

\displaystyle=2\pi\mathcal{J}_{\bar{m}}.

(3.45)

Thus the exact radiative unknown is the triple
(\phi_{0},\phi_{1},\phi_{2})
, and the reduced Maxwell system closes on this triple without introducing any gauge potential.

Proof.

For the first part, write the Coulomb potential (
3.2
) in advanced Kerr–Newman coordinates. Using

dt-a\sin^{2}\theta\,d\varphi=du-a\sin^{2}\theta\,d\varphi_{*}-\frac{\Sigma}{\Delta}\,dr,

we see that
A_{\mathrm{Coulomb}}(q)
differs from (
3.23
) by the exact one–form
q(r/\Delta)\,dr
, so both yield the same field strength. Contracting (
3.23
) with the tetrad (
3.9
) gives (
3.25
); indeed,

\widetilde{A}_{l}=\widetilde{A}(\partial_{r})=0,

while

\widetilde{A}_{m}=-\frac{qr}{\Sigma}\,\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}\,\Gamma}\Big(ia\sin\theta-a\sin^{2}\theta\,i\csc\theta\Big)=0,

and similarly
\widetilde{A}_{\bar{m}}=0
. Finally,

\widetilde{A}_{n}=-\frac{qr}{\Sigma}\left(\frac{r^{2}+a^{2}}{\Sigma}-a\sin^{2}\theta\,\frac{a}{\Sigma}\right)=-\frac{qr}{\Sigma}.

Now apply the exact GHP definitions of the Maxwell scalars (equations (3a)–(3c) in
[
5
]
) to the potential components in (
3.25
). Since
\widetilde{A}_{l}=\widetilde{A}_{m}=\widetilde{A}_{\bar{m}}=0
, we obtain immediately

\phi_{0}\big[F_{E}^{\mathrm{KN}}(q)\big]=(\text{\th}-\bar{\rho})\widetilde{A}_{m}-(\eth-\bar{\tau}_{0})\widetilde{A}_{l}=0.

Similarly,

\displaystyle 2\phi_{1}\big[F_{E}^{\mathrm{KN}}(q)\big]=(\text{\th}+\rho-\bar{\rho})\widetilde{A}_{n}=\left(\partial_{r}+\rho-\bar{\rho}\right)\!\left(-\frac{qr}{\Sigma}\right)=\frac{q}{\bar{\Gamma}^{2}},

and, using the weight
\{-1,-1\}
of
\widetilde{A}_{n}
together with (
3.12
),

\displaystyle\phi_{2}\big[F_{E}^{\mathrm{KN}}(q)\big]=(\eth_{0}-\bar{\tau})\widetilde{A}_{n}=\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}\,\bar{\Gamma}}\,\partial_{\theta}-\tau_{0}-\bar{\tau}\right)\!\left(-\frac{qr}{\Sigma}\right)=0.

This proves (
3.29
). Equation (
3.32
) follows from
F_{B}^{\mathrm{KN}}(q)={}^{\star}F_{E}^{\mathrm{KN}}(q)
and the standard action of Hodge duality on the complex Newman–Penrose scalars. Linearity then gives (
3.35
), and subtracting the stationary family yields (
3.38
).

For the second part, (
3.41
) is immediate from linearity and the sourcelessness of
F_{\mathrm{stat}}^{\mathrm{KN}}(q_{E},q_{B})
. The four equations (
3.42
)–(
3.45
) are the exact GHP form of Maxwell’s equations for the principal type–D tetrad; they are precisely equations (2a)–(2d) in
[
5
]
, written here for the radiative field
F_{\mathrm{rad}}
and the source
\mathcal{J}
. Since each equation involves only
\phi_{0}
,
\phi_{1}
,
\phi_{2}
, and the tetrad components of
\mathcal{J}
, the system is closed. No gauge choice for a vector potential is needed after the initial computation of the stationary middle component above, because the unknowns are defined directly from the gauge–invariant two–form
F_{\mathrm{rad}}
.
∎

Use of Proposition
3.5
in Theorems 1.1–1.4.
Proposition
3.5
is also a direct input to Theorem
1.1
(Theorem 1.1). It shows that the stationary electric–magnetic family is pure middle component, identifies the exact gauge–invariant radiative triple, and furnishes the closed first–order GHP system. These facts are used in the proof of item (1) and in the first structural step leading to item (3) of Theorem
1.1
. As with Proposition
3.3
, it is not used later as an additional independent hypothesis in the proofs of Theorems
1.2
–
1.4
; its contribution is already packaged in Theorem
1.1
.

Corollary 3.6

(Exact sourced Teukolsky equations for the extreme radiative variables)
.

Define the spin
+1
Teukolsky operator and source map by

\mathcal{O}_{+1}\Phi:=\big[(\text{\th}-\bar{\rho}-2\rho)(\text{\th}_{0}-\rho_{0})-(\eth-\bar{\tau}_{0}-2\tau)(\eth_{0}-\tau_{0})\big]\Phi,

(3.46)

\mathcal{T}_{+1}(\mathcal{J}):=\frac{1}{2}\big[(\eth-\bar{\tau}_{0}-2\tau)\mathcal{J}_{l}-(\text{\th}-\bar{\rho}-2\rho)\mathcal{J}_{m}\big].

(3.47)

Then

\mathcal{O}_{+1}\phi_{0}=-4\pi\mathcal{T}_{+1}(\mathcal{J}).

(3.48)

Applying the GHP prime involution
l\leftrightarrow n
,
m\leftrightarrow\bar{m}
,
\rho\leftrightarrow\rho_{0}
,
\tau\leftrightarrow\tau_{0}
gives the spin
-1
Teukolsky equation

\mathcal{O}_{-1}\phi_{2}=-4\pi\mathcal{T}_{-1}(\mathcal{J}),

(3.49)

where

\mathcal{O}_{-1}\Phi:=\big[(\text{\th}_{0}-\bar{\rho}_{0}-2\rho_{0})(\text{\th}-\rho)-(\eth_{0}-\bar{\tau}-2\tau_{0})(\eth-\tau)\big]\Phi,

(3.50)

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}_{-1}(\mathcal{J}):=\frac{1}{2}\big[(\eth_{0}-\bar{\tau}-2\tau_{0})\mathcal{J}_{n}-(\text{\th}_{0}-\bar{\rho}_{0}-2\rho_{0})\mathcal{J}_{\bar{m}}\big].

(3.51)

Thus the exact first–order radiative system of Proposition
3.5
compresses, at the level of the extreme components, to a closed pair of second–order spin
\pm 1
equations.

Proof.

The spin
+1
equation is exactly the Teukolsky operator identity
SE=OT
from equations (4a)–(4c) and (5) of
[
5
]
, applied to the radiative field
F_{\mathrm{rad}}
. Indeed, for a local potential
A
with
dA=F_{\mathrm{rad}}
, one has
TA=-\phi_{0}
and hence

\mathcal{O}_{+1}\phi_{0}=-O(TA)=-S(EA)=-4\pi\mathcal{T}_{+1}(\mathcal{J}).

Since the resulting equation is gauge invariant, it globalizes from local potentials. The spin
-1
equation follows by applying the prime involution to the same operator identity, or equivalently by repeating the derivation for the primed Maxwell scalar
\phi_{2}
and the primed source component
\mathcal{J}_{\bar{m}}
.
∎

Remark 3.7
.

Proposition
3.5
is the rigorous content of Step 2 in Remark
4.5
. The important structural fact is that the stationary electric–magnetic obstruction is pure middle component in the principal tetrad. Thus the genuinely radiative content is already visible in the extreme variables
\phi_{0}
and
\phi_{2}
, while
\phi_{1}
is the charge–free middle component coupled to them by the closed first–order system (
3.42
)–(
3.45
).

3.3
Horizon–regular exact Teukolsky equations and the short–range master class

The exact second–order equations in Corollary
3.6
are already regular at the future event horizon in the advanced coordinates
(u,r,\theta,\varphi_{*})
, but in their raw Teukolsky normalization they are not yet in the short–range form most natural for the vector–field method. Step 3 consists of isolating that distinction precisely and then fixing a master normalization in which the remaining lower–order terms are short range.

Proposition 3.8

(Step 3 for Theorem
1.8
: the exact Teukolsky equations are horizon–regular)
.

Let

\psi_{+}:=\Gamma^{2}\phi_{0},\qquad\psi_{-}:=\bar{\Gamma}^{2}\phi_{2}.

Then the sourced Teukolsky equations (
3.48
)–(
3.49
) can be rewritten on the closure of the exterior region

\overline{\mathcal{D}}:=\{r\geq r_{+}\}

in the form

(\Box_{g_{\mathrm{KN}}}+B_{+}^{a}\nabla_{a}+V_{+})\psi_{+}=\mathfrak{S}_{+}(\mathcal{J}),

(3.52)

(\Box_{g_{\mathrm{KN}}}+B_{-}^{a}\nabla_{a}+V_{-})\psi_{-}=\mathfrak{S}_{-}(\mathcal{J}),

(3.53)

where
B_{\pm}^{a}
are smooth complex vector fields on
\overline{\mathcal{D}}
,
V_{\pm}
are smooth complex scalars on
\overline{\mathcal{D}}
, and
\mathfrak{S}_{\pm}(\mathcal{J})
are first–order linear differential operators in the source current with smooth coefficients on
\overline{\mathcal{D}}
.

Moreover, in the advanced coordinates one has the asymptotic bounds

\displaystyle B_{\pm}^{u}

\displaystyle=O(r^{-1}),

(3.54)

\displaystyle B_{\pm}^{r}

\displaystyle=O(r^{-1}),

\displaystyle B_{\pm}^{\theta}

\displaystyle=O(r^{-2}),

\displaystyle B_{\pm}^{\varphi_{*}}

\displaystyle=O(r^{-2}),

\displaystyle V_{\pm}

\displaystyle=O(r^{-2}),

(3.55)

and the source operators can be written schematically as

\displaystyle\mathfrak{S}_{\pm}(\mathcal{J})=\sum_{\alpha\in\{u,r,\theta,\varphi_{*}\}}C_{\pm}^{\alpha}\,

(3.56)

\displaystyle\qquad\partial_{\alpha}\mathcal{J}+D_{\pm}\,\mathcal{J},

(3.57)

where

\displaystyle C_{\pm}^{u},C_{\pm}^{r}=O(r^{-1}),

\displaystyle C_{\pm}^{\theta},C_{\pm}^{\varphi_{*}}=O(r^{-2}),

\displaystyle D_{\pm}=O(r^{-2}).

In particular, the exact Teukolsky equations are horizon–regular but still
long range
at infinity because of the
O(r^{-1})
first–order coefficients.

Proof.

It is enough to analyze the operators
\mathcal{O}_{\pm 1}
from Corollary
3.6
, because conjugation by the smooth nowhere–vanishing weights
\Gamma^{2}
and
\bar{\Gamma}^{2}
only changes the lower–order coefficients. Write again the principal tetrad and its nonzero optical coefficients:

\displaystyle l=\partial_{r},

\displaystyle n=\frac{r^{2}+a^{2}}{\Sigma}\,\partial_{u}-\frac{\Delta}{2\Sigma}\,\partial_{r}+\frac{a}{\Sigma}\,\partial_{\varphi_{*}},

\displaystyle m=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}\,\Gamma}\Big(ia\sin\theta\,\partial_{u}+\partial_{\theta}+i\csc\theta\,\partial_{\varphi_{*}}\Big),

\displaystyle\rho=-\frac{1}{\bar{\Gamma}},

\displaystyle\rho_{0}=\frac{\Delta}{2\Sigma\bar{\Gamma}},

\displaystyle\tau=-\frac{ia\sin\theta}{\sqrt{2}\,\Sigma},

\displaystyle\tau_{0}=-\frac{ia\sin\theta}{\sqrt{2}\,\bar{\Gamma}^{2}}.

All coefficients are smooth on
\overline{\mathcal{D}}
: indeed
\Sigma=r^{2}+a^{2}\cos^{2}\theta>0
on the whole exterior closure, while the factor
\Delta
appears only in numerators in the advanced-coordinate form of
n
and
\rho_{0}
.

Now expand (
3.46
):

\mathcal{O}_{+1}=(\text{\th}-\bar{\rho}-2\rho)(\text{\th}_{0}-\rho_{0})-(\eth-\bar{\tau}_{0}-2\tau)(\eth_{0}-\tau_{0}).

The weighted corrections by
\rho,\rho_{0},\tau,\tau_{0}
are zeroth order, so the only second–order pieces are
\text{\th}\text{\th}_{0}-\eth\eth_{0}
. The same is true for
\mathcal{O}_{-1}
. Since commutators are first order, the symmetric part of these second derivatives is

\frac{1}{2}(\text{\th}\text{\th}_{0}+\text{\th}_{0}\text{\th})-\frac{1}{2}(\eth\eth_{0}+\eth_{0}\eth),

which is exactly the scalar wave principal symbol in the null tetrad convention, up to the fixed overall factor
-1/2
. Therefore
-2\mathcal{O}_{\pm 1}
has the same principal part as
\Box_{g_{\mathrm{KN}}}
, and hence each equation (
3.48
)–(
3.49
) can be rewritten in the form (
3.52
)–(
3.53
) for suitable smooth lower–order coefficients
B_{\pm}^{a}
,
V_{\pm}
, and source operators
\mathfrak{S}_{\pm}
.

To identify the large–
r
behavior, use the explicit background coefficients:

\displaystyle\Gamma=r+ia\cos\theta,

\displaystyle\Sigma=r^{2}+a^{2}\cos^{2}\theta,

\displaystyle\Delta=r^{2}-2Mr+a^{2}+Q^{2}.

As
r\to\infty
,

\displaystyle\frac{r^{2}+a^{2}}{\Sigma}=1+O(r^{-2}),

\displaystyle\frac{\Delta}{2\Sigma}=\frac{1}{2}-\frac{M}{r}+O(r^{-2}),

\displaystyle\frac{a}{\Sigma}=O(r^{-2}),

and

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\Gamma}=r^{-1}+O(r^{-2}),

\displaystyle\rho=-r^{-1}+O(r^{-2}),

\displaystyle\rho_{0}=\frac{1}{2}r^{-1}+O(r^{-2}),

\displaystyle\tau,\tau_{0}=O(r^{-2}).

Thus

l=\partial_{r},\qquad n=\partial_{u}-\frac{1}{2}\partial_{r}+O(r^{-1})\partial_{r}+O(r^{-2})\partial_{\varphi_{*}},

while

m=O(r^{-1})\partial_{u}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}\,r}\partial_{\theta}+\frac{i}{\sqrt{2}\,r}\csc\theta\,\partial_{\varphi_{*}}+O(r^{-2}).

Substituting these expansions into the formulas for
\mathcal{O}_{\pm 1}
shows that all first–order coefficients in the
(u,r)
directions are at worst
O(r^{-1})
, all angular first–order coefficients are
O(r^{-2})
, and every zeroth–order coefficient is
O(r^{-2})
. This yields (
3.54
)–(
3.55
). The source formulas (
3.47
) and (
3.51
) have exactly the same differential structure, so the same asymptotics give (
3.56
). The last sentence is now immediate.
∎

Use of Proposition
3.8
in Theorems 1.1–1.4.
Proposition
3.8
is the final direct proposition-level input used in the proof of Theorem
1.1
(Theorem 1.1). It rewrites the exact Teukolsky equations in a horizon–regular wave form and isolates the residual long–range
O(r^{-1})
terms that the short–range master reduction must remove. This is the proposition-level bridge to item (3) of Theorem
1.1
. The proofs of Theorems
1.2
,
1.3
, and
1.4
do not call Proposition
3.8
separately; they use it only through the already-proved Theorem
1.1
.

We now close Step 3 by proving the short–range reduction theorem announced in Subsection
1.5
.

For later reference, the structural reduction theorem furnishes a finite collection

U_{M}=(U_{M}^{(1)},\dots,U_{M}^{(N)})

of gauge–invariant radiative variables satisfying

\big(\Box_{g_{\mathrm{KN}}}I_{N}+\mathbb{A}^{a}\nabla_{a}+\mathbb{V}\big)U_{M}=\mathbb{S}(\mathcal{J}),

(3.58)

where
I_{N}
is the
N\times N
identity,
\mathbb{A}^{a}
and
\mathbb{V}
are smooth matrix–valued coefficients on
\overline{\mathcal{D}}
, and for every multiindex
\alpha
in the advanced coordinates

\displaystyle\partial^{\alpha}\mathbb{A}^{a}=O(r^{-2-|\alpha|}),

(3.59)

\displaystyle\partial^{\alpha}\mathbb{V}=O(r^{-3-|\alpha|})

(3.60)

\displaystyle(r\to\infty).

(3.61)

The source operator
\mathbb{S}
is first order in
\mathcal{J}
, and its coefficients obey the same short–range bounds as
\mathbb{A}^{a}
. When
N=1
, we write
U_{M}=u_{M}
.

Proof of Theorem
1.1
.

Start from Proposition
3.1
, Proposition
3.3
, and Proposition
3.5
. Proposition
3.5
proves that the stationary electric–magnetic Kerr–Newman family is pure middle component in the principal tetrad, i.e. its exact extreme radiative components vanish identically, whereas Proposition
3.3
proves that after stationary subtraction the remaining field lies in the charge–free sector and has vanishing spherewise harmonic mean mode. Consequently every admissible derived Teukolsky or generalized Regge–Wheeler variable built from the exact charge–free radiative triple vanishes on
F_{\mathrm{stat}}^{\mathrm{KN}}(q_{E},q_{B})
, which is item (1).

Next, Corollary
3.6
supplies the exact second–order spin
\pm 1
Teukolsky equations for the extreme radiative variables, and Proposition
3.8
rewrites them in horizon–regular wave form. The only obstruction to the physical–space vector–field form needed later is the remaining long–range
O(r^{-1})
first–order part. On the Kerr edge this is removed by the classical Fackerell–Ipser transform and equivalent geometric Maxwell reductions
[
8
]
; on the Reissner–Nordström edge it is removed by the electric/magnetic Regge–Wheeler variables and their local–energy theory
[
1
]
; and on general Kerr–Newman the coupled Teukolsky/generalized Regge–Wheeler framework of
[
3
,
5
]
furnishes a finite collection
U_{M}
obtained from the exact radiative variables by first–order background differential operators with smooth stationary–axisymmetric coefficients. In each realization the transformed equations have principal part
\Box_{g_{\mathrm{KN}}}I_{N}
, smooth coefficients up to the future horizon, and only short–range lower–order terms, yielding (
3.58
)–(
3.59
) and therefore item (3). Because both the Teukolsky step and the short–range reduction are first order in the source, the source operator
\mathbb{S}(\mathcal{J})
is first order in
\mathcal{J}
with the same short–range bounds.

Finally, the same reduction comes with a triangular transport/elliptic reconstruction system recovering the exact radiative variables from
U_{M}
. On the Kerr and Reissner–Nordström edges this is the standard Fackerell–Ipser/Regge–Wheeler reconstruction, while on general Kerr–Newman it is exactly the finite–time reconstruction built into the coupled Teukolsky/generalized Regge–Wheeler system. Since Proposition
3.3
removes the spherewise harmonic mean mode, the angular elliptic inversions are unique on every sphere; hence the reconstruction is same–order and loses no top derivatives. The full charge–free Maxwell field is then recovered algebraically from the exact radiative variables, which proves item (2).
∎

Remark 3.9
.

Proposition
3.8
and Theorem
1.1
isolate the exact content of Step 3 in Remark
4.5
. The raw Teukolsky equations are already smooth at the future horizon, but they still carry
O(r^{-1})
first–order terms and are therefore not yet in the short–range form on which the redshift/Morawetz/
r^{p}
argument is most naturally run. Step 3 is precisely the passage from that horizon–regular long–range Teukolsky normalization to a short–range master system of the form (
3.58
). On Kerr one realizes this through the Fackerell–Ipser transform or an equivalent geometric reformulation of the Maxwell system
[
8
]
; on Reissner–Nordström through the electric/magnetic Regge–Wheeler variables and their physical–space analysis
[
1
]
; and on rotating charged Kerr–Newman the current physical–space program is naturally phrased by a finite coupled Teukolsky/generalized Regge–Wheeler collection rather than by a canonical single scalar
[
3
]
.

3.4
Proofs of Theorems 1.1–1.4 and the weak Kerr–Newman radiative input

For ease of checking, Theorem
1.1
was proved in the preceding subsection immediately after Proposition
3.8
; the proposition chain used there is Proposition
3.1
, Proposition
3.3
, Proposition
3.5
, Corollary
3.6
, and Proposition
3.8
. The present subsection now isolates the literature–based radiative inputs that are already unconditional in the Kerr–Newman interior regimes presently under rigorous control and then proves Theorems
1.2
,
1.3
, and
1.4
explicitly, in the same numerical order as in Section 1. Combined with the full sub–extremal reconstruction/scattering package proved later in Section
4
, this yields the weak non–axisymmetric theorem package stated in Section 1.
For the reader checking Proposition
3.3
(Proposition 3.3), Proposition
3.5
(Proposition 3.5), and Proposition
3.8
(Proposition 3.8) specifically: these three propositions are direct inputs only to Theorem
1.1
(Theorem 1.1). In the proofs of Theorems
1.2
,
1.3
, and
1.4
(Theorems 1.2–1.4) they are used only indirectly, through Theorem
1.1
, and not as separate additional propositions.

Theorem 3.10

(Radiative Maxwell package on weak Kerr–Newman exteriors and related interior regimes)
.

There exist dimensionless constants
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{KN}},c_{a},c_{Q}\in(0,1)
, depending only on the smallness thresholds appearing in
[
6
,
7
]
, with the following properties.

1.

Axisymmetric relative slow–rotation regime.
Assume

0<|a|\leq\varepsilon_{\mathrm{KN}}|Q|<M

and restrict to axially symmetric radiative variables. Then the axisymmetric analogue of Theorem
1.8
holds for the Kerr–Newman radiative package realized by the coupled Teukolsky/generalized Regge–Wheeler variables of
[
3
,
7
]
. In particular, for every integer
k\geq 2
there exist higher–order non–degenerate energies
\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{axi}}^{(k)}
, spacetime Morawetz bulks
\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{axi}}^{(k)}
, conformal/
r^{p}
weighted energies
\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{C},\mathrm{axi}}^{(k)}
, and an outgoing
r^{p}
hierarchy such that

\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{axi}}^{(k)}(\tau)+\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{axi}}^{(k)}([0,\tau])\leq C_{k}\,\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{axi}}^{(k)}(0)

for all
\tau\geq 0
.

2.

General data in the doubly small regime.
Assume

0<|a|\leq c_{a}M,\qquad 0<|Q|\leq c_{Q}M.

Then Theorem
1.8
holds without any symmetry restriction for the same Kerr–Newman radiative package, by the small–parameter theorem of
[
6
]
.

Therefore the radiative stage is already unconditional in two interior Kerr–Newman regimes: the full
axisymmetric
package on the whole relative regime
a\ll|Q|<M
, and the full non–axisymmetric package on the doubly small regime
|a|,|Q|\ll M
. Accordingly, this theorem should be read as a literature–based verification of the single remaining Step 6 input and hence of Theorem
1.8
in those regimes.

Proof.

As in the paragraph preceding Theorem
1.8
, we exploit the freedom to realize the radiative package not by a single Fackerell–Ipser scalar but by a finite collection of equivalent gauge–invariant variables. For rotating charged Kerr–Newman the available realization is the coupled Teukolsky/generalized Regge–Wheeler system derived in
[
3
]
. We take as radiative unknowns the collection

u_{\mathrm{KN}}:=(p,q,B,F,A,X),

where
(B,F,A,X)
are Teukolsky variables and
(p,q)
are their Chandrasekhar transforms. The point is to show that, in the currently rigorous interior Kerr–Newman regimes singled out in the theorem, the available theorems imply exactly the higher–order non–degenerate energy, Morawetz, and
r^{p}
statements demanded by Theorem
1.8
.

Let
c_{\mathrm{axi}}>0
denote a smallness constant for the slowly rotating axisymmetric theorem of
[
7
, Theorem 3.1]
, so that for

|a|\leq c_{\mathrm{axi}}M,\qquad 0<|Q|<M,

and axisymmetric data, the commuted Teukolsky/gRW energies, Morawetz bulks, and weighted far–field norms are uniformly controlled for all
\tau\geq 0
. Let
c_{a},c_{Q}>0
denote smallness constants for the fully non–axisymmetric theorem of
[
6
, Theorem 3.2]
, so that for

|a|\leq c_{a}M,\qquad|Q|\leq c_{Q}M,

one has the corresponding higher–order energy boundedness, Morawetz estimates, and outgoing
r^{p}
hierarchy for general data. Define

\varepsilon_{\mathrm{KN}}:=\min\{c_{\mathrm{axi}},\,1\}.

We verify the two assertions separately.

For part (1), assume

0<|a|\leq\varepsilon_{\mathrm{KN}}|Q|<M

and restrict to axisymmetric data. Since
|Q|<M
, this immediately gives

|a|\leq\varepsilon_{\mathrm{KN}}M\leq c_{\mathrm{axi}}M.

Hence the hypothesis of
[
7
, Theorem 3.1]
is satisfied. The proof in
[
7
]
is carried out entirely in physical space: the main Morawetz estimate for the generalized Regge–Wheeler system controls the trapping region in axial symmetry, and the Teukolsky variables are then bounded through the coupled system and the Chandrasekhar transform. More concretely, if one writes

E_{\mathrm{axi}}^{(k)}[u_{\mathrm{KN}}](\tau),\qquad B_{\mathrm{axi}}^{(k)}[u_{\mathrm{KN}}]([0,\tau]),

for the order–
k
commuted energies and bulks appearing there, then
[
7
, Theorem 3.1 and the discussion immediately following it]
yields, for every
k\geq 2
,

E_{\mathrm{axi}}^{(k)}[u_{\mathrm{KN}}](\tau)+B_{\mathrm{axi}}^{(k)}[u_{\mathrm{KN}}]([0,\tau])\leq C_{k}\,E_{\mathrm{axi}}^{(k)}[u_{\mathrm{KN}}](0),

together with the corresponding
r^{p}
weighted estimates. We therefore define

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{axi}}^{(k)}:=E_{\mathrm{axi}}^{(k)}[u_{\mathrm{KN}}],

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{axi}}^{(k)}:=B_{\mathrm{axi}}^{(k)}[u_{\mathrm{KN}}],

and choose
\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{C},\mathrm{axi}}^{(k)}
to be any one of the admissible
r^{p}
weighted flux functionals plus an equivalent compact–region non–degenerate energy. Since Theorem
1.8
only requires the polynomially growing conformal bound (
1.2
), the bounded weighted energies furnished by the
r^{p}
hierarchy imply that weaker bound a fortiori. This proves the axisymmetric analogue of Theorem
1.8
on the whole relative regime
|a|\leq\varepsilon_{\mathrm{KN}}|Q|<M
.

For part (2), assume directly that

0<|a|\leq c_{a}M,\qquad 0<|Q|\leq c_{Q}M.

Thus the smallness assumptions of
[
6
, Theorem 3.2]
are satisfied for general, not necessarily axisymmetric, data. The cited theorem yields higher–order boundedness, integrated local energy decay, and the outgoing
r^{p}
hierarchy for the same collection of Teukolsky/gRW variables
u_{\mathrm{KN}}
. As above, one defines
\mathcal{E}_{0}^{(k)}
,
\mathcal{M}_{0}^{(k)}
, and
\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{C},0}^{(k)}
to be the corresponding commuted non–degenerate energies, Morawetz bulks, and admissible
r^{p}
weighted energies furnished by
[
6
]
. These satisfy (
1.1
) and the weaker conformal bound (
1.2
), and the theorem explicitly provides the outgoing
r^{p}
hierarchy required in Theorem
1.8
. Since the present statement is homogeneous, the dual norm
\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{N}_{0}^{*}}
may again be chosen as any fixed dual to the bulk control norm. This proves Theorem
1.8
without symmetry on the full doubly small regime
|a|,|Q|\ll M
.

Combining the two cases proves the theorem.
∎

Proof of Theorem
1.13
.

Let
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{KN}}
be the constant furnished by Theorem
3.10
(1). Because
F
is axisymmetric and
F_{\mathrm{stat}}^{\mathrm{KN}}\big(q_{E}[F],q_{B}[F]\big)
is stationary and axisymmetric, the charge–free field

F_{\mathrm{rad}}=F-F_{\mathrm{stat}}^{\mathrm{KN}}\big(q_{E}[F],q_{B}[F]\big)

is again axisymmetric. The admissible radiative reductions used throughout Sections
3
and
4
are built from linear differential operators with stationary axisymmetric coefficients, so the associated radiative variables of
F_{\mathrm{rad}}
remain in the axisymmetric sector. Theorem
3.10
(1) therefore verifies the hypotheses of Theorem
1.8
on the entire regime

0<|a|\leq\varepsilon_{\mathrm{KN}}|Q|<M.

Applying Theorem
1.10
to
F_{\mathrm{rad}}
yields (
1.9
), the tensorial boundedness/Morawetz/
r^{p}
/pointwise conclusions, and the restriction of the Cauchy–to–radiation maps to the axisymmetric charge–free sector. The final assertion for forced fields is the same argument with Corollary
1.11
in place of Theorem
1.10
, noting that axisymmetry of the source and charge–compatible stationary subtraction preserve the axisymmetric charge–free class.
∎

Proof of Theorem
1.15
.

Let
c_{a},c_{Q}
be the constants furnished by Theorem
3.10
(2). Under the hypotheses

0<|a|\leq c_{a}M,\qquad 0<|Q|\leq c_{Q}M,

that proposition verifies the radiative package of Theorem
1.8
without any symmetry restriction. The conclusions of Theorem
1.10
and Corollary
1.11
therefore apply directly.
∎

Remark 3.11

(Proposition-level closure of Theorems
1.2
–
1.4
)
.

Theorem
1.2
is obtained by inserting the weak radiative verification of Theorem
3.10
(2) into the conditional radiative closure proved in Proposition
4.20
, Proposition
4.22
, Proposition
4.27
, Proposition
4.29
, and the packaged proof of Theorem
1.8
. Theorem
1.3
then uses Proposition
4.40
–
4.51
via Theorem
1.10
and Corollary
1.11
. Theorem
1.4
uses Proposition
4.53
–
4.57
via Theorem
1.10
. We record the three proofs immediately below, in the same numerical order as Theorems 1.2–1.4.
Accordingly, Proposition
3.3
(3.3), Proposition
3.5
(3.5), and Proposition
3.8
(3.8) are not separate additional inputs in the proofs of Theorems
1.2
–
1.4
; their role in Theorems 1.2–1.4 is already absorbed into Theorem
1.1
(Theorem 1.1).

Proof of Theorem
1.2
.

At the proposition level, Proposition
3.3
, Proposition
3.5
, and Proposition
3.8
enter this proof only through Theorem
1.1
; they are not reused here as separate new inputs.
Let
c_{a},c_{Q}
be the constants furnished by Theorem
3.10
(2), and fix a weak Kerr–Newman background satisfying
0<|a|\leq c_{a}M
and
0<|Q|\leq c_{Q}M
. By Theorem
1.1
, the stationary–subtracted Maxwell field admits an admissible short–range master system. Theorem
3.10
(2) verifies on this background the only external radiative input required in Section
4
. Once that seed is available, Proposition
4.20
yields the one–level redshift–Morawetz estimate, Proposition
4.22
yields the full commuted non–degenerate energy/Morawetz hierarchy, Proposition
4.27
yields the one–level far–field weighted estimate, and Proposition
4.29
yields the full commuted outgoing
r^{p}
hierarchy together with the conformal control. The proof of Theorem
1.8
in Section
4
packages exactly these propositions. Therefore all bounds claimed in Theorem
1.2
hold at every commuted order
k\geq 0
throughout the weak regime.
∎

Proof of Theorem
1.3
.

The same point applies here: Proposition
3.3
, Proposition
3.5
, and Proposition
3.8
contribute only through Theorem
1.1
; the present proof does not invoke them as separate additional propositions.
Let

F_{\mathrm{rad}}:=F-F_{\mathrm{stat}}^{\mathrm{KN}}\big(q_{E}[F],q_{B}[F]\big).

By Proposition
3.1
,
F_{\mathrm{rad}}
is charge free. On the weak regime, Theorem
3.10
(2) verifies the radiative hypothesis needed in Section
4
. Consequently Proposition
4.40
furnishes the canonical master norm, Proposition
4.45
gives the initial comparison estimate, Proposition
4.48
gives the no–loss spacetime reconstruction estimate, Proposition
4.49
identifies the coercive tensorial physical–space norm, and Proposition
4.51
yields polynomial pointwise decay. Inserted into the proof of Theorem
1.10
, these results give the weak fixed–background consequence recorded earlier as Theorem
1.15
. Hence, for sourceless data,

\|F_{\mathrm{rad}}\|_{\mathcal{X}_{\mathrm{Max},0}^{(k)}([0,\tau])}^{2}\leq C_{k}\,\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{Max},0}^{(k)}[F_{\mathrm{rad}}](0).

This is exactly the first part of Theorem
1.3
. The forced estimate is the same weak specialization of Corollary
1.11
. The polynomial pointwise decay clause is precisely Proposition
4.51
after the same weak radiative input has been inserted. This proves Theorem
1.3
.
∎

Proof of Theorem
1.4
.

Again, Proposition
3.3
, Proposition
3.5
, and Proposition
3.8
are used only through Theorem
1.1
; the present scattering proof starts after that structural theorem has already been established.
Let
c_{a},c_{Q}
be as in Theorem
3.10
(2), and assume the weak Kerr–Newman hypotheses. The same weak radiative verification also supplies the scattering input needed in Section
4
. More precisely, Proposition
4.53
constructs the master radiation fields, Proposition
4.54
solves the master final–state problem, Proposition
4.55
reconstructs Maxwell radiation data asymptotically, and Proposition
4.57
transfers scattering from the master system back to the charge–free Maxwell field. Equivalently, once the weak radiative input is inserted, Theorem
1.10
yields bounded isomorphisms

\mathscr{S}_{\mathrm{Max},0}^{\pm}:\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{Max},0}^{(k)}(\Sigma_{0})\xrightarrow{\ \sim\ }\mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{Max},\pm}^{(k)}.

The wave operators are their bounded inverses, and the scattering operator

\mathscr{A}_{\mathrm{Max},0}:=\mathscr{S}_{\mathrm{Max},0}^{+}\circ(\mathscr{S}_{\mathrm{Max},0}^{-})^{-1}
