is therefore a bounded isomorphism between the past and future radiation spaces. Since the stationary field
F_{\mathrm{stat}}^{\mathrm{KN}}\big(q_{E}[F],q_{B}[F]\big)
is time independent and carries exactly the non–radiative charge sector, this is precisely the two–sided scattering theory of the stationary–subtracted field
F_{\mathrm{rad}}
. This proves Theorem
1.4
.
∎

Remark 3.12

(What the regime
Q\neq 0
and
a\ll|Q|<M
still does
not
prove)
.

Theorem
3.10
, together with Theorem
1.9
, already yields the unconditional fixed–background consequences stated in Theorem
1.13
and Theorem
1.15
. It still falls short, however, of a full verification of Theorem
1.8
on the entire set

\{Q\neq 0,\ |a|\ll|Q|<M\}

for
general
data.

Indeed, the present Kerr–Newman theory splits into two regimes. Part II of the Teukolsky series proves boundedness and decay for
|a|\ll M
,
|Q|<M
only in
axial symmetry

[
7
]
; this is why Theorem
3.10
(1) is valid on the whole relative regime
|a|\ll|Q|<M
. Part I proves the corresponding result for general data only when both parameters are perturbatively small, namely
|a|,|Q|\ll M

[
6
]
; this is exactly the content of Theorem
3.10
(2).

The remaining unresolved region is therefore the
non-axisymmetric strongly charged
regime

\displaystyle 0<|a|\ll M,

\displaystyle 0<|Q|<M,

\displaystyle|Q|=O(M),

which includes many points satisfying
|a|\ll|Q|<M
. In that regime the missing ingredient is not (
4.89
) or (
4.112
); those estimates are already proved in Propositions
4.20
and
4.27
. What remains open is the Step 6 local–energy input for the corresponding coupled short–range master system. Remark
4.11
explains that the displayed form (
4.46
) is stronger than the trapping–degenerate scalar-wave Kerr–Newman estimate currently available in the literature, so the unresolved issue is genuinely a hidden–symmetry/microlocal closure at trapping. Subsection
4.10
carries out the tensorial reconstruction and scattering stage abstractly, so no further transfer or scattering hypothesis is missing once that input is supplied.

4
Full sub–extremal Kerr–Newman: conditional reconstruction

This section proves the full sub–extremal conditional results stated in Subsection
1.7
. Starting from the short–range reduction of Section
3
, we derive the complete commuted redshift/Morawetz/
r^{p}
hierarchy under the Step 6 seed, package the canonical master norms, and then establish the no–loss finite–time reconstruction and two–sided scattering theory for the charge–free Maxwell field.

4.1
Scalar specialization of the short–range master system

The exact first–order GHP system in Proposition
3.5
is the canonical radiative formulation, while Proposition
3.8
and Theorem
1.1
isolate the precise analytic content of Step 3. For the remainder of the paper we fix once and for all one short–range radiative reduction furnished by Theorem
1.1
. To keep notation light we continue to write the abstract assumptions in the scalar case
N=1
; when one instead works with a finite collection of master variables, all norms and estimates below are interpreted componentwise and summed over the collection.

Proposition 4.1

(Radiative Maxwell reduction with source)
.

Let
F
solve the sourced Maxwell system with conserved source
\mathcal{J}
, fix a stationary field
F_{\mathrm{stat}}^{\mathrm{KN}}(q_{E},q_{B})
, and set

F_{\mathrm{rad}}:=F-F_{\mathrm{stat}}^{\mathrm{KN}}(q_{E},q_{B}).

Fix one scalar realization of the short–range radiative reduction furnished by Theorem
1.1
, and let
u_{M}
denote the corresponding master variable. Then

\big(\Box_{g_{\mathrm{KN}}}+\mathcal{V}_{M}\big)u_{M}=\mathcal{S}_{M}(\mathcal{J}),

(4.1)

where
\mathcal{V}_{M}
is a smooth complex potential on
\overline{\mathcal{D}}
satisfying

\partial^{\alpha}\mathcal{V}_{M}=O(r^{-3-|\alpha|})\qquad(r\to\infty)

(4.2)

for every multiindex
\alpha
in the advanced coordinates, and
\mathcal{S}_{M}(\mathcal{J})
is a first–order linear differential operator in the source current whose coefficients obey the same short–range bounds as in Theorem
1.1
. In particular
\mathcal{S}_{M}(\mathcal{J})
depends only on the source current and its first derivatives. In the sourceless case the natural choice
(q_{E},q_{B})=(q_{E}[F],q_{B}[F])
makes
F_{\mathrm{rad}}
charge–free.

Proof.

This is the scalar specialization
N=1
of Theorem
1.1
. When one chooses instead a finite collection
U_{M}
of master variables, (
4.1
) is replaced by the matrix system (
3.58
); all later energy, Morawetz, and
r^{p}
estimates are then interpreted componentwise and summed over the collection. The only structural information used later is precisely the horizon–regular short–range form isolated in Step 3. On the Kerr edge
Q=0
this is furnished by the Fackerell–Ipser scalar, while on the Reissner–Nordström edge one may take the electric and magnetic Regge–Wheeler variables; cf. Remark
3.9
.
∎

What the symbol
\mathcal{V}_{M}
stands for.
The notation
\mathcal{V}_{M}
in Proposition
4.1
denotes the scalar short–range zeroth–order coefficient in the
N=1
specialization of Theorem
1.1
. It is intentionally schematic. Three points are important.

(i) It is not canonical on Kerr–Newman.

Even after fixing the background
(\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{KN}},g_{\mathrm{KN}})
, the radiative scalar
u_{M}
is not unique: one may work with a Fackerell–Ipser scalar, with a renormalized middle Newman–Penrose component, or with an equivalent finite collection of Teukolsky/generalized Regge–Wheeler variables. Accordingly, the exact formula for
\mathcal{V}_{M}
depends on the reduction and normalization. In the fully rotating charged case
aQ\neq 0
, the current physical–space literature is in fact most naturally written as a
coupled
system, and then the role of
\mathcal{V}_{M}
is played by a matrix of zeroth–order coefficients together with first–order couplings
[
3
,
5
]
.

(ii) In the vacuum Kerr edge
Q=0
there is a standard explicit model.

For the usual Fackerell–Ipser scalar on Kerr one may choose

u_{M}=\kappa^{-1}\upsilon_{[0]}

in the notation of
[
2
]
; then

(\Box_{g_{a,M}}+\mathcal{V}_{M})u_{M}=0,\qquad\mathcal{V}_{M}(r,\theta)=\frac{2M}{(r-ia\cos\theta)^{3}}.

(4.3)

Writing
\Sigma=r^{2}+a^{2}\cos^{2}\theta
, this is equivalently

\Re\mathcal{V}_{M}=\frac{2Mr(r^{2}-3a^{2}\cos^{2}\theta)}{\Sigma^{3}},\qquad\Im\mathcal{V}_{M}=\frac{2Ma\cos\theta(3r^{2}-a^{2}\cos^{2}\theta)}{\Sigma^{3}}.

Hence
\mathcal{V}_{M}
is real when
a=0
, genuinely complex when
a\neq 0
, smooth on the entire exterior
r>r_{+}
, and short–range at infinity:

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}_{M}(r,\theta)=\frac{2M}{r^{3}}+O(r^{-4}),

(4.4)

\displaystyle\partial_{r}^{j}\partial_{\theta}^{\ell}\mathcal{V}_{M}=O(r^{-3-j})\quad(r\to\infty).

(4.5)

(iii) These are exactly the structural features used later.

The arguments in this paper never use a special algebraic identity for
\mathcal{V}_{M}
beyond the following: the principal part is the scalar wave operator, the lower–order coefficients are smooth and horizon–regular in a suitable frame, and they are short–range in the far field so that the redshift/Morawetz/
r^{p}
analysis can close. On the Reissner–Nordström edge one usually implements this not by a single complex potential but by real electric and magnetic Regge–Wheeler master equations; in that formulation the symbol
\mathcal{V}_{M}
should be read as shorthand for the corresponding diagonal zeroth–order potentials; cf. Proposition
4.35
below and
[
1
]
.

4.2
The hidden–symmetry commutation algebra

We now execute Step 4 in Remark
4.5
at the scalar master level used in Theorem
1.8
. Once one of the short–range reductions furnished by Theorem
1.1
has been fixed and specialized to a scalar master variable
u_{M}
, the natural commutation algebra is generated by the stationary and axial Killing fields together with the modified Laplacian coming from the Carter tensor. This is exactly the symmetry package used in the Andersson–Blue physical–space method and in Giorgi’s Kerr–Newman extension of that method
[
4
]
.

Write

\displaystyle\Sigma:=r^{2}+a^{2}\cos^{2}\theta,

\displaystyle T:=\partial_{u},

\displaystyle\Phi:=\partial_{\varphi_{*}},

and let
\Delta_{S^{2}}
and
\nabla_{S^{2}}
denote the standard Laplacian and Levi–Civita connection of the unit sphere metric
d\theta^{2}+\sin^{2}\theta\,d\varphi_{*}^{2}
in the angular coordinates
(\theta,\varphi_{*})
. Define the modified Laplacian

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}:=\Delta_{S^{2}}+2a\,T\Phi+a^{2}\sin^{2}\theta\,T^{2}=\frac{1}{\sin\theta}\partial_{\theta}(\sin\theta\,\partial_{\theta})

(4.6)

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{1}{\sin^{2}\theta}\partial_{\varphi_{*}}^{2}+2a\,\partial_{u}\partial_{\varphi_{*}}+a^{2}\sin^{2}\theta\,\partial_{u}^{2}.

(4.7)

By Proposition 1.12 of
[
4
]
,
\mathcal{O}
is the elliptic representative of the Carter hidden symmetry on Kerr–Newman and satisfies

[\mathcal{O},\Sigma\Box_{g_{\mathrm{KN}}}]=0.

(4.8)

Proposition 4.2

(Step 4 for Theorem
1.8
: commutation algebra for the scalar master equation)
.

Let
u
solve

(\Box_{g_{\mathrm{KN}}}+\mathcal{V}_{M})u=G

(4.9)

on the Kerr–Newman exterior, where the scalar potential is smooth, stationary, and axisymmetric,

T\mathcal{V}_{M}=0,\qquad\Phi\mathcal{V}_{M}=0,

(4.10)

and obeys the short–range bounds (
4.2
). Then the following hold.

1.

Exact commutation by the Killing fields.

For every integers
i,j\geq 0
,

(\Box_{g_{\mathrm{KN}}}+\mathcal{V}_{M})(T^{i}\Phi^{j}u)=T^{i}\Phi^{j}G.

(4.11)

2.

Exact hidden–symmetry commutation modulo a lower–order short–range error.

Set

b^{A}:=-2\Sigma^{-1}(\nabla_{S^{2}})^{A}(\Sigma\mathcal{V}_{M}),\qquad c:=-\Sigma^{-1}\Delta_{S^{2}}(\Sigma\mathcal{V}_{M}).

(4.12)

Then
\mathcal{O}u
satisfies

(\Box_{g_{\mathrm{KN}}}+\mathcal{V}_{M})(\mathcal{O}u)=\Sigma^{-1}\mathcal{O}(\Sigma G)+b^{A}\nabla^{S^{2}}_{A}u+c\,u.

(4.13)

In particular, if
G=0
, then the only commutator error produced by
\mathcal{O}
is a first–order angular term and a zeroth–order term.

3.

Short–range size of the commutator coefficients.

For every multiindex
\alpha
in the advanced coordinates
(u,r,\theta,\varphi_{*})
,

\displaystyle\partial^{\alpha}b^{A}=O(r^{-3-|\alpha|}),

(4.14)

\displaystyle\partial^{\alpha}c=O(r^{-3-|\alpha|})

(4.15)

\displaystyle(r\to\infty).

(4.16)

Hence the commuted equation (
4.13
) remains in the same short–range class as the original master equation.

4.

Relation with the Carter operator.

Let
\mathcal{K}
denote the scalar Carter differential operator. Then

\mathcal{K}=-a^{2}\cos^{2}\theta\,\Box_{g_{\mathrm{KN}}}+\mathcal{O},\qquad[\mathcal{K},\Box_{g_{\mathrm{KN}}}]=0.

(4.17)

Consequently, for a homogeneous solution
(\Box_{g_{\mathrm{KN}}}+\mathcal{V}_{M})u=0
,

\mathcal{K}u=\mathcal{O}u+a^{2}\cos^{2}\theta\,\mathcal{V}_{M}u.

(4.18)

Thus control of
\mathcal{O}u
is equivalent to control of the hidden–symmetry commutation
\mathcal{K}u
up to a short–range zeroth–order error.

5.

Angular elliptic recovery from
T
,
\Phi
, and
\mathcal{O}
.

For every smooth scalar
\psi
and every coordinate sphere
S_{u,r}
one has

\Delta_{S^{2}}\psi=\mathcal{O}\psi-2a\,T\Phi\psi-a^{2}\sin^{2}\theta\,T^{2}\psi.

(4.19)

Therefore the standard elliptic estimate on the unit sphere gives

\displaystyle\|\nabla_{S^{2}}^{2}\psi\|_{L^{2}(S_{u,r})}+\|\nabla_{S^{2}}\psi\|_{L^{2}(S_{u,r})}

\displaystyle\leq C\Big(\|\mathcal{O}\psi\|_{L^{2}(S_{u,r})}+\|T\Phi\psi\|_{L^{2}(S_{u,r})}

(4.20)

\displaystyle\qquad+\|T^{2}\psi\|_{L^{2}(S_{u,r})}+\|\psi\|_{L^{2}(S_{u,r})}\Big)

(4.21)

where
C
is independent of
(u,r)
. Thus angular derivatives are recovered elliptically from the same symmetry algebra.

Proof.

Because
T
and
\Phi
are Killing vector fields of Kerr–Newman, they commute with
\Box_{g_{\mathrm{KN}}}
on scalar functions. Together with (
4.10
), this gives

[T,\Box_{g_{\mathrm{KN}}}+\mathcal{V}_{M}]=0,\qquad[\Phi,\Box_{g_{\mathrm{KN}}}+\mathcal{V}_{M}]=0,

and iterating proves (
4.11
).

For the hidden symmetry, start from (
4.9
) and multiply by
\Sigma
:

\Sigma\Box_{g_{\mathrm{KN}}}u+(\Sigma\mathcal{V}_{M})u=\Sigma G.

(4.22)

Apply
\mathcal{O}
and use (
4.8
):

\Sigma\Box_{g_{\mathrm{KN}}}(\mathcal{O}u)+\mathcal{O}\big((\Sigma\mathcal{V}_{M})u\big)=\mathcal{O}(\Sigma G).

Rearranging,

\Sigma(\Box_{g_{\mathrm{KN}}}+\mathcal{V}_{M})(\mathcal{O}u)=\mathcal{O}(\Sigma G)-[\mathcal{O},\Sigma\mathcal{V}_{M}]u.

Now
\Sigma\mathcal{V}_{M}
depends only on
(r,\theta)
, so the
T\Phi
and
T^{2}
pieces of
\mathcal{O}
commute with multiplication by
\Sigma\mathcal{V}_{M}
. Hence

[\mathcal{O},\Sigma\mathcal{V}_{M}]=[\Delta_{S^{2}},\Sigma\mathcal{V}_{M}].

For any scalar
f
on
S^{2}
one has the standard commutator identity

[\Delta_{S^{2}},f]\psi=2(\nabla_{S^{2}})^{A}f\,\nabla^{S^{2}}_{A}\psi+(\Delta_{S^{2}}f)\psi.

(4.23)

Applying (
4.23
) with
f=\Sigma\mathcal{V}_{M}
and dividing by
\Sigma
gives (
4.13
) with the coefficients (
4.12
).

To prove (
4.14
), note first that (
4.2
) implies

\Sigma\mathcal{V}_{M}=O(r^{-1}),\qquad\partial^{\alpha}(\Sigma\mathcal{V}_{M})=O(r^{-1-|\alpha|})

for every multiindex
\alpha
, because
\Sigma\sim r^{2}
and its derivatives grow at most like
r
. Angular covariant derivatives on
S^{2}
do not affect the
r
-decay, so

(\nabla_{S^{2}})^{A}(\Sigma\mathcal{V}_{M})=O(r^{-1}),\qquad\Delta_{S^{2}}(\Sigma\mathcal{V}_{M})=O(r^{-1}).

Multiplying by
\Sigma^{-1}=O(r^{-2})
yields precisely (
4.14
).

The relation (
4.17
) is the content of Proposition 1.12 of
[
4
]
. If
(\Box_{g_{\mathrm{KN}}}+\mathcal{V}_{M})u=0
, then
\Box_{g_{\mathrm{KN}}}u=-\mathcal{V}_{M}u
, and substituting this into (
4.17
) gives (
4.18
).

Finally, (
4.19
) is just the definition (
4.6
) rewritten to isolate
\Delta_{S^{2}}
. The standard elliptic estimate on the unit sphere,

\|\nabla_{S^{2}}^{2}\psi\|_{L^{2}(S_{u,r})}+\|\nabla_{S^{2}}\psi\|_{L^{2}(S_{u,r})}\leq C\big(\|\Delta_{S^{2}}\psi\|_{L^{2}(S_{u,r})}+\|\psi\|_{L^{2}(S_{u,r})}\big),

combined with (
4.19
), yields (
4.20
). The constant
C
is independent of
(u,r)
because the angular metric used here is the fixed round metric on
S^{2}
in the coordinates
(\theta,\varphi_{*})
.
∎

Corollary 4.3

(Triangular higher–order commuted hierarchy)
.

For
i,j,m\in\mathbb{N}
, define

Z^{(i,j,m)}:=T^{i}\Phi^{j}\mathcal{O}^{m},\qquad u_{i,j,m}:=Z^{(i,j,m)}u.

If
u
solves the homogeneous master equation

(\Box_{g_{\mathrm{KN}}}+\mathcal{V}_{M})u=0,

then every commuted field
u_{i,j,m}
satisfies an equation of the form

\displaystyle(\Box_{g_{\mathrm{KN}}}+\mathcal{V}_{M})u_{i,j,m}=\sum_{m^{\prime}<m}\sum_{i^{\prime}+j^{\prime}+2m^{\prime}\leq i+j+2m-1}\Big(b^{A}_{i,j,m;i^{\prime},j^{\prime},m^{\prime}}\,\nabla^{S^{2}}_{A}u_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime},m^{\prime}}+c_{i,j,m;i^{\prime},j^{\prime},m^{\prime}}\,u_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime},m^{\prime}}\Big),

(4.24)

where the coefficients are smooth, stationary, axisymmetric, and satisfy the same short–range bounds

\partial^{\alpha}b^{A}_{i,j,m;i^{\prime},j^{\prime},m^{\prime}}=O(r^{-3-|\alpha|}),\qquad\partial^{\alpha}c_{i,j,m;i^{\prime},j^{\prime},m^{\prime}}=O(r^{-3-|\alpha|}).

In particular, the commutation algebra generated by
T
,
\Phi
, and
\mathcal{O}
preserves the scalar short–range master class, and the hierarchy is triangular in the number of hidden–symmetry commutations.

Proof.

The proof is by induction on
m
. The case
m=0
is exactly (
4.11
). Assume the claim has been proved for all triples with fewer than
m
factors of
\mathcal{O}
, and write the corresponding inductive source schematically as

G_{i,j,m-1}=\sum_{m^{\prime}<m-1}\Big(b^{A}_{m^{\prime}}\,\nabla^{S^{2}}_{A}u_{m^{\prime}}+c_{m^{\prime}}\,u_{m^{\prime}}\Big),

where each coefficient is stationary, axisymmetric, and short range. Apply Proposition
4.2
(2) to

u_{i,j,m-1}=T^{i}\Phi^{j}\mathcal{O}^{m-1}u.

This yields

(\Box_{g_{\mathrm{KN}}}+\mathcal{V}_{M})u_{i,j,m}=\Sigma^{-1}\mathcal{O}(\Sigma G_{i,j,m-1})+b^{A}\nabla_{A}^{S^{2}}u_{i,j,m-1}+c\,u_{i,j,m-1}.

It remains to expand the first term. By the Leibniz rule, every application of
\mathcal{O}
to
\Sigma G_{i,j,m-1}
either differentiates a coefficient or differentiates one of the lower–
m
fields. When
\mathcal{O}
hits a coefficient, the result remains short range because the coefficient depends only on
(r,\theta)
and angular differentiation does not change the
r
-decay. When
\mathcal{O}
hits
u_{m^{\prime}}
, one obtains
u_{m^{\prime}+1}
, and since
m^{\prime}<m-1
this still satisfies
m^{\prime}+1<m
. When
\mathcal{O}
hits an angular derivative, use that

[\mathcal{O},\nabla_{A}^{S^{2}}]=[\Delta_{S^{2}},\nabla_{A}^{S^{2}}],

because the
T\Phi
and
T^{2}
pieces commute with
\nabla_{A}^{S^{2}}
. On the unit sphere the commutator
[\Delta_{S^{2}},\nabla_{A}^{S^{2}}]
is a smooth first–order angular operator with bounded coefficients, so

\mathcal{O}(\nabla_{A}^{S^{2}}u_{m^{\prime}})=\nabla_{A}^{S^{2}}(\mathcal{O}u_{m^{\prime}})+L_{A}u_{m^{\prime}},

for some bounded first–order angular operator
L_{A}
. The term
\nabla_{A}^{S^{2}}(\mathcal{O}u_{m^{\prime}})
again involves the field
u_{m^{\prime}+1}
with
m^{\prime}+1<m
, while
L_{A}u_{m^{\prime}}
is lower order. Thus every term produced by
\Sigma^{-1}\mathcal{O}(\Sigma G_{i,j,m-1})
has exactly the form appearing on the right–hand side of (
4.24
), with strictly smaller hidden–symmetry count. This closes the induction. The coefficient bounds are inherited from (
4.14
) together with the fact that
T
and
\Phi
annihilate all stationary axisymmetric coefficient functions.
∎

Remark 4.4
.

Proposition
4.2
and Corollary
4.3
are the rigorous content of Step 4 in Remark
4.5
for the scalar master equation used in Theorem
1.8
. The key point is that the only obstruction to exact commutation comes from the hidden–symmetry operator hitting the potential, and that error is already short range and lower order. Thus the symmetry algebra generated by
T
,
\Phi
, and
\mathcal{O}
is robust enough to support the higher–order energy and Morawetz hierarchy once the basic Step 5/Step 6 estimates are available.

4.3
Proof architecture for the abstract radiative theorem

We now begin the proof of Theorem
1.8
, stated in Subsection
1.7
. This subsection isolates the logical inputs and explains how the later steps fit together. The actual coercive quantities are constructed only after the hierarchy has been developed: Proposition
4.20
first derives the one–level inhomogeneous redshift–Morawetz estimate from the order–zero Step 6 multiplier input, Proposition
4.27
proves the one–level far–field weighted estimate, Step 8 upgrades the first of these to the full commuted non–degenerate energy and Morawetz hierarchy, Step 9 upgrades the second to the commuted outgoing
r^{p}
hierarchy and conformal control, and Step 10 fixes one canonical family of equivalent norms and closes the theorem.

For convenience we recall that the theorem concerns the homogeneous master equation

\big(\Box_{g_{\mathrm{KN}}}+\mathcal{V}_{M}\big)u=0,

commuted by the scalar–master symmetry algebra generated by
T
,
\Phi
, and
\mathcal{O}
from Proposition
4.2
. The source norm
\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{N}_{0}^{*}}
and the packaged energies
\mathcal{E}_{0}^{(k)}
,
\mathcal{M}_{0}^{(k)}
, and
\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{C},0}^{(k)}
are fixed concretely only in Step 10, after the lower–order and far–field estimates have been derived. This avoids circularity: the theorem is stated abstractly in Subsection
1.7
, while the concrete realization used in the proof is produced later in (
4.121
)–(
4.127
).

Remark 4.5

(Roadmap for the proof of Theorem
1.8
and the remaining analytic step)
.

Theorem
1.8
should not be viewed as a black box of unrelated estimates. For charge–free Maxwell on Kerr–Newman there is a definite physical–space route to it, and the main unresolved issue can be isolated rather precisely. The proof would proceed through the following steps.

1.

Subtract the stationary sector and project to the charge–free class.

First replace
F
by

F_{\mathrm{rad}}=F-F_{\mathrm{stat}}^{\mathrm{KN}}\big(q_{E}[F],q_{B}[F]\big).

Proposition
3.3
below gives the rigorous content of this step: the two charges are conserved on homologous spheres for sourceless Maxwell, the subtraction removes them exactly, and the remaining sphere components lie in the mean–zero harmonic sector. Without this step no coercive Morawetz estimate can hold, because the stationary charge modes have nonzero energy but zero spacetime decay bulk.

2.

Choose a closed collection of gauge–invariant radiative variables.

One must work with variables which vanish on the stationary family and which satisfy a closed linear system. Depending on the formulation, this can be a single Fackerell–Ipser variable or, more realistically on Kerr–Newman, a finite collection of Teukolsky and generalized Regge–Wheeler variables such as those derived in
[
3
]
. The choice is not cosmetic: the entire multiplier argument must close on this collection.

3.

Write the reduced equations in a short–range, horizon–regular form.

After the reduction, the unknowns should satisfy a system schematically of the form

\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{gRW}}\,\mathfrak{q}=\mathcal{C}_{1}[\psi],\qquad\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{T}}\,\psi=\mathcal{C}_{2}[\mathfrak{q}],

where
\mathfrak{q}
denotes derived generalized Regge–Wheeler variables and
\psi
the Teukolsky variables. The coefficients must be smooth up to the future event horizon and decaying as
r\to\infty
strongly enough for the
r^{p}
method to apply. In the present paper this step is executed in two layers: Proposition
3.8
proves that the exact extreme equations are already horizon–regular at the Teukolsky level, while Theorem
1.1
proves the additional short–range master normalization needed for the physical–space vector–field argument. This is the analytic starting point of the Kerr–Newman physical–space program
[
3
]
.

4.

Construct a commutation algebra which preserves the system.

At minimum one needs commutation by
T
,
\Phi
, and a second–order hidden–symmetry operator coming from the Carter tensor, together with angular elliptic operators on the spheres. In the scalar master formulation used for Theorem
1.8
, Proposition
4.2
below executes this step exactly with Giorgi’s modified Laplacian
\mathcal{O}
, which differs from the Carter operator only by the lower–order multiple
-a^{2}\cos^{2}\theta\,\Box_{g_{\mathrm{KN}}}
. The point is not only to gain derivatives, but to do so without destroying the short–range structure of the reduced equation. The fact that the Carter operator still commutes with the wave operator on Kerr–Newman is therefore a decisive input
[
4
]
.

5.

Prove a basic non–degenerate energy estimate.

One must build a timelike multiplier which is positive on the foliation, produces a redshift flux at the horizon, and controls the ergoregion without losing positivity to superradiant terms. In schematic form the desired estimate is

E_{N}^{(k)}[u](\tau)\leq C\,E_{N}^{(k)}[u](0)+\text{acceptable lower--order or coupling errors}.

On Kerr–Newman this is already harder than on Reissner–Nordström because
T
is not everywhere timelike and because the radiative variables are coupled.

6.

Prove the commuted Morawetz / ILED estimate.

This is the heart of Theorem
1.8
. One seeks a radial multiplier and lower–order correction currents giving

E_{N}^{(k)}[u](\tau)+\mathcal{M}_{0}^{(k)}[u]([0,\tau])\leq C\,E_{N}^{(k)}[u](0),

where
\mathcal{M}_{0}^{(k)}
is positive away from the standard trapping degeneration. The main difficulty is that in the rotating charged case the generalized Regge–Wheeler variables are
coupled through angular elliptic operators and lower–order terms
which do not commute with the simplest radial multipliers. Thus one must show that the coupling errors can be estimated in the form

|\mathrm{Err}_{\mathrm{cpl}}|\leq\varepsilon\,\mathcal{M}_{0}^{(k)}[u]+C_{\varepsilon}\,E_{\mathrm{low}}^{(k)}[u],

with
E_{\mathrm{low}}^{(k)}
absorbable after summing the commuted hierarchy. This is exactly the step which is fully understood only in the small–parameter and/or axisymmetric regimes
[
6
,
7
]
.

7.

Recover the original radiative variables from the derived ones without derivative loss.

The Teukolsky variables and the full radiative Maxwell field are not controlled directly by the generalized Regge–Wheeler Morawetz estimate; they must be recovered through transport equations, Chandrasekhar transforms, and elliptic estimates on the symmetry spheres. One therefore needs estimates of the schematic form

\|u_{\mathrm{rad}}\|_{LE^{k}}\lesssim\|\mathfrak{q}\|_{LE^{k}}+E_{\mathrm{init}}^{(k)}[u_{\mathrm{rad}}],

with no loss of top derivatives. This step is routine in principle but delicate in practice because the elliptic pieces interact with the trapping region and with the charge coupling.

8.

Commute to higher order and close the derivative hierarchy.

After Steps 5–7 are proved at one derivative level, one repeats the argument for all commuted fields. The commutators must either preserve the class of admissible equations or produce only lower–order terms that can be absorbed by induction. This is the mechanism by which the order–
k
energies and Morawetz bulks in Theorem
1.8
are obtained.

9.

Establish the far–field
r^{p}
hierarchy.

In outgoing Eddington–Finkelstein coordinates, after a suitable renormalization
\Psi=r\,u
, one applies the Dafermos–Rodnianski weighted multiplier method to obtain, for
0\leq p\leq 2
,

E_{p}[\Psi](\tau_{2})+B_{p}[\Psi]([\tau_{1},\tau_{2}])\leq C\,E_{p}[\Psi](\tau_{1})+\text{lower--order coupling errors}.

The reduced system must therefore be short–range enough at infinity that the coupling terms are perturbative in the
r^{p}
identity. Once this is done, the outgoing radiation along null infinity is quantitatively controlled
[
9
,
10
]
.

10.

Package the previous estimates into the norms appearing in Theorem
1.8
.

The non–degenerate energy
\mathcal{E}_{0}^{(k)}
, the Morawetz bulk
\mathcal{M}_{0}^{(k)}
, and the conformal / weighted energy
\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{C},0}^{(k)}
are then defined as the sums of the commuted redshift, local–energy, and
r^{p}
quantities produced in the previous steps. The polynomially growing bound (
1.2
) is weaker than the full weighted hierarchy and is therefore the easy end product once the
r^{p}
estimates are available.

The main remaining analytic step at the radiative level is Step 6, together with the derivative–loss issue resolved in Step 7. In Reissner–Nordström the reduced Maxwell system is spherically symmetric and decouples mode by mode, and in Kerr the spin–
1
Teukolsky analysis can be combined with transport/elliptic estimates for the middle components
[
2
,
11
]
. In rotating charged Kerr–Newman one must instead prove a single coercive commuted redshift–Morawetz estimate for a
coupled
Teukolsky/generalized Regge–Wheeler system in the simultaneous presence of

1.

horizon redshift,

2.

ergoregion / superradiant effects,

3.

photon–sphere trapping, and

4.

nontrivial angular elliptic coupling terms.

Once that estimate is available, the remaining pieces of Theorem
1.8
are obtained by a standard physical–space continuation of the argument. Scattering and asymptotic reconstruction are additional issues not contained in Theorem
1.8
; they are treated separately in Subsection
4.10
.

4.4
The basic non–degenerate energy estimate

We now execute Step 5 in Remark
4.5
at the scalar master level relevant for Theorem
1.8
. The point of this step is not yet to prove the full Morawetz estimate; rather, it is to show that once one replaces the Killing field
T
by a globally timelike redshifted multiplier
N
, the remaining error terms are of exactly two types: a compact spacetime bulk coming from the transition through the ergoregion, and genuinely short–range lower–order terms coming from the master potential. Those are precisely the quantities Step 6 is designed to absorb. This is the same geometric mechanism underlying the redshift method of Dafermos–Rodnianski and the physical–space Kerr–Newman framework of Giorgi
[
4
,
9
]
.

Write
\mathcal{D}(\tau_{1},\tau_{2})
for the spacetime slab bounded by
\Sigma_{\tau_{1}}
,
\Sigma_{\tau_{2}}
, the corresponding segment of
\mathcal{H}^{+}
, and future null infinity. For a complex scalar
u
define the standard stress tensor

\displaystyle\mathbb{T}_{\mu\nu}[u]:=\Re(\nabla_{\mu}u\,\overline{\nabla_{\nu}u})

(4.25)

\displaystyle\qquad-\frac{1}{2}g_{\mu\nu}\,\Re(\nabla^{\alpha}u\,\overline{\nabla_{\alpha}u}),

(4.26)

and for a vector field
X
set

J_{\mu}^{X}[u]:=\mathbb{T}_{\mu\nu}[u]X^{\nu},\qquad\pi^{X}_{\mu\nu}:=\nabla_{\mu}X_{\nu}+\nabla_{\nu}X_{\mu}.

If
\mathcal{S}
is a spacelike hypersurface with future unit normal
n_{\mathcal{S}}
, define

E_{X}[u](\mathcal{S}):=\int_{\mathcal{S}}J_{\mu}^{X}[u]\,n_{\mathcal{S}}^{\mu},

and abbreviate
E_{X}[u](\tau):=E_{X}[u](\Sigma_{\tau})
. For the horizon segment
\mathcal{H}^{+}(\tau_{1},\tau_{2})
we write

F_{\mathcal{H}^{+}}^{X}[u](\tau_{1},\tau_{2}):=\int_{\mathcal{H}^{+}(\tau_{1},\tau_{2})}J_{\mu}^{X}[u]L_{\mathcal{H}}^{\mu},

where
L_{\mathcal{H}}
is any fixed future null generator of
\mathcal{H}^{+}
. All pointwise norms below are taken with respect to a fixed smooth positive–definite background metric on
\overline{\mathcal{D}}
; on compact sets any two such choices are equivalent.

Proposition 4.6

(Step 5 for Theorem
1.8
: redshifted basic energy estimate)
.

Let
u
solve

(\Box_{g_{\mathrm{KN}}}+\mathcal{V}_{M})u=0

(4.27)

on the fixed sub–extremal Kerr–Newman exterior, where
\mathcal{V}_{M}
is stationary, axisymmetric, and satisfies (
4.2
). Then there exist

r_{+}<r_{\mathrm{H}}<R_{\mathrm{erg}}<\infty,\qquad c_{\mathrm{H}},C>0,

and a smooth future timelike vector field
N
on
\overline{\mathcal{D}}
with the following properties:

1.

N
is stationary and axisymmetric;

2.

N=T
on the far region
\{r\geq R_{\mathrm{erg}}\}
;

3.

on the near–horizon region
\mathcal{U}_{\mathrm{H}}:=\{r_{+}<r<r_{\mathrm{H}}\}
one has the redshift coercivity

\frac{1}{2}\,\mathbb{T}^{\mu\nu}[u]\pi^{N}_{\mu\nu}\geq c_{\mathrm{H}}|\nabla u|^{2}.

(4.28)

Consequently, for every
0\leq\tau_{1}\leq\tau_{2}
,

\displaystyle E_{N}[u](\tau_{2})

\displaystyle+F_{\mathcal{H}^{+}}^{N}[u](\tau_{1},\tau_{2})+c_{\mathrm{H}}\!\!\int_{\mathcal{D}(\tau_{1},\tau_{2})\cap\mathcal{U}_{\mathrm{H}}}\!\!|\nabla u|^{2}

(4.29)

\displaystyle\leq E_{N}[u](\tau_{1})+C\!\!\int_{\mathcal{D}(\tau_{1},\tau_{2})\cap\{r_{+}<r<R_{\mathrm{erg}}\}}\!\!\big(|\nabla u|^{2}+|u|^{2}\big)

\displaystyle\qquad+C\!\!\int_{\mathcal{D}(\tau_{1},\tau_{2})\cap\{r\geq R_{\mathrm{erg}}\}}\!\!r^{-3}\big(|\nabla u|^{2}+|u|^{2}\big).

In particular, the basic non–degenerate energy estimate is reduced to a compact spacetime bulk plus a genuinely short–range far–field bulk.

Proof.

Let

\Omega_{\mathcal{H}}:=\frac{a}{r_{+}^{2}+a^{2}},\qquad K_{\mathcal{H}}:=T+\Omega_{\mathcal{H}}\Phi,

so that
K_{\mathcal{H}}
is the null generator of the future event horizon. Because the horizon is sub–extremal, its surface gravity is strictly positive. The redshift theorem of Dafermos–Rodnianski therefore provides a neighborhood
\mathcal{U}_{\mathrm{H}}
of
\mathcal{H}^{+}
and a smooth future timelike vector field
N_{\mathrm{H}}
there, equal to
K_{\mathcal{H}}
on
\mathcal{H}^{+}
up to a transversal correction, such that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\,\mathbb{T}^{\mu\nu}[u]\pi^{N_{\mathrm{H}}}_{\mu\nu}\geq 2c_{\mathrm{H}}|\nabla u|^{2}

(4.30)

\displaystyle\text{on }\mathcal{U}_{\mathrm{H}}

(4.31)

for some
c_{\mathrm{H}}>0
; see
[
9
]
. Since the ergoregion of a sub–extremal Kerr–Newman exterior is compact, we may choose
R_{\mathrm{erg}}
so large that
T
is future timelike on
\{r\geq R_{\mathrm{erg}}\}
.

Let
n_{\Sigma}
denote the future unit normal to the stationary foliation
\{\Sigma_{\tau}\}
. Choose smooth cutoffs
\chi_{\mathrm{H}},\chi_{\infty}\in C^{\infty}([r_{+},\infty))
such that

\chi_{\mathrm{H}}=1\text{ on }\{r\leq r_{\mathrm{H}}\},\qquad\chi_{\mathrm{H}}=0\text{ on }\{r\geq r_{\mathrm{H}}+\delta\},

\chi_{\infty}=0\text{ on }\{r\leq R_{\mathrm{erg}}-\delta\},\qquad\chi_{\infty}=1\text{ on }\{r\geq R_{\mathrm{erg}}\},

for some small fixed
\delta>0
, and
\chi_{\mathrm{H}}+\chi_{\infty}\leq 1
. Define

N:=\chi_{\mathrm{H}}N_{\mathrm{H}}+\chi_{\infty}T+(1-\chi_{\mathrm{H}}-\chi_{\infty})n_{\Sigma}.

(4.32)

Every coefficient in (
4.32
) is nonnegative, and on the support of each coefficient the corresponding vector field is future timelike. Since the cone of future timelike vectors is convex,
N
is future timelike on all of
\overline{\mathcal{D}}
. By construction it is stationary and axisymmetric, it agrees with
N_{\mathrm{H}}
on
\mathcal{U}_{\mathrm{H}}
after possibly shrinking
r_{\mathrm{H}}
, and it equals
T
on
\{r\geq R_{\mathrm{erg}}\}
. Hence (
4.28
) follows from (
4.30
) after possibly decreasing
c_{\mathrm{H}}
. Moreover, on the compact transition region

\mathcal{A}:=\{r_{\mathrm{H}}\leq r\leq R_{\mathrm{erg}}\}

one has the uniform bound

\left|\frac{1}{2}\,\mathbb{T}^{\mu\nu}[u]\pi^{N}_{\mu\nu}\right|\leq C|\nabla u|^{2},

(4.33)

while for
r\geq R_{\mathrm{erg}}
the same term vanishes identically because
N=T
there and
T
is Killing.

For a homogeneous solution of (
4.27
), the divergence identity for the current
J^{N}
reads

\displaystyle\nabla^{\mu}J_{\mu}^{N}[u]=\frac{1}{2}\,\mathbb{T}^{\mu\nu}[u]\pi^{N}_{\mu\nu}

(4.34)

\displaystyle\qquad+\Re\big((\Box_{g_{\mathrm{KN}}}u)\,\overline{Nu}\big)=\frac{1}{2}\,\mathbb{T}^{\mu\nu}[u]\pi^{N}_{\mu\nu}-\Re\big(\mathcal{V}_{M}u\,\overline{Nu}\big).

(4.35)

Apply the divergence theorem to the truncated slab
\mathcal{D}_{R}(\tau_{1},\tau_{2}):=\mathcal{D}(\tau_{1},\tau_{2})\cap\{r\leq R\}
and then let
R\to\infty
. Because
N
is future timelike, the slice fluxes
E_{N}[u](\tau)
are non–degenerate and the horizon flux
F_{\mathcal{H}^{+}}^{N}[u](\tau_{1},\tau_{2})
is nonnegative. Because
N=T
in the far region, the outer boundary flux converges to the standard future null–infinity flux associated with
T
, which is also nonnegative and may therefore be discarded in the inequality. Using (
4.28
) and (
4.33
), we obtain

\displaystyle E_{N}[u](\tau_{2})

\displaystyle+F_{\mathcal{H}^{+}}^{N}[u](\tau_{1},\tau_{2})+c_{\mathrm{H}}\!\!\int_{\mathcal{D}(\tau_{1},\tau_{2})\cap\mathcal{U}_{\mathrm{H}}}\!\!|\nabla u|^{2}

(4.36)

\displaystyle\leq E_{N}[u](\tau_{1})+C\!\!\int_{\mathcal{D}(\tau_{1},\tau_{2})\cap\mathcal{A}}\!\!|\nabla u|^{2}+\int_{\mathcal{D}(\tau_{1},\tau_{2})}\!\!\big|\mathcal{V}_{M}u\,\overline{Nu}\big|.

It remains to estimate the potential term.

On the compact region
\{r<R_{\mathrm{erg}}\}
the coefficient
\mathcal{V}_{M}
is smooth and bounded, while
N
is a fixed smooth vector field. Hence

\big|\mathcal{V}_{M}u\,\overline{Nu}\big|\leq C\big(|u|^{2}+|\nabla u|^{2}\big)\qquad\text{on }\{r<R_{\mathrm{erg}}\}.

(4.37)

On the far region
\{r\geq R_{\mathrm{erg}}\}
we have
N=T
, and (
4.2
) gives
|\mathcal{V}_{M}|\leq Cr^{-3}
. Therefore

\displaystyle\big|\mathcal{V}_{M}u\,\overline{Nu}\big|=\big|\mathcal{V}_{M}u\,\overline{Tu}\big|\leq Cr^{-3}|u|\,

(4.38)

\displaystyle\qquad|Tu|\leq Cr^{-3}\big(|u|^{2}+|Tu|^{2}\big)\leq Cr^{-3}\big(|u|^{2}+|\nabla u|^{2}\big).

(4.39)

Substituting (
4.37
) and (
4.38
) into (
4.36
), and enlarging the constant
C
if necessary, yields exactly (
4.29
).
∎

Corollary 4.7

(Higher–order conditional basic energy estimate)
.

Let
u
solve the homogeneous scalar master equation

(\Box_{g_{\mathrm{KN}}}+\mathcal{V}_{M})u=0.

For
i,j,m\in\mathbb{N}
, let
u_{i,j,m}=T^{i}\Phi^{j}\mathcal{O}^{m}u
as in Corollary
4.3
, and for an integer
k\geq 0
define

E_{N}^{(k)}[u](\tau):=\sum_{i+j+2m\leq k}E_{N}[u_{i,j,m}](\tau),

F_{\mathcal{H}^{+}}^{N,(k)}[u](\tau_{1},\tau_{2}):=\sum_{i+j+2m\leq k}F_{\mathcal{H}^{+}}^{N}[u_{i,j,m}](\tau_{1},\tau_{2}),

\displaystyle\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{red},\mathrm{H}}^{(k)}[u](\tau_{1},\tau_{2}):=\sum_{i+j+2m\leq k}\int_{\mathcal{D}(\tau_{1},\tau_{2})\cap\mathcal{U}_{\mathrm{H}}}|\nabla u_{i,j,m}|^{2},

\displaystyle\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{loc}}^{(k)}[u](\tau_{1},\tau_{2})

\displaystyle:=\sum_{i+j+2m\leq k}\int_{\mathcal{D}(\tau_{1},\tau_{2})\cap\{r_{+}<r<R_{\mathrm{erg}}\}}\big(|\nabla u_{i,j,m}|^{2}+|u_{i,j,m}|^{2}\big),

\displaystyle\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{far}}^{(k)}[u](\tau_{1},\tau_{2})

\displaystyle:=\sum_{i+j+2m\leq k}\int_{\mathcal{D}(\tau_{1},\tau_{2})\cap\{r\geq R_{\mathrm{erg}}\}}r^{-3}\big(|\nabla u_{i,j,m}|^{2}+|u_{i,j,m}|^{2}\big).

Then, for every
0\leq\tau_{1}\leq\tau_{2}
,

\displaystyle E_{N}^{(k)}[u](\tau_{2})+F_{\mathcal{H}^{+}}^{N,(k)}[u](\tau_{1},\tau_{2})

\displaystyle\qquad+c_{\mathrm{H}}\,\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{red},\mathrm{H}}^{(k)}[u](\tau_{1},\tau_{2})

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C\Big(E_{N}^{(k)}[u](\tau_{1})

(4.40)

\displaystyle\qquad+\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{loc}}^{(k)}[u](\tau_{1},\tau_{2})+\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{far}}^{(k)}[u](\tau_{1},\tau_{2})\Big).

Proof.

Corollary
4.3
shows that every commuted field
u_{i,j,m}
satisfies an equation of the form

(\Box_{g_{\mathrm{KN}}}+\mathcal{V}_{M})u_{i,j,m}=G_{i,j,m},

where
G_{i,j,m}
is a linear combination of terms

\displaystyle b^{A}\nabla_{A}^{S^{2}}u_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime},m^{\prime}}

and

\displaystyle c\,u_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime},m^{\prime}},

with
m^{\prime}<m
and coefficients obeying the short–range bounds

|\partial^{\alpha}b^{A}|+|\partial^{\alpha}c|\leq Cr^{-3-|\alpha|}.

Repeating the proof of Proposition
4.6
for the inhomogeneous equation satisfied by
u_{i,j,m}
, one gains an extra spacetime term

\int_{\mathcal{D}(\tau_{1},\tau_{2})}\Re\big(G_{i,j,m}\,\overline{Nu_{i,j,m}}\big).

On the compact region
\{r<R_{\mathrm{erg}}\}
the coefficients
b^{A}
and
c
are bounded, so by Cauchy–Schwarz and the inequality
|Nu_{i,j,m}|\lesssim|\nabla u_{i,j,m}|
one obtains, for every
\varepsilon>0
,

\displaystyle\Big|\int_{\mathcal{D}\cap\{r<R_{\mathrm{erg}}\}}\Re\big(G_{i,j,m}\,\overline{Nu_{i,j,m}}\big)\Big|

\displaystyle\leq\varepsilon\int_{\mathcal{D}\cap\{r<R_{\mathrm{erg}}\}}|\nabla u_{i,j,m}|^{2}

\displaystyle\quad+C_{\varepsilon}\sum_{m^{\prime}<m}\int_{\mathcal{D}\cap\{r<R_{\mathrm{erg}}\}}\big(|\nabla u_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime},m^{\prime}}|^{2}+|u_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime},m^{\prime}}|^{2}\big).

On the far region
\{r\geq R_{\mathrm{erg}}\}
, the same computation and the decay of the coefficients give

\displaystyle\Big|\int_{\mathcal{D}\cap\{r\geq R_{\mathrm{erg}}\}}\Re\big(G_{i,j,m}\,\overline{Nu_{i,j,m}}\big)\Big|

\displaystyle\leq\varepsilon\int_{\mathcal{D}\cap\{r\geq R_{\mathrm{erg}}\}}r^{-3}|\nabla u_{i,j,m}|^{2}

\displaystyle\quad+C_{\varepsilon}\sum_{m^{\prime}<m}\int_{\mathcal{D}\cap\{r\geq R_{\mathrm{erg}}\}}r^{-3}\big(|\nabla u_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime},m^{\prime}}|^{2}+|u_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime},m^{\prime}}|^{2}\big).

Choose
\varepsilon>0
small enough to absorb the current-field contributions into the left–hand side of the Step 5 estimate. Because the hierarchy is triangular in the hidden–symmetry count
m
, an induction on
m
, followed by summation over all triples with
i+j+2m\leq k
, yields (
4.40
).
∎

Remark 4.8
.

Proposition
4.6
and Corollary
4.7
are the rigorous content of Step 5 in Remark
4.5
. They show that, at the scalar master level, the ergoregion/superradiance issue is converted into a compact deformation bulk by replacing
T
with a globally timelike redshifted multiplier
N
. What remains unproved is exactly Step 6: one needs a Morawetz estimate strong enough to dominate both the compact bulk
\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{loc}}^{(k)}
and the short–range far–field bulk
\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{far}}^{(k)}
. Once that is done, the basic non–degenerate energy boundedness follows immediately from (
4.40
).

Remark 4.9
.

Although Step 5 was written above in the scalar notation of Theorem
1.8
, the same proof applies verbatim to any admissible short–range collection
U_{M}
furnished by Theorem
1.1
. The only change is that the first–order matrix coefficient
\mathbb{A}^{a}\nabla_{a}U_{M}
contributes an additional far–field error of size

\int_{\mathcal{D}(\tau_{1},\tau_{2})\cap\{r\geq R_{\mathrm{erg}}\}}r^{-2}|\nabla U_{M}|^{2},

which is still short range and therefore belongs to the same Morawetz bulk that Step 6 must control. This is the form in which Step 5 is actually used in the current coupled Kerr–Newman Teukolsky/generalized Regge–Wheeler program
[
3
,
6
,
7
]
.

4.5
The commuted Morawetz / ILED estimate

We now isolate Step 6 in Remark
4.5
at the scalar master level relevant for Theorem
1.8
. The corrected current identity below is rigorous and will be used later in the one–level inhomogeneous estimate. The coercive inequality (
4.46
), however, is
not
proved in the present manuscript from this scalar current identity alone; it is recorded as a concrete sufficient local–energy input strong enough to convert the compact transition bulk and the short–range far–field bulk left over from Step 5 into a coercive spacetime term. Appendix
A
makes the geometric point precise, while Appendix
A.2
reformulates the exact remaining analytic problem: the short–range potential is perturbative, but the first–order radial current necessarily degenerates at the trapped set, so any proof of (
4.46
) on the full rotating charged range would have to add a genuinely microlocal or hidden–symmetry ingredient.

For a real vector field
X
and a real scalar weight
w
, define the corrected current

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}_{\mu}^{X,w}[v]:=\mathbb{T}_{\mu\nu}[v]X^{\nu}

(4.41)

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{1}{2}w\,\Re(\bar{v}\,\nabla_{\mu}v)-\frac{1}{4}(\nabla_{\mu}w)|v|^{2}.

(4.42)

Its associated bulk density is

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}^{X,w}[v]:=\frac{1}{2}\,\mathbb{T}^{\mu\nu}[v]\pi^{X}_{\mu\nu}

(4.43)

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{1}{2}w\,\Re(\nabla^{\alpha}v\,\overline{\nabla_{\alpha}v})-\frac{1}{4}(\Box_{g_{\mathrm{KN}}}w)|v|^{2}.

(4.44)

Lemma 4.10

(Exact corrected–current identity)
.

Let
v
solve

(\Box_{g_{\mathrm{KN}}}+\mathcal{V}_{M})v=H

for some complex source
H
, where
X
and
w
are real. Then

\nabla^{\mu}\mathcal{J}_{\mu}^{X,w}[v]=\mathcal{K}^{X,w}[v]+\Re\Big((Xv+\tfrac{1}{2}wv)\,\overline{H-\mathcal{V}_{M}v}\Big).

(4.45)

Proof.

Differentiate (
4.41
) term by term. The divergence of the first term is

\nabla^{\mu}(\mathbb{T}_{\mu\nu}[v]X^{\nu})=\frac{1}{2}\,\mathbb{T}^{\mu\nu}[v]\pi^{X}_{\mu\nu}+\Re\big((\Box_{g_{\mathrm{KN}}}v)\,\overline{Xv}\big).

For the correction terms one computes

\displaystyle\nabla^{\mu}\!\left(\frac{1}{2}w\,\Re(\bar{v}\,\nabla_{\mu}v)\right)=\frac{1}{2}(\nabla^{\mu}w)\Re(\bar{v}\,\nabla_{\mu}v)

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{1}{2}w\,\Re(\nabla^{\mu}\bar{v}\,\nabla_{\mu}v)+\frac{1}{2}w\,\Re(\bar{v}\,\Box_{g_{\mathrm{KN}}}v),

while

\nabla^{\mu}\!\left(-\frac{1}{4}(\nabla_{\mu}w)|v|^{2}\right)=-\frac{1}{4}(\Box_{g_{\mathrm{KN}}}w)|v|^{2}-\frac{1}{4}(\nabla_{\mu}w)\nabla^{\mu}(|v|^{2}).

Because
\nabla^{\mu}(|v|^{2})=2\Re(\bar{v}\,\nabla^{\mu}v)
, the two
\nabla w
terms cancel exactly. Summing the remaining contributions gives

\nabla^{\mu}\mathcal{J}_{\mu}^{X,w}[v]=\mathcal{K}^{X,w}[v]+\Re\Big((Xv+\tfrac{1}{2}wv)\,\overline{\Box_{g_{\mathrm{KN}}}v}\Big).

Substituting
\Box_{g_{\mathrm{KN}}}v=H-\mathcal{V}_{M}v
yields (
4.45
).
∎

By the same formulas as in Corollary
4.7
, for an arbitrary family

v=\{v_{I}\}_{I\in\mathcal{I}_{k}},\qquad\mathcal{I}_{k}:=\{(i,j,m)\in\mathbb{N}^{3}:i+j+2m\leq k\},

we write
E_{N}^{(k)}[v](\tau)
,
F_{\mathcal{H}^{+}}^{N,(k)}[v](\tau_{1},\tau_{2})
,
\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{red},\mathrm{H}}^{(k)}[v](\tau_{1},\tau_{2})
,
\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{loc}}^{(k)}[v](\tau_{1},\tau_{2})
, and
\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{far}}^{(k)}[v](\tau_{1},\tau_{2})
for the corresponding sums over
I\in\mathcal{I}_{k}
.

Remark 4.11

(Consistency and status of the Step 6 input)
.

The estimate (
4.46
) should be read as an external local–energy seed input, not as a direct consequence of the scalar current identity (
4.45
). A single first–order radial pointwise multiplier
X=f(r)\partial_{r}
does not by itself produce the non–degenerate bulk in (
4.46
): on Kerr/Kerr–Newman the principal symbol of the associated bulk vanishes at trapped null covectors, and for general non–axisymmetric solutions there is in addition an indefinite mixed
T\Phi
contribution. Thus the pointwise current method proves at most a trapping–degenerate estimate. The scalar-wave Kerr–Newman analogue available in the literature reflects exactly this obstruction: Civin proves an integrated local energy decay statement with a cut–off
\chi_{\natural}
vanishing in a neighborhood of the physical–space projection of the trapped set, together with a separate non–degenerate energy bound
[
12
]
. Consequently the fully non–degenerate bulk
\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{loc}}^{(k)}+\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{far}}^{(k)}
in (
4.46
) is stronger than what follows from the first–order current alone and should be understood as representing the additional hidden–symmetry/microlocal closure needed for the short–range master equation; compare also the Andersson–Blue type Kerr–Newman discussion in
[
4
]
. Appendix
A
gives a short geometric proof of the trapping obstruction and a perturbative lemma showing that the short–range potential itself is not the missing ingredient.

Proposition 4.12

(Step 6 for Theorem
1.8
: one sufficient local–energy input and its consequence)
.

Fix
k\geq 0
. Suppose that there exist smooth stationary axisymmetric real vector fields
X_{I}
and real weights
w_{I}
, indexed by
I\in\mathcal{I}_{k}
, with
X_{I}=O(1)
,
w_{I}=O(1)
and finitely many derivatives uniformly bounded, and constants
\delta_{M},c_{M},C_{M}>0
, such that every smooth family
v=\{v_{I}\}_{I\in\mathcal{I}_{k}}
satisfies

\displaystyle\delta_{M}\Big(E_{N}^{(k)}[v](\tau_{2})+F_{\mathcal{H}^{+}}^{N,(k)}[v](\tau_{1},\tau_{2})\Big)+c_{M}\Big(\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{loc}}^{(k)}[v](\tau_{1},\tau_{2})+\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{far}}^{(k)}[v](\tau_{1},\tau_{2})\Big)

(4.46)

\displaystyle\qquad\leq C_{M}E_{N}^{(k)}[v](\tau_{1})+\sum_{I\in\mathcal{I}_{k}}\int_{\mathcal{D}(\tau_{1},\tau_{2})}\Re\Big((X_{I}v_{I}+\tfrac{1}{2}w_{I}v_{I})\,\overline{(\Box_{g_{\mathrm{KN}}}+\mathcal{V}_{M})v_{I}}\Big).

Then every homogeneous master solution
u
of

(\Box_{g_{\mathrm{KN}}}+\mathcal{V}_{M})u=0

satisfies, for every
0\leq\tau_{1}\leq\tau_{2}
,

\displaystyle E_{N}^{(k)}[u](\tau_{2})

\displaystyle+F_{\mathcal{H}^{+}}^{N,(k)}[u](\tau_{1},\tau_{2})+\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{red},\mathrm{H}}^{(k)}[u](\tau_{1},\tau_{2})

(4.47)

\displaystyle\qquad+\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{loc}}^{(k)}[u](\tau_{1},\tau_{2})+\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{far}}^{(k)}[u](\tau_{1},\tau_{2})\leq C\,E_{N}^{(k)}[u](\tau_{1}).

In particular, (
4.46
) is a concrete sufficient Step 6 input. It is stronger than the raw first–order current method proved above and should be viewed as an external strengthening of that method; see Remark
4.11
and Appendix
A
.

Proof.

For each
I=(i,j,m)\in\mathcal{I}_{k}
, write

u_{I}:=T^{i}\Phi^{j}\mathcal{O}^{m}u.

By Corollary
4.3
, every
u_{I}
satisfies

(\Box_{g_{\mathrm{KN}}}+\mathcal{V}_{M})u_{I}=G_{I},

(4.48)

where
G_{I}
is a finite linear combination of terms

\displaystyle b^{A}_{I,I^{\prime}}\nabla_{A}^{S^{2}}u_{I^{\prime}}

and

\displaystyle c_{I,I^{\prime}}u_{I^{\prime}},

with
I^{\prime}=(i^{\prime},j^{\prime},m^{\prime})
satisfying
m^{\prime}<m
and with coefficients stationary, axisymmetric, smooth, and obeying the short–range bounds

\displaystyle|\partial^{\alpha}b^{A}_{I,I^{\prime}}|+|\partial^{\alpha}c_{I,I^{\prime}}|\leq C_{\alpha}\times\begin{cases}1,&r<R_{\mathrm{erg}},\\
r^{-3-|\alpha|},&r\geq R_{\mathrm{erg}}.\end{cases}

(4.49)

Apply (
4.46
) to the family
v_{I}=u_{I}
. Since
(\Box_{g_{\mathrm{KN}}}+\mathcal{V}_{M})u_{I}=G_{I}
, the right–hand side becomes

\displaystyle C_{M}E_{N}^{(k)}[u](\tau_{1})

(4.50)

\displaystyle\qquad+\sum_{I\in\mathcal{I}_{k}}\int_{\mathcal{D}(\tau_{1},\tau_{2})}\Re\Big((X_{I}u_{I}+\tfrac{1}{2}w_{I}u_{I})\,\overline{G_{I}}\Big).

(4.51)

We estimate the last term by induction on the hidden–symmetry count
m
.

Fix
I=(i,j,m)
. On the compact region
\{r<R_{\mathrm{erg}}\}
the coefficients
X_{I},w_{I},b^{A}_{I,I^{\prime}},c_{I,I^{\prime}}
are bounded. Hence, by Cauchy–Schwarz and
|X_{I}u_{I}|\lesssim|\nabla u_{I}|
,

\displaystyle\left|\int_{\mathcal{D}(\tau_{1},\tau_{2})\cap\{r<R_{\mathrm{erg}}\}}\Re\Big((X_{I}u_{I}+\tfrac{1}{2}w_{I}u_{I})\,\overline{G_{I}}\Big)\right|

\displaystyle\qquad\leq\eta\int_{\mathcal{D}(\tau_{1},\tau_{2})\cap\{r<R_{\mathrm{erg}}\}}\big(|\nabla u_{I}|^{2}+|u_{I}|^{2}\big)+C_{\eta}\sum_{I^{\prime}\prec I}\int_{\mathcal{D}(\tau_{1},\tau_{2})\cap\{r<R_{\mathrm{erg}}\}}\big(|\nabla u_{I^{\prime}}|^{2}+|u_{I^{\prime}}|^{2}\big),

(4.52)

where
I^{\prime}\prec I
means precisely that
m^{\prime}<m
in (
4.48
).

On the far region
\{r\geq R_{\mathrm{erg}}\}
, the coefficients in (
4.49
) are
O(r^{-3})
. Again using the boundedness of
X_{I},w_{I}
and Cauchy–Schwarz, one gets

\displaystyle\left|\int_{\mathcal{D}(\tau_{1},\tau_{2})\cap\{r\geq R_{\mathrm{erg}}\}}\Re\Big((X_{I}u_{I}+\tfrac{1}{2}w_{I}u_{I})\,\overline{G_{I}}\Big)\right|

\displaystyle\qquad\leq\eta\int_{\mathcal{D}(\tau_{1},\tau_{2})\cap\{r\geq R_{\mathrm{erg}}\}}r^{-3}\big(|\nabla u_{I}|^{2}+|u_{I}|^{2}\big)+C_{\eta}\sum_{I^{\prime}\prec I}\int_{\mathcal{D}(\tau_{1},\tau_{2})\cap\{r\geq R_{\mathrm{erg}}\}}r^{-3}\big(|\nabla u_{I^{\prime}}|^{2}+|u_{I^{\prime}}|^{2}\big).

(4.53)

Summing (
4.5
) and (
4.5
) over
I\in\mathcal{I}_{k}
, and choosing
\eta>0
sufficiently small relative to
c_{M}
, yields

\displaystyle\delta_{M}\Big(E_{N}^{(k)}[u](\tau_{2})+F_{\mathcal{H}^{+}}^{N,(k)}[u](\tau_{1},\tau_{2})\Big)+\frac{c_{M}}{2}\Big(\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{loc}}^{(k)}[u](\tau_{1},\tau_{2})+\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{far}}^{(k)}[u](\tau_{1},\tau_{2})\Big)

\displaystyle\qquad\leq CE_{N}^{(k)}[u](\tau_{1})+C\sum_{I^{\prime}\prec I}\Big(\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{loc}}[u_{I^{\prime}}](\tau_{1},\tau_{2})+\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{far}}[u_{I^{\prime}}](\tau_{1},\tau_{2})\Big).

(4.54)

Because the hierarchy from Corollary
4.3
is triangular in
m
, an induction on the maximal hidden–symmetry count closes the last term on the right–hand side. Thus

\displaystyle\delta_{M}\Big(E_{N}^{(k)}[u](\tau_{2})+F_{\mathcal{H}^{+}}^{N,(k)}[u](\tau_{1},\tau_{2})\Big)

\displaystyle\qquad+c\Big(\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{loc}}^{(k)}[u](\tau_{1},\tau_{2})+\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{far}}^{(k)}[u](\tau_{1},\tau_{2})\Big)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle CE_{N}^{(k)}[u](\tau_{1})

(4.55)

for some constants
c,C>0
.

Finally, combine (
4.55
) with the Step 5 estimate (
4.40
):

\displaystyle E_{N}^{(k)}[u](\tau_{2})+F_{\mathcal{H}^{+}}^{N,(k)}[u](\tau_{1},\tau_{2})

\displaystyle\qquad+c_{\mathrm{H}}\,\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{red},\mathrm{H}}^{(k)}[u](\tau_{1},\tau_{2})

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C\Big(E_{N}^{(k)}[u](\tau_{1})

\displaystyle\qquad+\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{loc}}^{(k)}[u](\tau_{1},\tau_{2})+\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{far}}^{(k)}[u](\tau_{1},\tau_{2})\Big).

Using (
4.55
) to control the last two terms proves (
4.47
).
∎

Remark 4.13
.

Proposition
4.12
is the rigorous content of Step 6 in Remark
4.5
only at the level of logical structure: assuming the local–energy input (
4.46
), the proposition shows how Step 5 closes and how the triangular commutator errors from Step 4 are absorbed. It does
not
derive (
4.46
) itself from the scalar current identity. Appendix
A
makes this precise: the short–range potential is perturbative, but the first–order radial current degenerates at trapped null geodesics, so any genuine proof of (
4.46
) would require an additional microlocal or hidden–symmetry argument beyond what is developed here. In the currently verified Kerr–Newman regimes that stronger input is furnished externally by the literature cited later in Theorem
3.10
.

Corollary 4.14

(What Step 5 and Step 6 contribute to Theorem
1.8
)
.

Under the hypotheses of Proposition
4.12
, one may take

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{0}^{(k)}[u](\tau):=E_{N}^{(k)}[u](\tau),

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{0}^{(k)}[u]([\tau_{1},\tau_{2}]):=\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{red},\mathrm{H}}^{(k)}[u](\tau_{1},\tau_{2})+\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{loc}}^{(k)}[u](\tau_{1},\tau_{2})+\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{far}}^{(k)}[u](\tau_{1},\tau_{2}).

Then the non–degenerate energy / Morawetz part of Theorem
1.8
, namely (
1.1
), holds for the scalar master equation. The remaining work needed for the full theorem statement is precisely the weighted
r^{p}
hierarchy and its packaging into
\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{C},0}^{(k)}
.

Proof.

This is exactly the estimate (
4.47
). The last sentence simply records that Theorem
1.8
also requires the conformal / weighted estimate (
1.2
) and the outgoing
r^{p}
hierarchy, which belong to the later steps of the roadmap.
∎

4.6
No–loss recovery of the exact radiative variables

We now execute Step 7 in Remark
4.5
. At this stage the non–degenerate energy and Morawetz estimate have already been obtained for the chosen short–range master variables
U_{M}
. What remains is to recover, at the
same derivative level
, the exact horizon–regular radiative variables

\displaystyle W_{M}:=(\psi_{+},\phi_{1},\psi_{-}),

\displaystyle\psi_{+}:=\Gamma^{2}\phi_{0},

\displaystyle\psi_{-}:=\bar{\Gamma}^{2}\phi_{2},

or any equivalent finite collection of exact gauge–invariant Maxwell variables. This is not yet the full tensorial scattering theorem of Theorem
1.9
; it is the finite–time transport/elliptic step needed inside Theorem
1.8
to pass from generalized Regge–Wheeler or Fackerell–Ipser variables back to the Teukolsky and middle Maxwell components without losing derivatives.

For a finite collection

W=(W_{1},\dots,W_{L}),

define the order–
k
slice energy

E_{\mathrm{rad}}^{(k)}[W](\tau):=\sum_{a=1}^{L}\ \sum_{I\in\mathcal{I}_{k}}\|Z^{I}W_{a}\|_{L^{2}(\Sigma_{\tau})}^{2},

(4.56)

where, as above,

Z^{I}:=T^{i}\Phi^{j}\mathcal{O}^{m},\qquad\mathcal{I}_{k}:=\{(i,j,m)\in\mathbb{N}^{3}:\ i+j+2m\leq k\}.

For the master collection
U_{M}=(U_{M}^{(1)},\dots,U_{M}^{(N)})
we abbreviate

\displaystyle\|U_{M}\|_{\mathcal{Y}_{M}^{(k)}([0,\tau])}^{2}:=\sup_{0\leq s\leq\tau}E_{N}^{(k)}[U_{M}](s)

(4.57)

\displaystyle\qquad+\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{red},\mathrm{H}}^{(k)}[U_{M}](0,\tau)+\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{loc}}^{(k)}[U_{M}](0,\tau)+\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{far}}^{(k)}[U_{M}](0,\tau),

(4.58)

with the natural componentwise interpretation when
N>1
.

Lemma 4.15

(Uniform
L^{2}
transport estimate)
.

Let
X
be a smooth future–directed causal vector field on
\overline{\mathcal{D}}
, stationary and axisymmetric, and uniformly transversal to the foliation
\{\Sigma_{\tau}\}
in the sense that

g(X,n_{\Sigma})\geq c_{*}>0

for some constant
c_{*}>0
. Let
b\in C^{\infty}(\overline{\mathcal{D}};\mathbb{C})
be bounded together with finitely many derivatives, and let
v
solve

(X+b)v=H.

(4.59)

Then for every
0\leq\tau_{1}\leq\tau_{2}
,

\displaystyle\|v\|_{L^{2}(\Sigma_{\tau_{2}})}^{2}+\int_{\mathcal{D}(\tau_{1},\tau_{2})}|v|^{2}\leq C\Big(\|v\|_{L^{2}(\Sigma_{\tau_{1}})}^{2}+\int_{\mathcal{D}(\tau_{1},\tau_{2})}|H|^{2}\Big),

(4.60)

where
C
depends only on
c_{*}
, the
C^{1}
bounds for
X
, and the
L^{\infty}
bound for
b
.

Proof.

Set

P^{\mu}:=|v|^{2}X^{\mu}.

Since
X
is real,

\nabla_{\mu}P^{\mu}=2\Re(\bar{v}\,Xv)+(\nabla_{\mu}X^{\mu})|v|^{2}.

Using (
4.59
),

2\Re(\bar{v}\,Xv)=2\Re(\bar{v}\,H)-2\Re(b)|v|^{2}\leq|H|^{2}+\big(1+2|b|\big)|v|^{2}.

Hence

\nabla_{\mu}P^{\mu}\leq|H|^{2}+C_{0}|v|^{2}

(4.61)

for some
C_{0}
depending only on the stated bounds. Integrate (
4.61
) over the slab
\mathcal{D}(\tau_{1},\tau_{2})
. Because
X
is future causal, the flux of
P
through
\mathcal{H}^{+}
and through null infinity is nonnegative. Because
g(X,n_{\Sigma})\geq c_{*}>0
, the slice flux of
P
is uniformly equivalent to
\|v\|_{L^{2}(\Sigma_{\tau})}^{2}
. The divergence theorem therefore gives

\|v\|_{L^{2}(\Sigma_{\tau_{2}})}^{2}\leq C\|v\|_{L^{2}(\Sigma_{\tau_{1}})}^{2}+C\int_{\mathcal{D}(\tau_{1},\tau_{2})}\big(|H|^{2}+|v|^{2}\big).

Applying Grönwall on the time slabs
[0,\tau]
yields (
4.60
). The spacetime
L^{2}
term on the left is then obtained by integrating the preceding inequality in
\tau_{2}
over
[\tau_{1},\tau_{2}]
and absorbing the resulting multiple of
\int|v|^{2}
into the left–hand side.
∎

Lemma 4.16

(Uniform sphere elliptic estimate on the mean–zero sector)
.

Let

\mathfrak{E}_{\tau,r}=\Delta_{S^{2}}+L^{A}(\tau,r,\omega)\nabla_{A}^{S^{2}}+L^{0}(\tau,r,\omega)

(4.62)

be a smooth family of second–order operators on the unit sphere, with coefficients uniformly bounded in
C^{k+1}(S^{2})
for
(\tau,r)\in[0,\infty)\times[r_{+},\infty)
. Assume that for every
(\tau,r)
the restriction of
\mathfrak{E}_{\tau,r}
to the mean–zero subspace

L^{2}_{0}(S^{2}):=\Big\{f\in L^{2}(S^{2}):\ \int_{S^{2}}f\,d\omega=0\Big\}

has trivial kernel. Then for every integer
0\leq j\leq k
and every mean–zero function
w
on
S^{2}
,

\|w\|_{H^{j+2}(S^{2})}\leq C_{j}\,\|\mathfrak{E}_{\tau,r}w\|_{H^{j}(S^{2})},

(4.63)

where
C_{j}
is independent of
(\tau,r)
.

Proof.

Because the principal part of
\mathfrak{E}_{\tau,r}
is the fixed round Laplacian
\Delta_{S^{2}}
, the family (
4.62
) is uniformly elliptic on the compact manifold
S^{2}
. Standard elliptic regularity therefore gives

\|w\|_{H^{j+2}(S^{2})}\leq C_{j}\Big(\|\mathfrak{E}_{\tau,r}w\|_{H^{j}(S^{2})}+\|w\|_{L^{2}(S^{2})}\Big),

with
C_{j}
depending continuously on the coefficient bounds. Since the coefficients vary in a bounded set of
C^{k+1}
and
S^{2}
is compact, one may choose
C_{j}
uniformly in
(\tau,r)
.

It remains to remove the
L^{2}
term. If (
4.63
) failed, one could find a sequence
(\tau_{n},r_{n})
and mean–zero functions
w_{n}
with

\|w_{n}\|_{H^{j+2}(S^{2})}=1,\qquad\|\mathfrak{E}_{\tau_{n},r_{n}}w_{n}\|_{H^{j}(S^{2})}\to 0.

By Rellich compactness, after passing to a subsequence one has
w_{n}\to w_{\infty}
strongly in
H^{j+1}(S^{2})
and weakly in
H^{j+2}(S^{2})
. Passing to the limit in the coefficients as well, one obtains a mean–zero limit
w_{\infty}
in the kernel of the limiting operator on
L^{2}_{0}(S^{2})
, contradicting the assumed kernel triviality. Hence the
L^{2}
term is absorbable, proving (
4.63
).
∎

Proposition 4.17

(Step 7 for Theorem
1.8
: abstract no–loss reconstruction of the exact radiative variables)
.

Fix one short–range reduction
U_{M}
furnished by Theorem
1.1
, and let

W_{M}=(W_{1},\dots,W_{L})

be a finite collection of exact gauge–invariant radiative variables; for the Maxwell problem one may take

W_{M}=(\psi_{+},\phi_{1},\psi_{-}).

Assume that, after ordering the components, the commuted reconstruction system has the following triangular form for every
I\in\mathcal{I}_{k}
.

1.

Transport components.

For each index
a
in a subset
\mathcal{T}\subset\{1,\dots,L\}
there is a transport operator

\mathfrak{T}_{a}=X_{a}+b_{a},

where
X_{a}
is a smooth future–directed causal stationary axisymmetric vector field, uniformly transversal to
\{\Sigma_{\tau}\}
, and
b_{a}
is a smooth bounded scalar coefficient, such that

\mathfrak{T}_{a}(Z^{I}W_{a})=H_{a,I}.

(4.64)

The right–hand side obeys the pointwise estimate

\displaystyle|H_{a,I}|\leq C\Bigg(

\displaystyle\sum_{\alpha=1}^{N}\ \sum_{J\in\mathcal{I}_{k},\ |J|_{*}\leq|I|_{*}}\big(|Z^{J}U_{M}^{(\alpha)}|+|\nabla_{S^{2}}Z^{J}U_{M}^{(\alpha)}|\big)

(4.65)

\displaystyle\quad+\sum_{b<a}\ \sum_{J\in\mathcal{I}_{k},\ |J|_{*}\leq|I|_{*}}\big(|Z^{J}W_{b}|+|\nabla_{S^{2}}Z^{J}W_{b}|\big)\Bigg).

(4.66)

2.

Sphere–elliptic components.

For each index
a
in the complementary subset
\mathcal{E}:=\{1,\dots,L\}\setminus\mathcal{T}
there is a uniformly elliptic family on the symmetry spheres

\mathfrak{E}_{a}=\Delta_{S^{2}}+L_{a}^{A}\nabla_{A}^{S^{2}}+L_{a}^{0},

with coefficients smooth and uniformly bounded together with finitely many derivatives, such that every
Z^{I}W_{a}
has zero sphere average and

\mathfrak{E}_{a}(Z^{I}W_{a})=K_{a,I},

(4.67)

where

\displaystyle|K_{a,I}|\leq C\Bigg(

\displaystyle\sum_{\alpha=1}^{N}\ \sum_{J\in\mathcal{I}_{k},\ |J|_{*}\leq|I|_{*}}\big(|Z^{J}U_{M}^{(\alpha)}|+|\nabla_{S^{2}}Z^{J}U_{M}^{(\alpha)}|\big)

(4.68)

\displaystyle\quad+\sum_{b<a}\ \sum_{J\in\mathcal{I}_{k},\ |J|_{*}\leq|I|_{*}}\big(|Z^{J}W_{b}|+|\nabla_{S^{2}}Z^{J}W_{b}|\big)\Bigg).

(4.69)

Assume moreover that each
\mathfrak{E}_{a}
has trivial kernel on the mean–zero sector.

Then there exists a radiative norm

\displaystyle\|W_{M}\|_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{rad}}^{(k)}([0,\tau])}^{2}:=\

\displaystyle\sup_{0\leq s\leq\tau}E_{\mathrm{rad}}^{(k)}[W_{M}](s)

(4.70)

\displaystyle+\sum_{a\in\mathcal{T}}\ \sum_{I\in\mathcal{I}_{k}}\int_{\mathcal{D}(0,\tau)}|Z^{I}W_{a}|^{2}

(4.71)

\displaystyle+\sum_{a\in\mathcal{E}}\ \sum_{I\in\mathcal{I}_{k}}\int_{\mathcal{D}(0,\tau)}|\nabla_{S^{2}}Z^{I}W_{a}|^{2}.

(4.72)

such that, for every
0\leq\tau<\infty
,

\displaystyle\|W_{M}\|_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{rad}}^{(k)}([0,\tau])}^{2}\leq C\Big(E_{\mathrm{rad}}^{(k)}[W_{M}](0)+\|U_{M}\|_{\mathcal{Y}_{M}^{(k)}([0,\tau])}^{2}\Big).

(4.73)

In particular, the recovery of the exact radiative variables from the derived master collection is
without top–order derivative loss
: the same order
k
appears on both sides of (
4.73
).

Proof.

We argue by induction on the triangular index
a=1,\dots,L
.

Step 1: the first component.

If
a=1
is a transport component, then the second sum on the right–hand side of (
4.65
) is empty. Apply Lemma
4.15
to each equation (
4.64
) for
Z^{I}W_{1}
, sum over
I\in\mathcal{I}_{k}
, and use the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality to obtain

\displaystyle\sup_{0\leq s\leq\tau}E_{\mathrm{rad}}^{(k)}[W_{1}](s)

(4.74)

\displaystyle\qquad+\sum_{I\in\mathcal{I}_{k}}\int_{\mathcal{D}(0,\tau)}|Z^{I}W_{1}|^{2}\leq C\Big(E_{\mathrm{rad}}^{(k)}[W_{1}](0)+\|U_{M}\|_{\mathcal{Y}_{M}^{(k)}([0,\tau])}^{2}\Big).

(4.75)

If instead
a=1
is an elliptic component, then the right–hand side of (
4.68
) again depends only on
U_{M}
. For each fixed
(\tau,r)
and
I\in\mathcal{I}_{k}
, Lemma
4.16
gives

\|Z^{I}W_{1}\|_{H^{2}(S_{\tau,r})}\leq C\|K_{1,I}\|_{L^{2}(S_{\tau,r})}.

Because
Z^{I}W_{1}
has zero average on each sphere, the Poincaré inequality yields

\|Z^{I}W_{1}\|_{L^{2}(S_{\tau,r})}+\|\nabla_{S^{2}}Z^{I}W_{1}\|_{L^{2}(S_{\tau,r})}\leq C\|K_{1,I}\|_{L^{2}(S_{\tau,r})}.

Integrating in
r
over
\Sigma_{s}
and then taking
\sup_{0\leq s\leq\tau}
gives

\displaystyle\sup_{0\leq s\leq\tau}E_{\mathrm{rad}}^{(k)}[W_{1}](s)

(4.76)

\displaystyle\qquad+\sum_{I\in\mathcal{I}_{k}}\int_{\mathcal{D}(0,\tau)}|\nabla_{S^{2}}Z^{I}W_{1}|^{2}\leq C\Big(E_{\mathrm{rad}}^{(k)}[W_{1}](0)+\|U_{M}\|_{\mathcal{Y}_{M}^{(k)}([0,\tau])}^{2}\Big).

(4.77)

Step 2: the induction step.

Assume that for some
a\geq 2
all components
W_{b}
with
b<a
satisfy

\displaystyle\|W_{b}\|_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{rad}}^{(k)}([0,\tau])}^{2}\leq C\Big(E_{\mathrm{rad}}^{(k)}[W_{b}](0)+\|U_{M}\|_{\mathcal{Y}_{M}^{(k)}([0,\tau])}^{2}\Big).

(4.78)

We prove the same estimate for
W_{a}
.

If
a\in\mathcal{T}
, apply Lemma
4.15
to (
4.64
). The source bound (
4.65
), the definition (
4.57
) of the master norm, and the induction hypothesis (
4.78
) together give

\displaystyle\sum_{I\in\mathcal{I}_{k}}\int_{\mathcal{D}(0,\tau)}|H_{a,I}|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C\Big(\|U_{M}\|_{\mathcal{Y}_{M}^{(k)}([0,\tau])}^{2}+\sum_{b<a}\|W_{b}\|_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{rad}}^{(k)}([0,\tau])}^{2}\Big)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C\Big(E_{\mathrm{rad}}^{(k)}[W_{<a}](0)+\|U_{M}\|_{\mathcal{Y}_{M}^{(k)}([0,\tau])}^{2}\Big).

where
E_{\mathrm{rad}}^{(k)}[W_{<a}](0):=\sum_{b<a}E_{\mathrm{rad}}^{(k)}[W_{b}](0)
. Therefore

\displaystyle\sup_{0\leq s\leq\tau}E_{\mathrm{rad}}^{(k)}[W_{a}](s)

(4.79)

\displaystyle\qquad+\sum_{I\in\mathcal{I}_{k}}\int_{\mathcal{D}(0,\tau)}|Z^{I}W_{a}|^{2}\leq C\Big(E_{\mathrm{rad}}^{(k)}[W_{\leq a}](0)+\|U_{M}\|_{\mathcal{Y}_{M}^{(k)}([0,\tau])}^{2}\Big).

(4.80)

If
a\in\mathcal{E}
, then apply Lemma
4.16
to (
4.67
) on each sphere
S_{\tau,r}
. Using again the pointwise bound (
4.68
), Poincaré on the mean–zero sector, the master norm (
4.57
), and the induction hypothesis (
4.78
), we obtain

\displaystyle\sup_{0\leq s\leq\tau}E_{\mathrm{rad}}^{(k)}[W_{a}](s)

(4.81)

\displaystyle\qquad+\sum_{I\in\mathcal{I}_{k}}\int_{\mathcal{D}(0,\tau)}|\nabla_{S^{2}}Z^{I}W_{a}|^{2}\leq C\Big(E_{\mathrm{rad}}^{(k)}[W_{\leq a}](0)+\|U_{M}\|_{\mathcal{Y}_{M}^{(k)}([0,\tau])}^{2}\Big).

(4.82)

Step 3: sum over the triangular system.

Summing (
4.79
) and (
4.81
) over
a=1,\dots,L
yields exactly (
4.73
). Because the right–hand sides (
4.65
) and (
4.68
) only involve commuted master variables
Z^{J}U_{M}
with
|J|_{*}\leq|I|_{*}
, the derivative order on the right never exceeds the derivative order on the left. This is the precise no–loss statement.
∎

Corollary 4.18

(What Step 7 contributes to Theorem
1.8
)
.

Under the hypotheses of Proposition
4.17
, the physical–space bounds from Step 5/Step 6 for the master collection
U_{M}
imply the corresponding same–order local energy and Morawetz control for the exact radiative variables
W_{M}
. In particular, once Step 6 is available, Step 7 removes the derivative–loss obstruction in passing from the derived master variables back to the exact Maxwell radiative variables. The remaining ingredients for Theorem
1.8
are therefore the higher–order closure of Step 8, the far–field
r^{p}
hierarchy of Step 9, and the final packaging of Step 10.

Proof.

Insert the Step 5/Step 6 estimate for
U_{M}
into (
4.73
). Because the norm (
4.57
) controls the commuted slice energy and the local spacetime bulk of
U_{M}
, Proposition
4.17
immediately yields the same derivative order control for
W_{M}
. No further argument is needed.
∎

Remark 4.19
.

Proposition
4.17
is the rigorous content of Step 7 in Remark
4.5
. The only genuinely structural inputs are:

1.

the mean–zero property from Step 1, which removes the
\ell=0
obstruction from the sphere–elliptic inversion;

2.

a triangular transport/elliptic reconstruction system, which is exactly the finite–time part of Theorem
1.1
; and

3.

the Step 5/Step 6 master estimate for the derived variables.

In Reissner–Nordström the reconstruction is the Hodge/Regge–Wheeler inversion of the electric and magnetic master scalars; in Kerr it is the transport–elliptic recovery of the middle components from the Teukolsky/Fackerell–Ipser variables; and on rotating charged Kerr–Newman this is the stage at which one must invert the coupled Chandrasekhar–type transforms without raising the derivative count. That, and not the formal writing of the Teukolsky equations, is the real analytic content of Step 7.

4.7
Closure of the full higher–order derivative hierarchy

We now execute Step 8 in Remark
4.5
. The point of this step is to show that, now that Proposition
4.20
has established the one–level physical–space estimate for the short–range master equation from the
k=0
Step 6 input, no essentially new multiplier construction is needed at higher derivative order. The full hierarchy follows from two facts already established in Steps 4 and 7: the commuted equations are triangular in the weighted order

|I|_{*}:=i+j+2m,\qquad I=(i,j,m),

and the recovery of the exact radiative variables from the master variables is without top–order loss.

For a single scalar field
v
and
0\leq\tau_{1}\leq\tau_{2}
, write

\displaystyle\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{red},\mathrm{H}}[v](\tau_{1},\tau_{2})

\displaystyle:=\int_{\mathcal{D}(\tau_{1},\tau_{2})\cap\mathcal{U}_{\mathrm{H}}}|\nabla v|^{2},

\displaystyle\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{loc}}[v](\tau_{1},\tau_{2})

\displaystyle:=\int_{\mathcal{D}(\tau_{1},\tau_{2})\cap\{r_{+}<r<R_{\mathrm{erg}}\}}\big(|\nabla v|^{2}+|v|^{2}\big),

\displaystyle\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{far}}[v](\tau_{1},\tau_{2})

\displaystyle:=\int_{\mathcal{D}(\tau_{1},\tau_{2})\cap\{r\geq R_{\mathrm{erg}}\}}r^{-3}\big(|\nabla v|^{2}+|v|^{2}\big).

Define the dual source norm

\displaystyle\|H\|_{\mathcal{N}_{M,*}(\tau_{1},\tau_{2})}^{2}:=\int_{\mathcal{D}(\tau_{1},\tau_{2})\cap\{r_{+}<r<R_{\mathrm{erg}}\}}|H|^{2}

(4.83)

\displaystyle\qquad+\int_{\mathcal{D}(\tau_{1},\tau_{2})\cap\{r\geq R_{\mathrm{erg}}\}}r^{3}|H|^{2}.

(4.84)

The weight
r^{3}
is exactly dual to the far–field bulk
r^{-3}|\nabla v|^{2}
from Steps 5 and 6.

Proposition 4.20

(Derivation of the one–level inhomogeneous redshift–Morawetz estimate)
.

Assume the
k=0
case of the multiplier hypothesis (
4.46
) from Proposition
4.12
. Then every smooth solution
v
of

(\Box_{g_{\mathrm{KN}}}+\mathcal{V}_{M})v=H

satisfies the one–level estimate stated later as (
4.89
); explicitly,

\displaystyle E_{N}[v](\tau_{2})+F_{\mathcal{H}^{+}}^{N}[v](\tau_{1},\tau_{2})+\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{red},\mathrm{H}}[v](\tau_{1},\tau_{2})+\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{loc}}[v](\tau_{1},\tau_{2})+\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{far}}[v](\tau_{1},\tau_{2})

\displaystyle\qquad\leq C_{*}\Big(E_{N}[v](\tau_{1})+\|H\|_{\mathcal{N}_{M,*}(\tau_{1},\tau_{2})}^{2}\Big).

Proof.

Let
X,w,\delta_{M},c_{M},C_{M}
be the
k=0
multiplier data appearing in (
4.46
). Apply that inequality to the one–component family
v
. Since
(\Box_{g_{\mathrm{KN}}}+\mathcal{V}_{M})v=H
, we obtain

\displaystyle\delta_{M}\Big(E_{N}[v](\tau_{2})+F_{\mathcal{H}^{+}}^{N}[v](\tau_{1},\tau_{2})\Big)+c_{M}\Big(\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{loc}}[v](\tau_{1},\tau_{2})+\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{far}}[v](\tau_{1},\tau_{2})\Big)

\displaystyle\qquad\leq C_{M}E_{N}[v](\tau_{1})+\int_{\mathcal{D}(\tau_{1},\tau_{2})}\Re\Big((Xv+\tfrac{1}{2}wv)\,\overline{H}\Big).

We estimate the source term by splitting the slab into the compact and far regions. Since
X
and
w
are bounded and stationary,

|Xv+\tfrac{1}{2}wv|\leq C\big(|\nabla v|+|v|\big).

Hence, on
\{r_{+}<r<R_{\mathrm{erg}}\}
,

\displaystyle\left|\int_{\mathcal{D}\cap\{r_{+}<r<R_{\mathrm{erg}}\}}\Re\Big((Xv+\tfrac{1}{2}wv)\,\overline{H}\Big)\right|\leq\eta\,

\displaystyle\qquad\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{loc}}[v](\tau_{1},\tau_{2})+C_{\eta}\int_{\mathcal{D}\cap\{r_{+}<r<R_{\mathrm{erg}}\}}|H|^{2}.

On
\{r\geq R_{\mathrm{erg}}\}
we insert the dual weights
r^{\pm 3/2}
and use Cauchy–Schwarz:

\displaystyle\left|\int_{\mathcal{D}\cap\{r\geq R_{\mathrm{erg}}\}}\Re\Big((Xv+\tfrac{1}{2}wv)\,\overline{H}\Big)\right|

\displaystyle\qquad\leq\left(\int_{\mathcal{D}\cap\{r\geq R_{\mathrm{erg}}\}}r^{3}|H|^{2}\right)^{1/2}\left(\int_{\mathcal{D}\cap\{r\geq R_{\mathrm{erg}}\}}r^{-3}|Xv+\tfrac{1}{2}wv|^{2}\right)^{1/2}

\displaystyle\qquad\leq\eta\,\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{far}}[v](\tau_{1},\tau_{2})+C_{\eta}\int_{\mathcal{D}\cap\{r\geq R_{\mathrm{erg}}\}}r^{3}|H|^{2}.

Combining the last two bounds and choosing
\eta>0
small relative to
c_{M}
yields

\displaystyle E_{N}[v](\tau_{2})+F_{\mathcal{H}^{+}}^{N}[v](\tau_{1},\tau_{2})+\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{loc}}[v](\tau_{1},\tau_{2})+\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{far}}[v](\tau_{1},\tau_{2})\leq C\Big(E_{N}[v](\tau_{1})+\|H\|_{\mathcal{N}_{M,*}(\tau_{1},\tau_{2})}^{2}\Big).

It remains to recover the redshift bulk. Repeat the proof of Proposition
4.6
for the inhomogeneous equation
(\Box_{g_{\mathrm{KN}}}+\mathcal{V}_{M})v=H
. The divergence identity (
4.34
) acquires the extra source term
\Re(H\,\overline{Nv})
, so exactly the same argument gives

\displaystyle E_{N}[v](\tau_{2})+F_{\mathcal{H}^{+}}^{N}[v](\tau_{1},\tau_{2})+c_{\mathrm{H}}\,\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{red},\mathrm{H}}[v](\tau_{1},\tau_{2})

\displaystyle\qquad\leq E_{N}[v](\tau_{1})+C\Big(\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{loc}}[v](\tau_{1},\tau_{2})+\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{far}}[v](\tau_{1},\tau_{2})\Big)+\int_{\mathcal{D}(\tau_{1},\tau_{2})}|H|\,|Nv|.

Since
N
is a fixed smooth timelike vector field,

|Nv|\leq C|\nabla v|.

Therefore the same compact/far decomposition used above gives

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{D}(\tau_{1},\tau_{2})}|H|\,|Nv|\leq\eta\Big(\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{loc}}[v](\tau_{1},\tau_{2})+\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{far}}[v](\tau_{1},\tau_{2})\Big)

\displaystyle\qquad+C_{\eta}\|H\|_{\mathcal{N}_{M,*}(\tau_{1},\tau_{2})}^{2}.

Insert this into the preceding redshift inequality and use the already established local/far bound to control the local and far bulks. This yields

\displaystyle E_{N}[v](\tau_{2})+F_{\mathcal{H}^{+}}^{N}[v](\tau_{1},\tau_{2})+\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{red},\mathrm{H}}[v](\tau_{1},\tau_{2})\leq C\Big(E_{N}[v](\tau_{1})+\|H\|_{\mathcal{N}_{M,*}(\tau_{1},\tau_{2})}^{2}\Big).

Finally, add the local/far estimate and the redshift estimate. After enlarging the constant, this is exactly the desired one–level estimate.
∎

Lemma 4.21

(Commutator sources are lower order in the dual norm)
.

Let
u
solve the homogeneous scalar master equation

(\Box_{g_{\mathrm{KN}}}+\mathcal{V}_{M})u=0,

and let

u_{I}:=Z^{I}u=T^{i}\Phi^{j}\mathcal{O}^{m}u,\qquad|I|_{*}:=i+j+2m.

For every
I
with
|I|_{*}=n\geq 1
, let
G_{I}
denote the commutator source in

(\Box_{g_{\mathrm{KN}}}+\mathcal{V}_{M})u_{I}=G_{I}.

(4.85)

Then for every
0\leq\tau_{1}\leq\tau_{2}
,

\displaystyle\|G_{I}\|_{\mathcal{N}_{M,*}(\tau_{1},\tau_{2})}^{2}\leq C_{n}\sum_{|J|_{*}\leq n-1}\Big(\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{loc}}[u_{J}](\tau_{1},\tau_{2})+\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{far}}[u_{J}](\tau_{1},\tau_{2})\Big).

(4.86)

Proof.

By Corollary
4.3
,
G_{I}
is a finite sum of terms of the form

\displaystyle b^{A}_{I,J}\nabla_{A}^{S^{2}}u_{J}

and

\displaystyle c_{I,J}u_{J},

with
|J|_{*}\leq n-1
and coefficients satisfying the piecewise bounds

\displaystyle|\partial^{\alpha}b^{A}_{I,J}|+|\partial^{\alpha}c_{I,J}|\leq C_{\alpha,n}\times\begin{cases}1,&r<R_{\mathrm{erg}},\\
r^{-3-|\alpha|},&r\geq R_{\mathrm{erg}}.\end{cases}

Hence, on the compact region
\{r<R_{\mathrm{erg}}\}
,

|G_{I}|^{2}\leq C_{n}\sum_{|J|_{*}\leq n-1}\big(|\nabla_{S^{2}}u_{J}|^{2}+|u_{J}|^{2}\big)\leq C_{n}\sum_{|J|_{*}\leq n-1}\big(|\nabla u_{J}|^{2}+|u_{J}|^{2}\big),

where the last inequality uses that the sphere derivatives are bounded by the full gradient with respect to the fixed background Riemannian metric used throughout the paper. Integrating this over
\mathcal{D}(\tau_{1},\tau_{2})\cap\{r_{+}<r<R_{\mathrm{erg}}\}
gives

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{D}\cap\{r_{+}<r<R_{\mathrm{erg}}\}}|G_{I}|^{2}\leq C_{n}\sum_{|J|_{*}\leq n-1}\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{loc}}[u_{J}](\tau_{1},\tau_{2}).

(4.87)

On the far region
\{r\geq R_{\mathrm{erg}}\}
the coefficients are
O(r^{-3})
, so

|G_{I}|^{2}\leq C_{n}\sum_{|J|_{*}\leq n-1}r^{-6}\big(|\nabla_{S^{2}}u_{J}|^{2}+|u_{J}|^{2}\big).

Multiplying by
r^{3}
and using again
|\nabla_{S^{2}}u_{J}|\leq|\nabla u_{J}|
yields

r^{3}|G_{I}|^{2}\leq C_{n}\sum_{|J|_{*}\leq n-1}r^{-3}\big(|\nabla u_{J}|^{2}+|u_{J}|^{2}\big).

Integrating over
\mathcal{D}(\tau_{1},\tau_{2})\cap\{r\geq R_{\mathrm{erg}}\}
gives

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{D}\cap\{r\geq R_{\mathrm{erg}}\}}r^{3}|G_{I}|^{2}\leq C_{n}\sum_{|J|_{*}\leq n-1}\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{far}}[u_{J}](\tau_{1},\tau_{2}).

(4.88)

Combining (
4.87
) and (
4.88
) proves (
4.86
).
∎

Proposition 4.22

(Step 8 for Theorem
1.8
: closure of the full higher–order derivative hierarchy)
.

Assume the
k=0
case of the multiplier hypothesis (
4.46
) from Proposition
4.12
. Proposition
4.20
then furnishes a constant
C_{*}
such that every smooth solution
v
of the inhomogeneous scalar master equation

(\Box_{g_{\mathrm{KN}}}+\mathcal{V}_{M})v=H

satisfies, for every
0\leq\tau_{1}\leq\tau_{2}
, the one–level estimate

\displaystyle E_{N}[v](\tau_{2})+F_{\mathcal{H}^{+}}^{N}[v](\tau_{1},\tau_{2})+\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{red},\mathrm{H}}[v](\tau_{1},\tau_{2})+\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{loc}}[v](\tau_{1},\tau_{2})+\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{far}}[v](\tau_{1},\tau_{2})

(4.89)

\displaystyle\qquad\leq C_{*}\Big(E_{N}[v](\tau_{1})+\|H\|_{\mathcal{N}_{M,*}(\tau_{1},\tau_{2})}^{2}\Big).

(4.90)

Thus, within the scalar master framework of Steps 5 and 6, (
4.89
) is no longer a hypothesis: it is the proved order–zero redshift–Morawetz estimate with source, recorded here for later reference.

Then every homogeneous master solution
u
of

(\Box_{g_{\mathrm{KN}}}+\mathcal{V}_{M})u=0

satisfies, for every integer
k\geq 0
and every
0\leq\tau_{1}\leq\tau_{2}
,

\displaystyle E_{N}^{(k)}[u](\tau_{2})+F_{\mathcal{H}^{+}}^{N,(k)}[u](\tau_{1},\tau_{2})+\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{red},\mathrm{H}}^{(k)}[u](\tau_{1},\tau_{2})

(4.91)

\displaystyle\qquad+\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{loc}}^{(k)}[u](\tau_{1},\tau_{2})+\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{far}}^{(k)}[u](\tau_{1},\tau_{2})\leq C_{k}\,E_{N}^{(k)}[u](\tau_{1}).

(4.92)

If, in addition,
W_{M}
is any exact radiative collection reconstructed from the master variable as in Proposition
4.17
, then

\displaystyle\|W_{M}\|_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{rad}}^{(k)}([0,\tau])}^{2}\leq C_{k}\Big(E_{\mathrm{rad}}^{(k)}[W_{M}](0)+E_{N}^{(k)}[u](0)\Big)

(4.93)

\displaystyle\text{for all }\tau\geq 0.

(4.94)

Proof.

For each integer
n\geq 0
, define the weighted shell

\mathcal{J}_{n}:=\{I=(i,j,m):\ |I|_{*}=i+j+2m=n\},

and the corresponding shell energy-bulk quantity

\displaystyle\mathfrak{H}_{n}[u](\tau_{1},\tau_{2}):=\sum_{I\in\mathcal{J}_{n}}\Big(

\displaystyle E_{N}[u_{I}](\tau_{2})+F_{\mathcal{H}^{+}}^{N}[u_{I}](\tau_{1},\tau_{2})

(4.95)

\displaystyle+\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{red},\mathrm{H}}[u_{I}](\tau_{1},\tau_{2})+\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{loc}}[u_{I}](\tau_{1},\tau_{2})+\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{far}}[u_{I}](\tau_{1},\tau_{2})\Big)

(4.96)

We prove by induction on
n
that

\sum_{m=0}^{n}\mathfrak{H}_{m}[u](\tau_{1},\tau_{2})\leq C_{n}\sum_{|I|_{*}\leq n}E_{N}[u_{I}](\tau_{1}).

(4.97)

Since the right–hand side is exactly
E_{N}^{(n)}[u](\tau_{1})
and the left–hand side equals the order–
n
version of the left side of (
4.91
), this proves the master estimate.

Base case
n=0
.

The shell
\mathcal{J}_{0}
contains only
I=(0,0,0)
, so
u_{I}=u
and
G_{I}=0
. Applying the proved estimate (
4.89
) with
H=0
gives

\mathfrak{H}_{0}[u](\tau_{1},\tau_{2})\leq C_{*}E_{N}[u](\tau_{1}),

which is exactly (
4.97
) for
n=0
.

Induction step.

Assume (
4.97
) has been proved up to level
n-1
, and let
I\in\mathcal{J}_{n}
. Then
u_{I}
satisfies (
4.85
). Apply the one–level estimate (
4.89
) to
v=u_{I}
and
H=G_{I}
:

\mathfrak{H}_{I}[u](\tau_{1},\tau_{2})\leq C_{*}\Big(E_{N}[u_{I}](\tau_{1})+\|G_{I}\|_{\mathcal{N}_{M,*}(\tau_{1},\tau_{2})}^{2}\Big),

(4.98)

where
\mathfrak{H}_{I}[u]
denotes the
I
-summand in (
4.95
). By Lemma
4.21
,

\displaystyle\|G_{I}\|_{\mathcal{N}_{M,*}(\tau_{1},\tau_{2})}^{2}\leq C_{n}\sum_{|J|_{*}\leq n-1}\Big(\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{loc}}[u_{J}](\tau_{1},\tau_{2})+\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{far}}[u_{J}](\tau_{1},\tau_{2})\Big).

Substituting this into (
4.98
) and summing over
I\in\mathcal{J}_{n}
yields

\displaystyle\mathfrak{H}_{n}[u](\tau_{1},\tau_{2})\leq C_{n}\sum_{I\in\mathcal{J}_{n}}E_{N}[u_{I}](\tau_{1})

(4.99)

\displaystyle\qquad+C_{n}\sum_{|J|_{*}\leq n-1}\Big(\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{loc}}[u_{J}](\tau_{1},\tau_{2})+\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{far}}[u_{J}](\tau_{1},\tau_{2})\Big).

(4.100)

The second term on the right is bounded by the lower–order induction hypothesis (
4.97
). Therefore,

\mathfrak{H}_{n}[u](\tau_{1},\tau_{2})\leq C_{n}\sum_{|I|_{*}\leq n}E_{N}[u_{I}](\tau_{1}),

and adding the already controlled lower shells gives (
4.97
) at level
n
. This closes the induction and proves (
4.91
).

Finally, (
4.93
) follows by inserting (
4.91
) into the no–loss reconstruction estimate (
4.73
) from Proposition
4.17
. Because Proposition
4.17
uses the same derivative order
k
on both sides, no derivative loss is introduced at this stage.
∎

Corollary 4.23

(What Step 8 contributes to Theorem
1.8
)
.

Assume the
k=0
Step 6 multiplier hypothesis (
4.46
), together with the Step 7 reconstruction hypotheses. Then the non–degenerate energy and Morawetz parts of Theorem
1.8
hold at every commuted order
k
. In particular, after Step 8 the only remaining analytic input for Theorem
1.8
is the far–field
r^{p}
hierarchy of Step 9, followed by the bookkeeping of Step 10.

Proof.

Proposition
4.20
first establishes the one–level estimate (
4.89
) from the
k=0
case of (
4.46
). The first assertion is then exactly the content of (
4.91
) and (
4.93
). The last sentence is only a reformulation of the roadmap in Remark
4.5
: Steps 1–8 now provide stationary subtraction, exact radiative variables, a short–range reduction, the commutation algebra, the basic redshift estimate, Morawetz closure, no–loss reconstruction, and the full higher–order derivative hierarchy.
