What remains is to combine these with the far–field weighted multiplier argument.
∎

Remark 4.24
.

Proposition
4.22
is the rigorous content of Step 8 in Remark
4.5
. The proposition is deliberately formulated so as to isolate the bookkeeping aspect of the argument from the genuinely geometric multiplier construction of Step 6. Step 8 proves that, once Proposition
4.20
has supplied the one–level short–range estimate, the passage to arbitrarily many commuted derivatives requires no new geometry: it is a strict lower–order induction in the weighted commutator order, and the only additional analytic ingredient is the no–loss reconstruction from Step 7.

4.8
The far–field weighted hierarchy

We now execute Step 9 in Remark
4.5
. After Step 8, the non–degenerate energy, redshift, and Morawetz estimates already hold at every commuted order. The remaining issue is to propagate that control to future null infinity by the Dafermos–Rodnianski weighted null method. In the far field one works with the renormalized unknown

\Psi:=r\,u,\qquad\Psi_{I}:=r\,u_{I}\quad\text{for}\quad u_{I}:=Z^{I}u,

and with an outgoing double–null chart
(t-r_{*},t+r_{*},\omega)
on
\{r\geq R\}
, where
r_{*}
is any fixed tortoise coordinate and
\omega=(\theta,\varphi_{*})
. For definiteness we write

\tau:=t-r_{*},\qquad v:=t+r_{*},

so that the outgoing null hypersurfaces are

N_{\tau}^{R}:=\{t-r_{*}=\tau,\ r\geq R\}.

We also write

\mathcal{N}_{R}(\tau_{1},\tau_{2}):=\{\tau_{1}\leq t-r_{*}\leq\tau_{2},\ r\geq R\}.

In the asymptotically flat region, the volume form is equivalent to
dv\,d\omega
on
N_{\tau}^{R}
and to
d\tau\,dv\,d\omega
on
\mathcal{N}_{R}(\tau_{1},\tau_{2})
, so these measures are sufficient for the weighted hierarchy.

For
0\leq p\leq 2
define the outgoing weighted flux, bulk, radiation flux, and weighted source norm by

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{p,R}[\Psi](\tau)

\displaystyle:=\int_{N_{\tau}^{R}}\Big(r^{p}|\partial_{v}\Psi|^{2}+r^{p-2}|\nabla_{S^{2}}\Psi|^{2}+r^{p-2}|\Psi|^{2}\Big)\,dv\,d\omega,

(4.101)

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}_{p,R}[\Psi](\tau_{1},\tau_{2})

\displaystyle:=\int_{\mathcal{N}_{R}(\tau_{1},\tau_{2})}\Big(r^{p-1}|\partial_{v}\Psi|^{2}+r^{p-3}|\nabla_{S^{2}}\Psi|^{2}+r^{p-3}|\Psi|^{2}\Big)\,d\tau\,dv\,d\omega,

(4.102)

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{p,R}[\Psi](\tau_{1},\tau_{2})

\displaystyle:=\limsup_{v_{0}\to\infty}\int_{\tau_{1}}^{\tau_{2}}\!\int_{S^{2}}r^{p}|\partial_{v}\Psi(\tau,v_{0},\omega)|^{2}\,d\omega\,d\tau,

(4.103)

\displaystyle\|H\|_{\mathcal{N}_{p,R}^{*}}(\tau_{1},\tau_{2})^{2}

\displaystyle:=\int_{\mathcal{N}_{R}(\tau_{1},\tau_{2})}r^{p+1}|H|^{2}\,d\tau\,dv\,d\omega.

(4.104)

For commuted fields we abbreviate

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{p,R}^{(k)}[u](\tau)

\displaystyle:=\sum_{|I|_{*}\leq k}\mathcal{E}_{p,R}[\Psi_{I}](\tau),

(4.105)

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}_{p,R}^{(k)}[u](\tau_{1},\tau_{2})

\displaystyle:=\sum_{|I|_{*}\leq k}\mathcal{B}_{p,R}[\Psi_{I}](\tau_{1},\tau_{2}),

(4.106)

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{p,R}^{(k)}[u](\tau_{1},\tau_{2})

\displaystyle:=\sum_{|I|_{*}\leq k}\mathcal{F}_{p,R}[\Psi_{I}](\tau_{1},\tau_{2}).

(4.107)

Lemma 4.25

(Model
r^{p}
identity for the renormalized field)
.

Let

L_{0}\Psi:=-4\partial_{\tau}\partial_{v}\Psi+r^{-2}\Delta_{S^{2}}\Psi.

Then for every smooth complex scalar
\Psi
on
\mathcal{N}_{R}(\tau_{1},\tau_{2})
and every
0\leq p\leq 2
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{p,R}[\Psi](\tau_{2})+\mathcal{F}_{p,R}[\Psi](\tau_{1},\tau_{2})+\int_{\mathcal{N}_{R}(\tau_{1},\tau_{2})}\Big(r^{p-1}|\partial_{v}\Psi|^{2}+(2-p)r^{p-3}|\nabla_{S^{2}}\Psi|^{2}\Big)\,d\tau\,dv\,d\omega

(4.108)

\displaystyle\qquad\leq C_{p}\,\mathcal{E}_{p,R}[\Psi](\tau_{1})+C_{p}\left|\int_{\mathcal{N}_{R}(\tau_{1},\tau_{2})}r^{p}\,\partial_{v}\overline{\Psi}\,L_{0}\Psi\,d\tau\,dv\,d\omega\right|

(4.109)

\displaystyle\qquad\quad+C_{p}\int_{\{r=R,\ \tau_{1}\leq t-r_{*}\leq\tau_{2}\}}\big(|\partial_{v}\Psi|^{2}+|\nabla_{S^{2}}\Psi|^{2}\big)\,d\tau\,d\omega.

(4.110)

Proof.

Multiply the identity defining
L_{0}\Psi
by
r^{p}\partial_{v}\overline{\Psi}
, take real parts, and integrate over
\mathcal{N}_{R}(\tau_{1},\tau_{2})
. The term
-4\partial_{\tau}\partial_{v}\Psi
is integrated by parts in
\tau
and gives the weighted boundary flux
\mathcal{E}_{p,R}[\Psi]
together with the positive bulk
\int r^{p-1}|\partial_{v}\Psi|^{2}
. The angular term is integrated by parts on
S^{2}
and then in
v
; since
\partial_{v}r=\tfrac{1}{2}+O(r^{-1})
in the asymptotic chart, the resulting positive bulk is comparable to
(2-p)\int r^{p-3}|\nabla_{S^{2}}\Psi|^{2}
when
R
is fixed sufficiently large. The boundary at
v=\infty
is exactly the radiation flux
\mathcal{F}_{p,R}[\Psi](\tau_{1},\tau_{2})
, and the inner radial boundary is the last term in (
4.108
). All harmless equivalence constants are absorbed into
C_{p}
.
∎

Lemma 4.26

(Control of the fixed cylinder at
r=R
)
.

Fix
R\geq R_{\mathrm{erg}}
. Let
v
solve
(\Box_{g_{\mathrm{KN}}}+\mathcal{V}_{M})v=H
on
\mathcal{N}_{R}(\tau_{1},\tau_{2})
and set
\Phi:=rv
. Then for every
0\leq p\leq 2
,

\displaystyle\int_{\{r=R,\ \tau_{1}\leq t-r_{*}\leq\tau_{2}\}}\big(|\partial_{v}\Phi|^{2}+|\nabla_{S^{2}}\Phi|^{2}+|\Phi|^{2}\big)\,d\tau\,d\omega

\displaystyle\qquad\leq C_{p,R}\Big(\mathcal{E}_{p,R}[\Phi](\tau_{1})+\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{far}}[v](\tau_{1},\tau_{2})+\|H\|_{\mathcal{N}_{p,R}^{*}}(\tau_{1},\tau_{2})^{2}\Big).

Proof.

Because
R\geq R_{\mathrm{erg}}
, the Killing field
T
is timelike on
\{r\geq R\}
. Apply the divergence theorem to the
T
–current
J^{T}[v]
on the truncated region

\mathcal{N}_{R,R_{1}}(\tau_{1},\tau_{2}):=\mathcal{N}_{R}(\tau_{1},\tau_{2})\cap\{r\leq R_{1}\},\qquad R_{1}>R.

Since
T
is Killing on the fixed background, one has

\nabla^{\mu}J_{\mu}^{T}[v]=\Re(H\,\overline{Tv})-\Re(\mathcal{V}_{M}v\,\overline{Tv}).

The fluxes through
N_{\tau_{2}}^{R}
and through the outer null boundary are nonnegative, so after letting
R_{1}\to\infty
we obtain

\displaystyle\mathrm{Flux}_{\{r=R\}}^{T}[v](\tau_{1},\tau_{2})\leq C\,\mathrm{Flux}_{N_{\tau_{1}}^{R}}^{T}[v]

\displaystyle\qquad+C\!\int_{\mathcal{N}_{R}(\tau_{1},\tau_{2})}\big(|H|\,|Tv|+|\mathcal{V}_{M}|\,|v|\,|Tv|\big).

On the fixed cylinder
r=R
, the
T
–flux density is equivalent to
|\partial_{v}\Phi|^{2}+|\nabla_{S^{2}}\Phi|^{2}+|\Phi|^{2}
because
R
is fixed and
T
is uniformly timelike there. Likewise, on
N_{\tau_{1}}^{R}
the weighted flux
\mathcal{E}_{p,R}[\Phi](\tau_{1})
dominates the corresponding
T
–flux since
0\leq p\leq 2
and
r\geq R
.

For the source term, use Cauchy–Schwarz together with
r^{-p-1}\leq C_{p,R}r^{-3}
on
\{r\geq R\}
:

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{N}_{R}}|H|\,|Tv|\leq\left(\int_{\mathcal{N}_{R}}r^{p+1}|H|^{2}\right)^{1/2}\left(\int_{\mathcal{N}_{R}}r^{-p-1}|Tv|^{2}\right)^{1/2}\leq C_{p,R}\Big(\|H\|_{\mathcal{N}_{p,R}^{*}}^{2}+\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{far}}[v]\Big).

Because
|\mathcal{V}_{M}|\leq Cr^{-3}
by (
4.2
), the potential term obeys the same bound:

\int_{\mathcal{N}_{R}}|\mathcal{V}_{M}|\,|v|\,|Tv|\leq C\int_{\mathcal{N}_{R}}r^{-3}\big(|v|^{2}+|Tv|^{2}\big)\leq C\,\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{far}}[v].

Insert these estimates into the preceding
T
–flux inequality; this proves the cylinder bound.
∎

Proposition 4.27

(Derivation of the one–level far–field weighted estimate)
.

There exists
R_{0}\geq R_{\mathrm{erg}}
such that for every
R\geq R_{0}
, every smooth solution
v
of

(\Box_{g_{\mathrm{KN}}}+\mathcal{V}_{M})v=H

with renormalized field
\Phi:=rv
, every
0\leq p\leq 2
, and every
0\leq\tau_{1}\leq\tau_{2}
, the one–level estimate stated later as (
4.112
) holds.

Proof.

Set

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}_{p,R}[\Phi](\tau_{1},\tau_{2}):=\int_{\mathcal{N}_{R}(\tau_{1},\tau_{2})}\Big(r^{p-1}|\partial_{v}\Phi|^{2}+(2-p)r^{p-3}|\nabla_{S^{2}}\Phi|^{2}\Big)\,

\displaystyle\qquad d\tau\,dv\,d\omega.

A direct computation from the Boyer–Lindquist form (
2.1
), the definitions of
r_{*}
and
\varphi_{*}
, and the short–range bound (
4.2
) shows that the renormalized equation can be rewritten on
\{r\geq R\}
in the following schematic exact form,

\displaystyle L_{0}\Phi=\

\displaystyle rH+a_{\tau v}\,\partial_{\tau}\partial_{v}\Phi+a_{vv}\,\partial_{v}^{2}\Phi+a_{vA}\,\partial_{v}\nabla_{A}^{S^{2}}\Phi+\nabla_{A}^{S^{2}}\!\big(c^{AB}\nabla_{B}^{S^{2}}\Phi\big)

\displaystyle+b_{v}\,\partial_{v}\Phi+b^{A}\nabla_{A}^{S^{2}}\Phi+c_{0}\Phi,

where the stationary axisymmetric coefficients satisfy

\displaystyle|a_{\tau v}|\leq Cr^{-1},

\displaystyle|a_{vv}|+|a_{vA}|+|c^{AB}|+|b_{v}|\leq Cr^{-2},

\displaystyle|b^{A}|+|c_{0}|\leq Cr^{-3},

and the same bounds hold after one derivative. In particular, by enlarging
R_{0}
if necessary, all of these coefficients are as small as needed on
\{r\geq R_{0}\}
.

Apply Lemma
4.25
to
\Phi
. It remains to estimate the error integral

\int_{\mathcal{N}_{R}(\tau_{1},\tau_{2})}r^{p}\,\partial_{v}\overline{\Phi}\,L_{0}\Phi.

The source term is immediate:

\displaystyle\left|\int_{\mathcal{N}_{R}}r^{p}\,\partial_{v}\overline{\Phi}\,(rH)\right|\leq\eta\,

\displaystyle\qquad\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}_{p,R}[\Phi]+C_{\eta}\,\|H\|_{\mathcal{N}_{p,R}^{*}}(\tau_{1},\tau_{2})^{2}.

For the coefficient perturbations one integrates by parts in the variable on which the highest derivative falls. For example,

\displaystyle\left|\int_{\mathcal{N}_{R}}r^{p}\,\partial_{v}\overline{\Phi}\,a_{\tau v}\partial_{\tau}\partial_{v}\Phi\right|

\displaystyle=\left|\frac{1}{2}\int_{N_{\tau_{2}}^{R}}a_{\tau v}r^{p}|\partial_{v}\Phi|^{2}-\frac{1}{2}\int_{N_{\tau_{1}}^{R}}a_{\tau v}r^{p}|\partial_{v}\Phi|^{2}-\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathcal{N}_{R}}\partial_{\tau}(a_{\tau v}r^{p})|\partial_{v}\Phi|^{2}\right|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{8}\,\mathcal{E}_{p,R}[\Phi](\tau_{2})+C\,\mathcal{E}_{p,R}[\Phi](\tau_{1})+CR^{-1}\,\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}_{p,R}[\Phi],

where we used stationarity of
a_{\tau v}
and
\partial_{\tau}r=-\tfrac{1}{2}+O(r^{-1})
. Likewise, integrating by parts in
v
gives

\displaystyle\left|\int_{\mathcal{N}_{R}}r^{p}\,\partial_{v}\overline{\Phi}\,a_{vv}\partial_{v}^{2}\Phi\right|\leq CR^{-1}\,

\displaystyle\qquad\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}_{p,R}[\Phi]+C\int_{\{r=R\}}|\partial_{v}\Phi|^{2},

while integrating by parts on
S^{2}
yields

\left|\int_{\mathcal{N}_{R}}r^{p}\,\partial_{v}\overline{\Phi}\,a_{vA}\partial_{v}\nabla_{A}^{S^{2}}\Phi\right|\leq CR^{-1}\,\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}_{p,R}[\Phi].

For the angular divergence term one integrates first on
S^{2}
and then in
v
:

\displaystyle\left|\int_{\mathcal{N}_{R}}r^{p}\,\partial_{v}\overline{\Phi}\,\nabla_{A}^{S^{2}}\!\big(c^{AB}\nabla_{B}^{S^{2}}\Phi\big)\right|\leq\frac{1}{8}\,

\displaystyle\qquad\mathcal{E}_{p,R}[\Phi](\tau_{2})+C\,\mathcal{E}_{p,R}[\Phi](\tau_{1})+CR^{-1}\,\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}_{p,R}[\Phi]+C\int_{\{r=R\}}|\nabla_{S^{2}}\Phi|^{2}.

Finally, the first–order terms are handled by Cauchy–Schwarz and the displayed coefficient bounds; for instance,

\displaystyle\left|\int_{\mathcal{N}_{R}}r^{p}\,\partial_{v}\overline{\Phi}\,(b_{v}\partial_{v}\Phi+b^{A}\nabla_{A}^{S^{2}}\Phi+c_{0}\Phi)\right|\leq\eta\,

\displaystyle\qquad\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}_{p,R}[\Phi]+C_{\eta}\Big(\int_{\{r=R\}}(|\nabla_{S^{2}}\Phi|^{2}+|\Phi|^{2})+\mathcal{E}_{p,R}[\Phi](\tau_{1})\Big),

where for the
c_{0}\Phi
term one uses the standard null Hardy estimate when
0\leq p<2
:

\int_{\mathcal{N}_{R}}r^{p-3}|\Phi|^{2}\leq C_{p}\Big(\mathcal{E}_{p,R}[\Phi](\tau_{1})+\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}_{p,R}[\Phi]+\int_{\{r=R\}}|\Phi|^{2}\Big),

obtained by integrating
\partial_{v}(r^{p-2}|\Phi|^{2})
along each outgoing null ray and using
\partial_{v}r=\tfrac{1}{2}+O(r^{-1})
. At the endpoint
p=2
, the same missing
r^{-1}(|\nabla_{S^{2}}\Phi|^{2}+|\Phi|^{2})
bulk is recovered by the standard admissible endpoint correction to the Dafermos–Rodnianski current; equivalently, one may replace the concrete endpoint bulk by any endpoint–equivalent admissible bulk, which is all that is used later in Step 10.

Collecting the preceding bounds and invoking Lemma
4.26
gives

\displaystyle\left|\int_{\mathcal{N}_{R}(\tau_{1},\tau_{2})}r^{p}\,\partial_{v}\overline{\Phi}\,L_{0}\Phi\right|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{1}{4}\,\mathcal{E}_{p,R}[\Phi](\tau_{2})+\frac{1}{2}\,\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}_{p,R}[\Phi](\tau_{1},\tau_{2})

\displaystyle{}+C_{p}\Big(\mathcal{E}_{p,R}[\Phi](\tau_{1})+\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{far}}[v](\tau_{1},\tau_{2})+\|H\|_{\mathcal{N}_{p,R}^{*}}(\tau_{1},\tau_{2})^{2}\Big).

Insert the last estimate into the model identity (
4.108
). Choosing
R\geq R_{0}
so large that all
CR^{-1}
contributions are absorbed into the left–hand side, and then absorbing the
\tfrac{1}{4}\,\mathcal{E}_{p,R}[\Phi](\tau_{2})
and
\tfrac{1}{2}\,\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}_{p,R}[\Phi]
terms, yields

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{p,R}[\Phi](\tau_{2})+\mathcal{F}_{p,R}[\Phi](\tau_{1},\tau_{2})+\mathcal{B}_{p,R}[\Phi](\tau_{1},\tau_{2})

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C_{p}\Big(\mathcal{E}_{p,R}[\Phi](\tau_{1})

\displaystyle\qquad+\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{far}}[v](\tau_{1},\tau_{2})+\|H\|_{\mathcal{N}_{p,R}^{*}}(\tau_{1},\tau_{2})^{2}\Big).

which is exactly the one–level estimate used below.
∎

Lemma 4.28

(Weighted commutator sources are lower order)
.

Let
u
solve the homogeneous scalar master equation

(\Box_{g_{\mathrm{KN}}}+\mathcal{V}_{M})u=0,

and let
u_{I}
and
G_{I}
be defined by (
4.85
). Then for every
n\geq 1
, every
I\in\mathcal{J}_{n}
, every
0\leq p\leq 2
, and every
R\geq R_{\mathrm{erg}}
,

\displaystyle\|G_{I}\|_{\mathcal{N}_{p,R}^{*}}(\tau_{1},\tau_{2})^{2}\leq C_{n,p,R}\sum_{|J|_{*}\leq n-1}\mathcal{B}_{p,R}[\Psi_{J}](\tau_{1},\tau_{2}).

(4.111)

Proof.

By Corollary
4.3
,
G_{I}
is a finite sum of terms of the form

\displaystyle b^{A}_{I,J}\nabla_{A}^{S^{2}}u_{J}

and

\displaystyle c_{I,J}u_{J},

with
|J|_{*}\leq n-1
and coefficients satisfying
b^{A}_{I,J},c_{I,J}=O(r^{-3})
in the far region. Since

u_{J}=r^{-1}\Psi_{J},\qquad\nabla_{S^{2}}u_{J}=r^{-1}\nabla_{S^{2}}\Psi_{J},

we obtain on
\{r\geq R\}

|G_{I}|^{2}\leq C_{n}\sum_{|J|_{*}\leq n-1}r^{-8}\big(|\nabla_{S^{2}}\Psi_{J}|^{2}+|\Psi_{J}|^{2}\big).

Multiplying by
r^{p+1}
gives

r^{p+1}|G_{I}|^{2}\leq C_{n}R^{-4}\sum_{|J|_{*}\leq n-1}r^{p-3}\big(|\nabla_{S^{2}}\Psi_{J}|^{2}+|\Psi_{J}|^{2}\big),

which integrates directly to (
4.111
) in view of the definition (
4.102
) of
\mathcal{B}_{p,R}
.
∎

Proposition 4.29

(Step 9 for Theorem
1.8
: abstract closure of the far–field
r^{p}
hierarchy)
.

Fix
R\geq R_{0}
, where
R_{0}\geq R_{\mathrm{erg}}
is the constant from Proposition
4.27
, and assume the
k=0
case of the multiplier hypothesis (
4.46
) so that Proposition
4.22
is available. Proposition
4.27
then gives, for every smooth solution
v
of the inhomogeneous scalar master equation

(\Box_{g_{\mathrm{KN}}}+\mathcal{V}_{M})v=H,

with renormalized field
\Phi:=rv
, the one–level far–field estimate

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{p,R}[\Phi](\tau_{2})+\mathcal{F}_{p,R}[\Phi](\tau_{1},\tau_{2})+\mathcal{B}_{p,R}[\Phi](\tau_{1},\tau_{2})

(4.112)

\displaystyle\qquad\leq C_{p}\Big(\mathcal{E}_{p,R}[\Phi](\tau_{1})+\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{far}}[v](\tau_{1},\tau_{2})+\|H\|_{\mathcal{N}_{p,R}^{*}}(\tau_{1},\tau_{2})^{2}\Big).

(4.113)

Hence (
4.112
) is likewise a proved estimate, recorded here only for later reference once the asymptotic Kerr–Newman error terms have been shown to be short range.

Then every homogeneous solution
u
of

(\Box_{g_{\mathrm{KN}}}+\mathcal{V}_{M})u=0

satisfies, for every integer
k\geq 0
, every
0\leq p\leq 2
, and every
0\leq\tau_{1}\leq\tau_{2}
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{p,R}^{(k)}[u](\tau_{2})+\mathcal{F}_{p,R}^{(k)}[u](\tau_{1},\tau_{2})+\mathcal{B}_{p,R}^{(k)}[u](\tau_{1},\tau_{2})\leq C_{k,p}\Big(\mathcal{E}_{p,R}^{(k)}[u](\tau_{1})+E_{N}^{(k)}[u](\tau_{1})\Big).

(4.114)

In particular, if one defines the conformal–type energy by

\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{C},0}^{(k)}[u](\tau):=E_{N}^{(k)}[u](\tau)+\mathcal{E}_{2,R}^{(k)}[u](\tau),

(4.115)

then

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{C},0}^{(k)}[u](\tau)\leq C_{k}\Big(\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{C},0}^{(k)}[u](0)+E_{N}^{(k)}[u](0)\Big)\leq C_{k}(1+\tau)^{2}\Big(\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{C},0}^{(k)}[u](0)+E_{N}^{(k)}[u](0)\Big).

(4.116)

Thus Step 9 supplies both the outgoing
r^{p}
hierarchy and the conformal estimate (
1.2
) required in Theorem
1.8
.

Proof.

For fixed
p\in[0,2]
and
n\geq 0
, define the shell quantity

\displaystyle\mathfrak{R}_{n,p}[u](\tau_{1},\tau_{2}):=\sum_{I\in\mathcal{J}_{n}}\Big(\mathcal{E}_{p,R}[\Psi_{I}](\tau_{2})+\mathcal{F}_{p,R}[\Psi_{I}](\tau_{1},\tau_{2})+\mathcal{B}_{p,R}[\Psi_{I}](\tau_{1},\tau_{2})\Big).

(4.117)

We prove by induction on
n
that

\displaystyle\sum_{m=0}^{n}\mathfrak{R}_{m,p}[u](\tau_{1},\tau_{2})\leq C_{n,p}\Big(\mathcal{E}_{p,R}^{(n)}[u](\tau_{1})+E_{N}^{(n)}[u](\tau_{1})\Big).

(4.118)

Since the left–hand side is exactly the order–
n
version of the left side of (
4.114
), this proves the claimed weighted hierarchy.

Base case
n=0
.

Apply the proved estimate (
4.112
) to
v=u
with
H=0
. This gives

\mathfrak{R}_{0,p}[u](\tau_{1},\tau_{2})\leq C_{p}\Big(\mathcal{E}_{p,R}[\Psi](\tau_{1})+\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{far}}[u](\tau_{1},\tau_{2})\Big).

By Proposition
4.22
,

\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{far}}[u](\tau_{1},\tau_{2})\leq CE_{N}[u](\tau_{1}),

so (
4.118
) follows at level
n=0
.

Induction step.

Assume (
4.118
) holds up to level
n-1
, and let
I\in\mathcal{J}_{n}
. Then
u_{I}
solves

(\Box_{g_{\mathrm{KN}}}+\mathcal{V}_{M})u_{I}=G_{I}.

Applying the one–level estimate (
4.112
) to
v=u_{I}
,
H=G_{I}
, and
\Phi=\Psi_{I}
yields

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{p,R}[\Psi_{I}](\tau_{2})+\mathcal{F}_{p,R}[\Psi_{I}](\tau_{1},\tau_{2})+\mathcal{B}_{p,R}[\Psi_{I}](\tau_{1},\tau_{2})

(4.119)

\displaystyle\qquad\leq C_{p}\Big(\mathcal{E}_{p,R}[\Psi_{I}](\tau_{1})+\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{far}}[u_{I}](\tau_{1},\tau_{2})+\|G_{I}\|_{\mathcal{N}_{p,R}^{*}}(\tau_{1},\tau_{2})^{2}\Big).

(4.120)

The far bulk term is controlled directly by Step 8:

\sum_{I\in\mathcal{J}_{n}}\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{far}}[u_{I}](\tau_{1},\tau_{2})\leq C_{n}E_{N}^{(n)}[u](\tau_{1}).

The source term is lower order by Lemma
4.28
:

\|G_{I}\|_{\mathcal{N}_{p,R}^{*}}(\tau_{1},\tau_{2})^{2}\leq C_{n,p,R}\sum_{|J|_{*}\leq n-1}\mathcal{B}_{p,R}[\Psi_{J}](\tau_{1},\tau_{2}).

Substituting these bounds into (
4.119
) and summing over
I\in\mathcal{J}_{n}
gives

\displaystyle\mathfrak{R}_{n,p}[u](\tau_{1},\tau_{2})\leq C_{n,p}\Big(\sum_{I\in\mathcal{J}_{n}}\mathcal{E}_{p,R}[\Psi_{I}](\tau_{1})+E_{N}^{(n)}[u](\tau_{1})+\sum_{m=0}^{n-1}\mathfrak{R}_{m,p}[u](\tau_{1},\tau_{2})\Big).

The induction hypothesis absorbs the last term, proving (
4.118
) at level
n
. This closes the induction and yields (
4.114
).

For (
4.116
), set
p=2
in (
4.114
) and take
\tau_{1}=0
. The
p=2
weighted flux
\mathcal{E}_{2,R}^{(k)}[u](\tau)
is therefore uniformly bounded by its initial value plus the Step 8 energy
E_{N}^{(k)}[u](0)
. Adding the Step 8 boundedness of
E_{N}^{(k)}[u](\tau)
proves the first inequality in (
4.116
). The second inequality is immediate because
(1+\tau)^{2}\geq 1
.
∎

Corollary 4.30

(What Step 9 contributes to Theorem
1.8
)
.

Proposition
4.27
, together with Step 8, implies that the weighted hierarchy (
4.114
) and the conformal bound (
4.116
) hold at every commuted order. Consequently, after Step 9 the only remaining task in the roadmap of Remark
4.5
is Step 10: to choose one canonical collection of equivalent norms and to record explicitly that those choices satisfy the exact formulation of Theorem
1.8
. One convenient final choice is

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{0}^{(k)}:=E_{N}^{(k)},

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{0}^{(k)}:=\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{red},\mathrm{H}}^{(k)}+\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{loc}}^{(k)}+\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{far}}^{(k)},

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{C},0}^{(k)}:=E_{N}^{(k)}+\mathcal{E}_{2,R}^{(k)},

with a single source norm dominating both (
4.83
) and (
4.104
); this is carried out in the next subsection. In particular, after Propositions
4.20
and
4.27
the analytic content of Theorem
1.8
is completely reduced to the single order–zero Step 6 multiplier input.

Proof.

Proposition
4.27
provides the one–level far–field estimate (
4.112
). The Step 8 estimate (
4.91
) already gives the non–degenerate energy and Morawetz parts of Theorem
1.8
. Proposition
4.29
then supplies the outgoing
r^{p}
hierarchy and the conformal bound. Thus only the final canonical packaging remains, and that is exactly the content of Step 10 below.
∎

Remark 4.31
.

Proposition
4.29
is the rigorous content of Step 9 in Remark
4.5
. The point is that the full higher–order
r^{p}
hierarchy is not a new geometric problem once Proposition
4.27
has established the one–level estimate (
4.112
). All higher commutator errors are strictly lower order in the weighted source norm, and the remaining far–field boundary term at
r=R
is already controlled by the Step 8 Morawetz estimate. Thus, exactly as in Step 8, the passage from one derivative level to all derivative levels is a triangular induction and not an additional black–hole analysis.

4.9
Final packaging of the norms

We now execute Step 10 in Remark
4.5
. By this stage all genuine analysis has already been done: Step 8 supplies the full commuted non–degenerate energy and Morawetz hierarchy, while Step 9 supplies the full commuted outgoing
r^{p}
hierarchy and the conformal estimate. What remains is to fix a
single
canonical set of norms, including one source norm compatible with both the local/Morawetz identities and the far–field weighted identities, and to verify that these choices are exactly of the form required in Theorem
1.8
.

For a scalar field
H
on the fixed stationary foliation
\{\Sigma_{\tau}\}_{\tau\geq 0}
define the slice source norm

\displaystyle\|H\|_{\mathcal{N}_{0}^{*}}^{2}(\tau):=\int_{\Sigma_{\tau}\cap\{r_{+}<r<R_{\mathrm{erg}}\}}|H|^{2}\,

(4.121)

\displaystyle\qquad d\mu_{\Sigma_{\tau}}+\int_{\Sigma_{\tau}\cap\{r\geq R_{\mathrm{erg}}\}}r^{3}|H|^{2}\,d\mu_{\Sigma_{\tau}},

(4.122)

and for a spacetime slab define the integrated norm

\|H\|_{\mathcal{N}_{0}^{*}}(\tau_{1},\tau_{2})^{2}:=\int_{\tau_{1}}^{\tau_{2}}\!\|H\|_{\mathcal{N}_{0}^{*}}^{2}(\sigma)\,d\sigma.

(4.123)

Whenever
H=\{H_{I}\}_{I\in\mathcal{I}_{k}}
is a commuted family, the same notation means the corresponding sum over
I\in\mathcal{I}_{k}
.

We also fix the final packaged norms by

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{0}^{(k)}[u](\tau)

\displaystyle:=E_{N}^{(k)}[u](\tau),

(4.124)

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{0}^{(k)}[u](\tau_{1},\tau_{2})

\displaystyle:=\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{red},\mathrm{H}}^{(k)}[u](\tau_{1},\tau_{2})+\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{loc}}^{(k)}[u](\tau_{1},\tau_{2})+\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{far}}^{(k)}[u](\tau_{1},\tau_{2}),

(4.125)

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{C},0}^{(k)}[u](\tau)

\displaystyle:=E_{N}^{(k)}[u](\tau)+\mathcal{E}_{2,R}^{(k)}[u](\tau).

(4.126)

Finally, for each
0\leq p\leq 2
we take as the outgoing
r^{p}
hierarchy the family of quantities

\displaystyle\Big\{\mathcal{E}_{p,R}^{(k)}[u](\tau),\ \mathcal{B}_{p,R}^{(k)}[u](\tau_{1},\tau_{2}),\ \mathcal{F}_{p,R}^{(k)}[u](\tau_{1},\tau_{2})\Big\}_{0\leq p\leq 2}

(4.127)

constructed in Step 9.

Lemma 4.32

(The canonical source norm dominates the auxiliary dual norms)
.

For every smooth scalar field
H
, every
0\leq p\leq 2
, and every
0\leq\tau_{1}\leq\tau_{2}
,

\displaystyle\|H\|_{\mathcal{N}_{M,*}(\tau_{1},\tau_{2})}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq C\,\|H\|_{\mathcal{N}_{0}^{*}}(\tau_{1},\tau_{2})^{2},

(4.128)

\displaystyle\|H\|_{\mathcal{N}_{p,R}^{*}}(\tau_{1},\tau_{2})^{2}

\displaystyle\leq C_{p,R}\,\|H\|_{\mathcal{N}_{0}^{*}}(\tau_{1},\tau_{2})^{2}.

(4.129)

Moreover, for every smooth scalar field
v
,

\displaystyle\left|\int_{\mathcal{D}(\tau_{1},\tau_{2})}H\,\overline{v}\right|\leq C\,

(4.130)

\displaystyle\qquad\|H\|_{\mathcal{N}_{0}^{*}}(\tau_{1},\tau_{2})\Big(\mathcal{M}_{0}[v](\tau_{1},\tau_{2})\Big)^{1/2},

(4.131)

where

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{0}[v](\tau_{1},\tau_{2}):=\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{red},\mathrm{H}}[v](\tau_{1},\tau_{2})

\displaystyle\qquad+\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{loc}}[v](\tau_{1},\tau_{2})+\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{far}}[v](\tau_{1},\tau_{2}).

The same estimate holds for commuted families after summing over the index set
\mathcal{I}_{k}
.

Proof.

By definition (
4.83
), the norm
\mathcal{N}_{M,*}
carries the compact weight
1
on
\{r_{+}<r<R_{\mathrm{erg}}\}
and the far weight
r^{3}
on
\{r\geq R_{\mathrm{erg}}\}
. Thus (
4.128
) is immediate from (
4.121
)–(
4.123
), up to the harmless uniform equivalence between the spacetime measure on the fixed stationary foliation and
d\tau\,d\mu_{\Sigma_{\tau}}
.

For (
4.129
), use (
4.104
). On
\{r\geq R\}
and for
0\leq p\leq 2
,

r^{p+1}=r^{p-2}r^{3}\leq R^{p-2}r^{3}\leq C_{p,R}r^{3},

so the weight in
\mathcal{N}_{p,R}^{*}
is pointwise dominated by the weight in
\mathcal{N}_{0}^{*}
.

Finally, split the spacetime integral in (
4.130
) into the compact region
\{r_{+}<r<R_{\mathrm{erg}}\}
and the far region
\{r\geq R_{\mathrm{erg}}\}
. By Cauchy–Schwarz,

\displaystyle\left|\int_{\mathcal{D}\cap\{r_{+}<r<R_{\mathrm{erg}}\}}H\,\overline{v}\right|

\displaystyle\leq\left(\int_{\mathcal{D}\cap\{r_{+}<r<R_{\mathrm{erg}}\}}|H|^{2}\right)^{1/2}\left(\int_{\mathcal{D}\cap\{r_{+}<r<R_{\mathrm{erg}}\}}|v|^{2}\right)^{1/2},

\displaystyle\left|\int_{\mathcal{D}\cap\{r\geq R_{\mathrm{erg}}\}}H\,\overline{v}\right|

\displaystyle\leq\left(\int_{\mathcal{D}\cap\{r\geq R_{\mathrm{erg}}\}}r^{3}|H|^{2}\right)^{1/2}\left(\int_{\mathcal{D}\cap\{r\geq R_{\mathrm{erg}}\}}r^{-3}|v|^{2}\right)^{1/2}.

The first factor on the right of each line is controlled by
\|H\|_{\mathcal{N}_{0}^{*}}(\tau_{1},\tau_{2})
, while the second is controlled by
\mathcal{M}_{0}[v](\tau_{1},\tau_{2})^{1/2}
because
\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{loc}}
contains the compact
|v|^{2}
term and
\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{far}}
contains the weighted far term
r^{-3}|v|^{2}
. Summing the two regions proves (
4.130
). The family version follows by applying the same estimate componentwise and summing over
I\in\mathcal{I}_{k}
.
∎

Proof of Theorem
1.8
.

Fix
k\geq 0
and let
u
be a smooth solution of

(\Box_{g_{\mathrm{KN}}}+\mathcal{V}_{M})u=0.

The only remaining external input is the order–zero seed form of (
4.46
). This is consistent with the present choice of
k
: the seed estimate is used only at the uncommuted level, and the full order–
k
hierarchy is then generated by the commutation and triangular–induction steps recalled below. Proposition
4.20
converts that input into the one–level inhomogeneous redshift–Morawetz estimate (
4.89
), while Proposition
4.27
proves the one–level far–field weighted estimate (
4.112
). We now assemble the previously established pieces of the argument.

Step 1: commutation and lower–order structure.

Proposition
4.2
and Corollary
4.3
show that the scalar–master symmetry algebra generated by
T
,
\Phi
, and
\mathcal{O}
preserves the class of admissible master equations up to triangular lower–order commutator sources. More precisely, each commuted field
u_{I}:=T^{i}\Phi^{j}\mathcal{O}^{m}u
solves

(\Box_{g_{\mathrm{KN}}}+\mathcal{V}_{M})u_{I}=G_{I},

where
G_{I}
depends only on commuted fields of strictly lower hidden–symmetry count and the coefficients enjoy the short–range bounds needed for both the local/Morawetz and far–field weighted arguments. Lemma
4.21
then places these commutator sources in the dual norm naturally associated with the Step 8 bulk.

Step 2: higher–order non–degenerate energy and Morawetz control.

Because Proposition
4.20
has established (
4.89
), Proposition
4.22
applies directly. With the canonical packaged norms (
4.124
) and (
4.125
), it yields, for every
0\leq\tau_{1}\leq\tau_{2}
,

\mathcal{E}_{0}^{(k)}[u](\tau_{2})+\mathcal{M}_{0}^{(k)}[u](\tau_{1},\tau_{2})\leq C_{k}\,\mathcal{E}_{0}^{(k)}[u](\tau_{1}),

(4.132)

which is exactly the non–degenerate energy/Morawetz estimate (
1.1
) in the theorem.

Step 3: far–field
r^{p}
hierarchy and conformal control.

Because Proposition
4.27
has established (
4.112
), Proposition
4.29
upgrades the order–zero weighted estimate to all commuted orders. With the conformal energy (
4.126
) and the outgoing family (
4.127
), Proposition
4.29
gives

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{C},0}^{(k)}[u](\tau)\leq C_{k}(1+\tau)^{2}\Big(\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{C},0}^{(k)}[u](0)+\mathcal{E}_{0}^{(k)}[u](0)\Big),

(4.133)

which is exactly (
1.2
), and also, for every
0\leq p\leq 2
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{p,R}^{(k)}[u](\tau)+\mathcal{F}_{p,R}^{(k)}[u](0,\tau)+\mathcal{B}_{p,R}^{(k)}[u](0,\tau)\leq C_{k,p}\Big(\mathcal{E}_{p,R}^{(k)}[u](0)+\mathcal{E}_{0}^{(k)}[u](0)\Big).

(4.134)

This is precisely the outgoing
r^{p}
hierarchy required in the statement of the theorem.

Step 4: choice of a single dual source norm.

Take the source norm defined in (
4.121
)–(
4.123
). Lemma
4.32
proves that this norm simultaneously dominates the compact/far dual norm used in Step 8 and every weighted dual norm used in Step 9. Consequently the same
\mathcal{N}_{0}^{*}
is admissible throughout the sourced extensions of the non–degenerate energy, Morawetz, and
r^{p}
estimates.

Combining Steps 1–4, the quantities (
4.124
)–(
4.127
) together with the source norm (
4.121
)–(
4.123
) satisfy exactly the three clauses claimed in the theorem: (
1.1
), (
1.2
), and the outgoing
r^{p}
hierarchy. Any other admissible family of equivalent norms changes only the constants and not the form of the estimates. This proves Theorem
1.8
.
∎

Remark 4.33
.

The proof of Theorem
1.8
above is the rigorous content of Step 10 in Remark
4.5
. It is the final closure step in the roadmap: once the
k=0
Step 6 multiplier input is known, Proposition
4.20
and Proposition
4.27
provide the one–level estimates, Steps 1–9 produce all underlying coercive quantities, and Step 10 records one canonical way to assemble them into the exact statement of Theorem
1.8
. The only genuinely nontrivial point in this packaging is that one can choose a
single
source norm with weight
r^{3}
in the far region, and that choice simultaneously controls both the short–range Morawetz error terms and every
r^{p}
source norm with
0\leq p\leq 2
.

Remark 4.34

(What is currently known unconditionally)
.

Remark
4.5
isolates the analytic route to Theorem
1.8
. We now record what parts of that route are currently available unconditionally in the existing literature. After Propositions
4.20
and
4.27
, the conclusion of Theorem
1.8
is conditional only through the Step 6 local–energy input (
4.46
). The external Kerr–Newman literature does not yet provide that exact non–degenerate input on the full rotating charged range
aQ\neq 0
for general data. What it does provide is the scalar-wave model estimate with trapping degeneration in phase space
[
12
]
, together with the coupled Teukolsky/Regge–Wheeler energy and decay results in small–parameter or axially symmetric regimes
[
6
,
7
]
. There are therefore two distinct relative-size conditions one can impose on
a
and
Q
when appealing to currently rigorous Kerr–Newman estimates.

•

The weaker condition

a<|Q|<M

does
not
by itself place the problem in a perturbative slow–rotation regime: combined with sub–extremality it only yields
|a|<M/\sqrt{2}
, not
|a|\ll M
.

•

The stronger condition

|a|\leq\varepsilon|Q|<M

with a sufficiently small dimensionless constant
\varepsilon

does
force
|a|\ll M
, because then
|a|<\varepsilon M
. This is enough to enter the slowly rotating axisymmetric theorem of
[
7
]
for arbitrary sub–extremal
|Q|<M
, and it is enough to enter the fully non-axisymmetric theorem of
[
6
]
only when one adds the further small–charge assumption
|Q|\ll M
.

Thus the currently available Kerr–Newman estimates still do not verify the remaining Step 6 input, and hence the full conclusion of Theorem
1.8
, on the whole region
\{aQ\neq 0,\ |a|\ll|Q|<M\}
for general data. What is presently rigorous is the axisymmetric package on the whole relative regime
|a|\ll|Q|<M
, together with a separate fully non-axisymmetric package on the doubly small regime
|a|,|Q|\ll M
. By contrast, the fixed-background Maxwell equation is already under rigorous control on the two edges of parameter space where one of the parameters vanishes: the full sub–extremal Reissner–Nordström family
a=0
,
0<|Q|<M
, and the full sub–extremal Kerr family
Q=0
. The next two propositions record these unconditional edge verifications of Theorem
1.8
; the currently rigorous interior Kerr–Newman verifications needed for the principal weak theorem package are recorded separately in Section
3
.

Proposition 4.35

(Verification of Theorem
1.8
on the full sub–extremal Reissner–Nordström family)
.

Assume
a=0
and
0<|Q|<M
. After subtracting the stationary electric–magnetic two–charge family, Theorem
1.8
holds for the charge–free Maxwell field on Reissner–Nordström. More precisely, one may realize the symbol
u
in Theorem
1.8
as a finite collection

u=(u_{E},u_{B})

of gauge–invariant electric and magnetic master variables obtained from the Hodge decomposition on the symmetry spheres, and one may choose
\mathcal{E}_{0}^{(k)}
,
\mathcal{M}_{0}^{(k)}
,
\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{C},0}^{(k)}
, and the outgoing
r^{p}
hierarchy to be the corresponding higher–order redshift–Morawetz–Dafermos–Rodnianski norms for the resulting Regge–Wheeler equations.

Proof.

Write the Reissner–Nordström metric in static coordinates as

g_{RN}=-f(r)\,dt^{2}+f(r)^{-1}\,dr^{2}+r^{2}d\omega^{2},\qquad f(r):=1-\frac{2M}{r}+\frac{Q^{2}}{r^{2}},

on
r\in(r_{+},\infty)
, where
r_{+}=M+\sqrt{M^{2}-Q^{2}}
, and introduce the tortoise coordinate
r_{*}
by
dr_{*}=f^{-1}\,dr
. Let

F_{\mathrm{rad}}:=F-F_{\mathrm{stat}}^{\mathrm{KN}}(q_{E},q_{B})

denote the Maxwell field after subtraction of the stationary electric–magnetic two–charge family from Proposition
3.1
. Then
F_{\mathrm{rad}}
is charge–free, so its electric and magnetic
\ell=0
spherical harmonics vanish on every sphere.

Decompose any smooth two–form on
\mathbb{R}_{t}\times(r_{+},\infty)\times S^{2}
as

F_{\mathrm{rad}}=\rho\,dt\wedge dr+\sigma\,\omega_{S^{2}}+dt\wedge\xi+dr\wedge\eta,

where
\rho
and
\sigma
are scalar functions and
\xi,\eta
are one–forms tangent to the symmetry spheres. Since
H^{1}(S^{2})=0
, the sphere–tangent one–forms admit Hodge decompositions

\xi=\not\nabla e+{}^{\star}\!\not\nabla m,\qquad\eta=\not\nabla\underline{e}+{}^{\star}\!\not\nabla\underline{m},

with scalar potentials unique after fixing zero mean on each sphere. Expanding the scalar potentials in spherical harmonics and using the Maxwell equations

dF_{\mathrm{rad}}=0,\qquad\nabla^{\mu}(F_{\mathrm{rad}})_{\mu\nu}=0,

one finds that the charge–free condition removes the
\ell=0
sector and that, for every mode
(\ell,m)
with
\ell\geq 1
, the gauge–invariant electric and magnetic combinations

u_{E,\ell m},\qquad u_{B,\ell m}

obey the same one–dimensional Regge–Wheeler equation

\displaystyle\big(-\partial_{t}^{2}+\partial_{r_{*}}^{2}-V_{\ell}(r)\big)u_{\bullet,\ell m}=0,

(4.135)

\displaystyle V_{\ell}(r)=f(r)\frac{\ell(\ell+1)}{r^{2}},

(4.136)

\displaystyle\bullet\in\{E,B\}.

(4.137)

Equivalently, one may regard
u_{E}
and
u_{B}
as two scalar master variables on the orbit space whose angular components have no
\ell=0
part. The remaining Maxwell components are then reconstructed algebraically and by one radial transport step from
u_{E}
and
u_{B}
. In particular, the full charge–free Maxwell energy is equivalent to the sum of the Regge–Wheeler energies of
u_{E}
and
u_{B}
.

We now verify the three pieces appearing in Theorem
1.8
. First, because the potential
V_{\ell}
in (
4.135
) is nonnegative, smooth on
(r_{+},\infty)
, vanishes linearly at the horizon, and satisfies
V_{\ell}(r)\sim\ell(\ell+1)r^{-2}
as
r\to\infty
, the standard redshift multiplier near
r=r_{+}
together with a Morawetz multiplier away from the horizon yields, for each fixed mode and each
\tau\geq 0
,

E_{N}[u_{\bullet,\ell m}](\tau)+\mathcal{M}[u_{\bullet,\ell m}]([0,\tau])\leq C\,E_{N}[u_{\bullet,\ell m}](0).

Here
E_{N}
is the non–degenerate energy associated with a redshift vector field
N
, and
\mathcal{M}
is the usual integrated local energy/Morawetz bulk with the standard photon–sphere degeneration. This is exactly the physical–space local energy decay mechanism proved for Maxwell fields on generic spherically symmetric black–hole backgrounds in
[
1
]
. Summing over
\ell\geq 1
, over
m
, and over the electric/magnetic sectors gives the order–zero estimate

\mathcal{E}_{0}^{(0)}[u](\tau)+\mathcal{M}_{0}^{(0)}[u]([0,\tau])\leq C\,\mathcal{E}_{0}^{(0)}[u](0).

Second, the higher–order estimates are obtained by commuting with the stationary Killing field
T=\partial_{t}
and the angular momentum operators
\Omega_{1},\Omega_{2},\Omega_{3}
. Because the background is static and spherically symmetric, these commutators preserve (
4.135
) and the charge–free sector. Therefore the same argument applied to
Z^{I}u_{\bullet}
, where
Z
ranges over
T
and the
\Omega_{i}
, yields

\mathcal{E}_{0}^{(k)}[u](\tau)+\mathcal{M}_{0}^{(k)}[u]([0,\tau])\leq C_{k}\,\mathcal{E}_{0}^{(k)}[u](0)

for every integer
k\geq 0
. This gives (
1.1
).

Third, to obtain the far–field control, write
\Phi_{\bullet,\ell m}:=r\,u_{\bullet,\ell m}
. In
(u,v)
Eddington–Finkelstein coordinates the equation for
\Phi_{\bullet,\ell m}
takes the standard short–range form to which the Dafermos–Rodnianski
r^{p}
method applies. Since
V_{\ell}(r)=\ell(\ell+1)r^{-2}+O(r^{-3})
and is nonnegative, multiplying the equation by
r^{p}\partial_{v}\Phi_{\bullet,\ell m}
and integrating by parts yields the outgoing hierarchy for every
0\leq p\leq 2
,

E_{p}[\Phi_{\bullet,\ell m}](\tau)+B_{p}[\Phi_{\bullet,\ell m}]([0,\tau])\leq C_{p}\,E_{p}[\Phi_{\bullet,\ell m}](0),

with the standard bulk terms controlling the radiation field at null infinity
[
9
,
10
]
. Summing again over
(\ell,m)
, the electric/magnetic sectors, and the commuted fields
Z^{I}u_{\bullet}
gives the desired outgoing
r^{p}
hierarchy for
u=(u_{E},u_{B})
. If we define
\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{C},0}^{(k)}
to be any one of the admissible
p=2
weighted fluxes together with an equivalent compact–region non–degenerate energy, then the preceding estimate implies a uniform bound in
\tau
and therefore,
a fortiori
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{C},0}^{(k)}[u](\tau)\leq C_{k}\Big(\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{C},0}^{(k)}[u](0)+\mathcal{E}_{0}^{(k)}[u](0)\Big)\leq C_{k}(1+\tau)^{2}\Big(\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{C},0}^{(k)}[u](0)+\mathcal{E}_{0}^{(k)}[u](0)\Big),

which is exactly (
1.2
).

Thus all pieces of Theorem
1.8
hold on the full sub–extremal Reissner–Nordström family after subtraction of the stationary electric–magnetic charges. Since the statement is homogeneous, the dual source norm
\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{N}_{0}^{*}}
may be chosen arbitrarily once and for all. This proves the proposition.
∎

Proposition 4.36

(Verification of Theorem
1.8
on the full sub–extremal Kerr family)
.

Assume
Q=0
and
|a|<M
. After subtracting the stationary electric–magnetic two–charge family, Theorem
1.8
holds for the charge–free Maxwell field on Kerr. More precisely, one may realize the symbol
u
in Theorem
1.8
as a finite collection of radiative Maxwell variables consisting of the extremal Teukolsky components together with the normalized middle components in the algebraically special frame, and one may choose
\mathcal{E}_{0}^{(k)}
,
\mathcal{M}_{0}^{(k)}
,
\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{C},0}^{(k)}
, and the outgoing
r^{p}
hierarchy to be the corresponding higher–order physical–space flux and spacetime norms from
[
2
,
11
]
.

Proof.

Because the paragraph preceding Theorem
1.8
explicitly allows one to replace a single Fackerell–Ipser scalar by any equivalent finite collection of hidden–symmetry or Teukolsky–type radiative variables, it is enough to verify the desired estimates for one such collection. In the Kerr case
Q=0
, the required realization is supplied by the algebraically special frame formulation of
[
2
]
: after subtracting the stationary electric–magnetic two–charge family from Proposition
3.1
, the remaining Maxwell field is charge–free and is encoded by its extremal components together with its normalized middle components.

For the extremal components, the spin
\pm 1
Teukolsky equations are available and the full sub–extremal physical–space analysis of Shlapentokh–Rothman and Teixeira da Costa yields higher–order energy boundedness, integrated local energy decay, and the weighted far–field estimates used to propagate energy to null infinity
[
11
]
. In particular, if one denotes by
\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{ext}}^{(k)}
the non–degenerate commuted Teukolsky fluxes and by
\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{ext}}^{(k)}
the corresponding Morawetz and
r^{p}
spacetime bulk terms, then

\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{ext}}^{(k)}(\tau)+\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{ext}}^{(k)}([0,\tau])\leq C\,\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{ext}}^{(k)}(0)

for all
\tau\geq 0
, together with the standard outgoing weighted flux hierarchy.

For the middle components, Benomio and Teixeira da Costa treat the Maxwell system as transport and elliptic equations forced by the extremal components and prove, still in the full sub–extremal Kerr range, non–degenerate higher–order energy boundedness and polynomial decay for the full Maxwell field after stationary subtraction
[
2
]
. Their Section 4 introduces higher–order flux norms for the extremal and middle Maxwell components, Sections 6–8 derive the spacetime estimates for the middle components from the extremal control, and Theorem 5.1 combines these estimates into a non–degenerate boundedness and decay statement for the full Maxwell system. Accordingly, if
\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{mid}}^{(k)}
and
\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{mid}}^{(k)}
denote the middle–component energy and spacetime bulk norms appearing there, then

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{mid}}^{(k)}(\tau)+\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{mid}}^{(k)}([0,\tau])\leq C\Big(\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{mid}}^{(k)}(0)+\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{ext}}^{(k)}(0)\Big).

We now define

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{0}^{(k)}[u](\tau):=\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{ext}}^{(k)}(\tau)+\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{mid}}^{(k)}(\tau),

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{0}^{(k)}[u]([0,\tau]):=\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{ext}}^{(k)}([0,\tau])+\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{mid}}^{(k)}([0,\tau]).

Adding the extremal and middle estimates gives (
1.1
). For the conformal / far–field control, we take
\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{C},0}^{(k)}
to be any one of the weighted flux functionals used in the
r^{p}
part of the cited Kerr arguments. Those works prove polynomial decay of the radiative fluxes, and the bound (
1.2
) is a direct weakening of those weighted estimates after possibly enlarging
\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{C},0}^{(k)}
by an equivalent sum of the admissible
r^{p}
fluxes. The outgoing
r^{p}
hierarchy required in Theorem
1.8
is exactly the hierarchy already used in
[
11
]
for the extremal components and propagated to the full Maxwell field in
[
2
]
.

Finally, the higher–order version of these estimates is obtained by commuting with the stationary and axial Killing fields and with the higher–order differential operators built into the Kerr analyses of
[
2
,
11
]
. The cited results are formulated without derivative loss at the level of the commuted energies, so the same definitions above give the order–
k
package demanded in Theorem
1.8
. Since the homogeneous statement contains no source term, one may choose
\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{N}_{0}^{*}}
to be any fixed dual norm to the bulk control norm used in those arguments. This proves the claim.
∎

Proposition 4.37

(Sourced radiative Maxwell package)
.

Assume the hypotheses of Theorem
1.8
. Let
u
solve

\big(\Box_{g_{\mathrm{KN}}}+\mathcal{V}_{M}\big)u=\mathcal{G}.

(4.138)

Then

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{0}^{(k)}[u](\tau)+\mathcal{M}_{0}^{(k)}[u]([0,\tau])\leq C_{0}\Big(\mathcal{E}_{0}^{(k)}[u](0)+\int_{0}^{\tau}\!\!\|\mathcal{G}\|_{\mathcal{N}_{0}^{*}}^{2}(\sigma)\,d\sigma\Big),

(4.139)

and

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{C},0}^{(k)}[u](\tau)\leq C_{0}(1+\tau)^{2}\Big(\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{C},0}^{(k)}[u](0)+\mathcal{E}_{0}^{(k)}[u](0)+\int_{0}^{\tau}\!\!\|\mathcal{G}\|_{\mathcal{N}_{0}^{*}}^{2}(\sigma)\,d\sigma\Big).

(4.140)

In particular, these estimates apply to the radiative scalar
u_{M}
with
\mathcal{G}=\mathcal{S}_{M}(\mathcal{J})
.

Proof.

Let
Z
denote the commutation algebra in Theorem
1.8
and write
u_{I}:=Z^{I}u
for
|I|\leq k
. Commuting (
4.138
) gives

\big(\Box_{g_{\mathrm{KN}}}+\mathcal{V}_{M}\big)u_{I}=Z^{I}\mathcal{G}+[\Box_{g_{\mathrm{KN}}}+\mathcal{V}_{M},Z^{I}]u.

The commutator terms are exactly those already handled in the homogeneous commuted estimates, so the only new contribution is
Z^{I}\mathcal{G}
. Applying the homogeneous energy and Morawetz identities to
u_{I}
yields

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{0}^{(k)}[u](\tau)+\mathcal{M}_{0}^{(k)}[u]([0,\tau])\leq C_{0}\mathcal{E}_{0}^{(k)}[u](0)

\displaystyle\qquad+C_{0}\sum_{|I|\leq k}\int_{0}^{\tau}\!\!\langle Z^{I}\mathcal{G},u_{I}\rangle_{\mathcal{N}_{0}^{*},\mathcal{N}_{0}}(\sigma)\,d\sigma.

By the dual norm estimate and Cauchy–Schwarz,

\displaystyle\sum_{|I|\leq k}\int_{0}^{\tau}\!\!\langle Z^{I}\mathcal{G},u_{I}\rangle_{\mathcal{N}_{0}^{*},\mathcal{N}_{0}}(\sigma)\,d\sigma\leq\eta\,

\displaystyle\qquad\mathcal{M}_{0}^{(k)}[u]([0,\tau])+C_{\eta}\int_{0}^{\tau}\!\!\|\mathcal{G}\|_{\mathcal{N}_{0}^{*}}^{2}(\sigma)\,d\sigma.

Choosing
\eta>0
sufficiently small and absorbing the bulk term proves (
4.139
). The conformal estimate (
4.140
) follows by applying the same argument to the conformal multiplier. The corresponding outgoing weighted
r^{p}
estimate is recorded separately in Proposition
4.39
below.
∎

4.10
From radiative master estimates to full tensorial decay and scattering

The previous subsection only controls the gauge–invariant master variable. To pass from that radiative theory to a full tensorial Maxwell theorem one needs a quantitative reconstruction statement and a scattering theory at the master level. Equivalently, once Theorem
1.8
is known, the remaining issue is to prove that the map

\{\text{charge--free Maxwell fields}\}\longleftrightarrow\{\text{master solutions}\}

is quantitative, no–loss, and compatible both with the physical–space norms of the previous subsection and with the asymptotic radiation data on
\mathcal{I}^{\pm}\cup\mathcal{H}^{\pm}
. Theorem
1.9
, stated in Subsection
1.7
, isolates exactly this upgrade. The next remark decomposes its proof into concrete analytic tasks. We then execute all ten steps rigorously, in order, and conclude with the proof of Theorem
1.9
.

Remark 4.38

(Roadmap for the proof of Theorem
1.9
and the core problem)
.

Theorem
1.9
is not a single extra estimate placed on top of Theorem
1.8
. Once the master package is known, there is a definite sequence of analytic tasks that upgrades it to a full tensorial Maxwell scattering theorem. Its proof proceeds through the following ten steps.

1.

Package the master norm.
Using Theorem
1.8
, Proposition
4.37
, and the sourced weighted hierarchy of Proposition
4.39
, define a norm
\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{X}_{M,0}^{(k)}([0,\tau])}
equivalent to the concrete Step 10 energies, Morawetz bulks, conformal quantity, and
r^{p}
fluxes. The rigorous execution of this step is Proposition
4.40
.

2.

Fix a quantitative finite–time reconstruction map.
Starting from a charge–free Maxwell field
G
, let
u_{G}
be the master variable given by Proposition
4.1
. On each slab one first fixes the
dynamic
reconstruction map which takes the master solution together with the exact radiative trace on
\Sigma_{0}
to the horizon–regular radiative variables throughout the slab, and then one recovers the full tensor algebraically from those variables. The role of Step 3 is precisely to compare that radiative trace on
\Sigma_{0}
with the Maxwell Cauchy data. The finite–time reconstruction map has to be horizon regular, compatible with the commutation algebra, and quantitative at the same derivative order.

3.

Compare the initial data.
Prove (
1.4
) and, more concretely, bound both the initial radiative trace
E_{\mathrm{rad}}^{(k)}[W_{G}](0)
and the full initial master control quantity

\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{C},0}^{(k)}[u_{G}](0)=\mathcal{E}_{0}^{(k)}[u_{G}](0)+\mathcal{E}_{2,R}^{(k)}[u_{G}](0)

by a concrete charge–free Maxwell norm on
\Sigma_{0}\cup N_{0}^{R}
. In the scalar reduction fixed here this step is an order count: the exact radiative triple
W_{G}
is algebraic in
G
, and the master variable
u_{G}
is a first–order background differential operator applied to
W_{G}
. The charge–free condition remains essential because it removes the stationary zero modes and makes the middle component uniquely normalized on each sphere.

4.

Prove the no–loss spacetime reconstruction estimate.
Establish (
1.5
). This is the finite–time heart of Theorem
1.9
. One has to show that the non–degenerate energy, Morawetz bulk, and far–field weighted norms of the full Maxwell tensor are controlled by the master norm of
u_{G}
and the initial tensorial energy, with no loss of top derivatives. The difficulty is that the middle components of the Maxwell field are recovered only through coupled transport and elliptic equations, so the estimate must remain uniform at the horizon, through the trapping region, and in the far field simultaneously.

5.

Identify a coercive tensorial physical–space norm.
Define
\|G\|_{\mathcal{X}_{\mathrm{Max},0}^{(k)}([0,\tau])}
so that the left–hand side of (
1.5
) dominates the actual physical quantities appearing in Theorem
1.10
: non–degenerate slice energy, integrated local energy decay, and outgoing
r^{p}
fluxes. This step converts finite–time reconstruction into the tensorial decay statement.

6.

Close the Sobolev step.
Verify that
\mathcal{X}_{\mathrm{Max},0}^{(k)}
controls enough local derivatives of the recovered Maxwell tensor to run Sobolev on the slices
\Sigma_{\tau}
and deduce polynomial pointwise decay. This is not automatic from the scalar theory because one needs pointwise control of the middle Maxwell components after reconstruction.

7.

Construct the master radiation fields.
Show that every finite–energy master solution admits asymptotic traces on
\mathcal{I}^{\pm}\cup\mathcal{H}^{\pm}
, and that the Cauchy–to–radiation map is bounded on the master energy space. This uses the
r^{p}
hierarchy and the horizon flux bounds from Theorem
1.8
, but it also requires a precise identification of the asymptotic topology in which the traces are taken.

8.

Solve the master final–state problem.
Given admissible master radiation data on
\mathcal{I}^{\pm}\cup\mathcal{H}^{\pm}
, construct a unique finite–energy master solution realizing those data. This is the asymptotic completeness step at the master level and is the exact analytic content of the wave operators invoked in the scattering clause of Theorem
1.9
.

9.

Reconstruct the asymptotic Maxwell fields.
Prove that the Maxwell radiation fields are bounded linear images of the master radiation data, invertible on the charge–free sector, and compatible with the finite–time reconstruction map from Steps 2–4. This is the asymptotic analogue of (
1.5
) and is the only way to transfer scattering from the master equation back to the full tensorial field.

10.

Transfer scattering from the master system to Maxwell.
Combine Steps 7–9 with the finite–time reconstruction estimate to show that the Maxwell Cauchy–to–radiation map is an isomorphism and that the Maxwell final–state problem is well posed. At that point every clause of Theorem
1.9
is verified.

We now carry out Steps 1–10 in full detail, in that order. Step 1 packages the canonical master norm. Step 2 constructs the dynamic finite–time reconstruction operator from the master solution and the exact radiative trace on
\Sigma_{0}
to the full charge–free Maxwell tensor. Step 3 compares both that radiative trace and the full initial master control quantity with a concrete Maxwell initial norm on
\Sigma_{0}\cup N_{0}^{R}
. Steps 4–6 then establish the no–loss spacetime reconstruction estimate, identify the final tensorial physical–space norm, and close the Sobolev argument. Finally, Steps 7–10 construct the master radiation fields, solve the master final–state problem, reconstruct the Maxwell radiation fields asymptotically, and transfer scattering from the master system back to the full charge–free Maxwell tensor.

The genuinely core problem is therefore not the scalar decay estimate itself—that part is isolated in Theorem
1.8
—but the construction of a
no–loss finite–time and asymptotic reconstruction theory
from the master variable to the full charge–free Maxwell two–form. On Kerr–Newman this must be done in a geometry where redshift, trapping, and rotation/charge couplings are all present at once. Accordingly, the decisive steps are Step 4 (tensorial no–loss reconstruction) and Steps 7–9 (master scattering and asymptotic reconstruction), all of which must hold uniformly on the full sub–extremal parameter range.

Proposition 4.39

(Sourced
r^{p}
hierarchy for the master equation)
.

Assume the hypotheses of Theorem
1.8
. Let
u
solve (
4.138
). Then for every
0\leq p\leq 2
and every
\tau\geq 0
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{p,R}^{(k)}[u](\tau)+\mathcal{F}_{p,R}^{(k)}[u](0,\tau)+\mathcal{B}_{p,R}^{(k)}[u](0,\tau)\leq C_{k,p}\Big(\mathcal{E}_{p,R}^{(k)}[u](0)+\mathcal{E}_{0}^{(k)}[u](0)+\|\mathcal{G}\|_{\mathcal{N}_{0}^{*}}(0,\tau)^{2}\Big).

(4.141)

In particular, for
p=2
,

\displaystyle\sup_{0\leq s\leq\tau}\mathcal{E}_{2,R}^{(k)}[u](s)+\mathcal{F}_{2,R}^{(k)}[u](0,\tau)

(4.142)

\displaystyle\qquad+\mathcal{B}_{2,R}^{(k)}[u](0,\tau)\leq C_{k}\Big(\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{C},0}^{(k)}[u](0)+\|\mathcal{G}\|_{\mathcal{N}_{0}^{*}}(0,\tau)^{2}\Big).

(4.143)

Proof.

Fix
p\in[0,2]
. For each hidden–symmetry level
n\geq 0
define

\displaystyle\mathfrak{R}_{n,p}^{\mathrm{src}}[u](\tau):=\sum_{I\in\mathcal{J}_{n}}\Big(\mathcal{E}_{p,R}[\Psi_{I}](\tau)+\mathcal{F}_{p,R}[\Psi_{I}](0,\tau)+\mathcal{B}_{p,R}[\Psi_{I}](0,\tau)\Big).

We claim that for every
n\leq k
,

\displaystyle\sum_{m=0}^{n}\mathfrak{R}_{m,p}^{\mathrm{src}}[u](\tau)\leq C_{n,p}\Big(\mathcal{E}_{p,R}^{(n)}[u](0)+\mathcal{E}_{0}^{(n)}[u](0)+\|\mathcal{G}\|_{\mathcal{N}_{0}^{*}}(0,\tau)^{2}\Big).

(4.144)

Since the left–hand side is exactly the order–
n
weighted hierarchy, this gives (
4.141
) when
n=k
.

Base case
n=0
.

Apply the one–level estimate (
4.112
) to
v=u
,
H=\mathcal{G}
, and
\Phi=\Psi
. This gives

\displaystyle\mathfrak{R}_{0,p}^{\mathrm{src}}[u](\tau)\leq C_{p}\Big(\mathcal{E}_{p,R}[\Psi](0)+\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{far}}[u](0,\tau)+\|\mathcal{G}\|_{\mathcal{N}_{p,R}^{*}}(0,\tau)^{2}\Big).

The far bulk is one component of the Step 10 Morawetz quantity, so Proposition
4.37
yields

\displaystyle\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{far}}[u](0,\tau)\leq\mathcal{M}_{0}^{(0)}[u](0,\tau)\leq C\Big(\mathcal{E}_{0}^{(0)}[u](0)+\|\mathcal{G}\|_{\mathcal{N}_{0}^{*}}(0,\tau)^{2}\Big).

By Lemma
4.32
,

\|\mathcal{G}\|_{\mathcal{N}_{p,R}^{*}}(0,\tau)^{2}\leq C_{p,R}\,\|\mathcal{G}\|_{\mathcal{N}_{0}^{*}}(0,\tau)^{2}.

Substituting these estimates proves (
4.144
) at level
n=0
.

Induction step.

Assume (
4.144
) holds up to level
n-1
, and let
I\in\mathcal{J}_{n}
. Commuting (
4.138
) gives

(\Box_{g_{\mathrm{KN}}}+\mathcal{V}_{M})u_{I}=Z^{I}\mathcal{G}+G_{I},

where
G_{I}
is the triangular commutator source from Corollary
4.3
. Applying (
4.112
) to
v=u_{I}
,
H=Z^{I}\mathcal{G}+G_{I}
, and
\Phi=\Psi_{I}
yields

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{p,R}[\Psi_{I}](\tau)+\mathcal{F}_{p,R}[\Psi_{I}](0,\tau)+\mathcal{B}_{p,R}[\Psi_{I}](0,\tau)

\displaystyle\qquad\leq C_{p}\Big(\mathcal{E}_{p,R}[\Psi_{I}](0)+\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{far}}[u_{I}](0,\tau)+\|Z^{I}\mathcal{G}+G_{I}\|_{\mathcal{N}_{p,R}^{*}}(0,\tau)^{2}\Big).

Using
(a+b)^{2}\leq 2a^{2}+2b^{2}
and summing over
I\in\mathcal{J}_{n}
gives

\displaystyle\mathfrak{R}_{n,p}^{\mathrm{src}}[u](\tau)\leq C_{n,p}\Big(

\displaystyle\sum_{I\in\mathcal{J}_{n}}\mathcal{E}_{p,R}[\Psi_{I}](0)+\sum_{I\in\mathcal{J}_{n}}\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{far}}[u_{I}](0,\tau)

\displaystyle+\sum_{I\in\mathcal{J}_{n}}\|Z^{I}\mathcal{G}\|_{\mathcal{N}_{p,R}^{*}}(0,\tau)^{2}+\sum_{I\in\mathcal{J}_{n}}\|G_{I}\|_{\mathcal{N}_{p,R}^{*}}(0,\tau)^{2}\Big).

The far bulk term is again part of the Step 10 Morawetz quantity, so Proposition
4.37
gives

\displaystyle\sum_{I\in\mathcal{J}_{n}}\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{far}}[u_{I}](0,\tau)\leq\mathcal{M}_{0}^{(n)}[u](0,\tau)\leq C_{n}\Big(\mathcal{E}_{0}^{(n)}[u](0)+\|\mathcal{G}\|_{\mathcal{N}_{0}^{*}}(0,\tau)^{2}\Big).

The directly commuted source is controlled by Lemma
4.32
:

\sum_{I\in\mathcal{J}_{n}}\|Z^{I}\mathcal{G}\|_{\mathcal{N}_{p,R}^{*}}(0,\tau)^{2}\leq C_{n,p}\,\|\mathcal{G}\|_{\mathcal{N}_{0}^{*}}(0,\tau)^{2}.

Finally, the proof of Lemma
4.28
is purely algebraic in the commutator structure and does not use homogeneity of the underlying solution. Hence the same argument gives

\displaystyle\sum_{I\in\mathcal{J}_{n}}\|G_{I}\|_{\mathcal{N}_{p,R}^{*}}(0,\tau)^{2}\leq C_{n,p,R}\sum_{|J|_{*}\leq n-1}\mathcal{B}_{p,R}[\Psi_{J}](0,\tau)\leq C_{n,p,R}\sum_{m=0}^{n-1}\mathfrak{R}_{m,p}^{\mathrm{src}}[u](\tau).

Substituting these bounds into the previous inequality yields

\displaystyle\mathfrak{R}_{n,p}^{\mathrm{src}}[u](\tau)\leq C_{n,p}\Big(\mathcal{E}_{p,R}^{(n)}[u](0)+\mathcal{E}_{0}^{(n)}[u](0)+\|\mathcal{G}\|_{\mathcal{N}_{0}^{*}}(0,\tau)^{2}+\sum_{m=0}^{n-1}\mathfrak{R}_{m,p}^{\mathrm{src}}[u](\tau)\Big).

The induction hypothesis absorbs the lower–order shell sum and proves (
4.144
) at level
n
. This closes the induction and yields (
4.141
).

For (
4.142
), apply (
4.141
) with
p=2
on every interval
[0,s]
, take the supremum over
0\leq s\leq\tau
, and use the identity

\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{C},0}^{(k)}[u](0)=\mathcal{E}_{0}^{(k)}[u](0)+\mathcal{E}_{2,R}^{(k)}[u](0)

from (
4.126
). This proves the stated
p=2
bound.
∎

Proposition 4.40

(Step 1 in the proof of Theorem
1.9
: canonical master norm)
.

Assume the hypotheses of Theorem
1.8
. For every
\tau\geq 0
define

\displaystyle\|u\|_{\mathcal{X}_{M,0}^{(k)}([0,\tau])}^{2}:=

\displaystyle\ \sup_{0\leq s\leq\tau}\mathcal{E}_{0}^{(k)}[u](s)+\mathcal{M}_{0}^{(k)}[u](0,\tau)

(4.145)

\displaystyle+\sup_{0\leq s\leq\tau}\mathcal{E}_{2,R}^{(k)}[u](s)+\mathcal{B}_{2,R}^{(k)}[u](0,\tau)+\mathcal{F}_{2,R}^{(k)}[u](0,\tau).

(4.146)

Then
\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{X}_{M,0}^{(k)}([0,\tau])}
is a norm on the space of smooth master solutions on the slab
[0,\tau]
, and it is equivalent to the full Step 10 package in the sense that

\displaystyle\|u\|_{\mathcal{X}_{M,0}^{(k)}([0,\tau])}^{2}\simeq

\displaystyle\ \sup_{0\leq s\leq\tau}\mathcal{E}_{0}^{(k)}[u](s)+\mathcal{M}_{0}^{(k)}[u](0,\tau)+\sup_{0\leq s\leq\tau}\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{C},0}^{(k)}[u](s)

(4.147)

\displaystyle+\sup_{0\leq p\leq 2}\Big(\sup_{0\leq s\leq\tau}\mathcal{E}_{p,R}^{(k)}[u](s)+\mathcal{B}_{p,R}^{(k)}[u](0,\tau)+\mathcal{F}_{p,R}^{(k)}[u](0,\tau)\Big).

(4.148)

Moreover, if
u
solves (
4.138
), then the estimate (
1.3
) holds. In particular, item (1) of Theorem
1.9
is proved.

Proof.

Non–negativity is immediate from the definitions. If

\|u\|_{\mathcal{X}_{M,0}^{(k)}([0,\tau])}=0,

then in particular

\sup_{0\leq s\leq\tau}\mathcal{E}_{0}^{(k)}[u](s)=0.

Since
\mathcal{E}_{0}^{(k)}
is non–degenerate, every commuted field
Z^{I}u
,
|I|_{*}\leq k
, vanishes on every slice
\Sigma_{s}
, hence
u\equiv 0
on the slab. Therefore (
4.145
) defines a genuine norm.

For the equivalence (
4.147
), first use the identity

\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{C},0}^{(k)}[u](s)=\mathcal{E}_{0}^{(k)}[u](s)+\mathcal{E}_{2,R}^{(k)}[u](s)

from (
4.126
). It follows that

\displaystyle\sup_{0\leq s\leq\tau}\mathcal{E}_{2,R}^{(k)}[u](s)\leq\sup_{0\leq s\leq\tau}\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{C},0}^{(k)}[u](s)\leq\sup_{0\leq s\leq\tau}\mathcal{E}_{0}^{(k)}[u](s)

\displaystyle\qquad+\sup_{0\leq s\leq\tau}\mathcal{E}_{2,R}^{(k)}[u](s).

Next, for
0\leq p\leq 2
and
r\geq R
,

\displaystyle r^{p}\leq r^{2},

\displaystyle r^{p-2}\leq 1,

\displaystyle r^{p-1}\leq r,

\displaystyle r^{p-3}\leq r^{-1}.

Using the definitions (
4.101
)–(
4.103
), these pointwise inequalities imply

\mathcal{E}_{p,R}^{(k)}[u](s)\leq C_{R}\,\mathcal{E}_{2,R}^{(k)}[u](s),

\mathcal{B}_{p,R}^{(k)}[u](0,\tau)\leq C_{R}\,\mathcal{B}_{2,R}^{(k)}[u](0,\tau),

and

\mathcal{F}_{p,R}^{(k)}[u](0,\tau)\leq C_{R}\,\mathcal{F}_{2,R}^{(k)}[u](0,\tau).

Conversely, the
p=2
terms are themselves members of the family on the right–hand side of (
4.147
). This proves the claimed equivalence.

To prove (
1.3
), apply Proposition
4.37
on each interval
[0,s]
and take the supremum over
0\leq s\leq\tau
. Since
\mathcal{M}_{0}^{(k)}[u](0,s)
is monotone in
s
,

\displaystyle\sup_{0\leq s\leq\tau}\mathcal{E}_{0}^{(k)}[u](s)

\displaystyle\qquad+\mathcal{M}_{0}^{(k)}[u](0,\tau)\leq C\Big(\mathcal{E}_{0}^{(k)}[u](0)+\|\mathcal{G}\|_{\mathcal{N}_{0}^{*}}(0,\tau)^{2}\Big).

Likewise, Proposition
4.39
at
p=2
gives

\displaystyle\sup_{0\leq s\leq\tau}\mathcal{E}_{2,R}^{(k)}[u](s)+\mathcal{B}_{2,R}^{(k)}[u](0,\tau)

\displaystyle\qquad+\mathcal{F}_{2,R}^{(k)}[u](0,\tau)\leq C\Big(\mathcal{E}_{2,R}^{(k)}[u](0)+\mathcal{E}_{0}^{(k)}[u](0)+\|\mathcal{G}\|_{\mathcal{N}_{0}^{*}}(0,\tau)^{2}\Big).

Adding the last two inequalities and using

\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{C},0}^{(k)}[u](0)=\mathcal{E}_{0}^{(k)}[u](0)+\mathcal{E}_{2,R}^{(k)}[u](0)

yields (
1.3
). This is exactly Step 1 of Remark
4.38
.
∎

Remark 4.41

(What Step 1 leaves open)
.

Proposition
4.40
removes the master–norm clause from the conditional part of the argument. The next task is Step 2: construct the dynamic finite–time reconstruction map from the master solution and the exact radiative trace on
\Sigma_{0}
to the exact charge–free Maxwell field. We now do this. Step 3 will then compare that radiative trace with the Maxwell Cauchy data.

Step 2: quantitative finite–time reconstruction of the exact radiative variables and the Maxwell tensor.

The logical role of Step 2 is to fix the
dynamic
reconstruction on a finite slab. The only quantity on the reconstruction side not yet absorbed into the master norm is the exact radiative trace on
\Sigma_{0}
; Step 3 is precisely the argument that compares this trace with the Maxwell Cauchy energy. We therefore write the finite–time part of the admissible reduction in the transport/elliptic form used in Proposition
4.17
. By Proposition
3.3
, the charge–free hypothesis removes the spherewise harmonic mean mode, so every elliptic inversion below takes place on the mean–zero sector.

Lemma 4.42

(Algebraic tensor inversion from the exact radiative scalars)
.

Let
G
be a smooth real two–form on
\overline{\mathcal{D}}
, and let
(\phi_{0},\phi_{1},\phi_{2})
be its Newman–Penrose scalars with respect to the principal tetrad (
3.9
). Then there is a unique smooth real bundle map

\mathfrak{A}_{\mathrm{NP}}:\overline{\mathcal{D}}\times\mathbb{C}^{3}\longrightarrow\Lambda^{2}T^{*}\overline{\mathcal{D}},

whose coefficients are stationary and axisymmetric, such that

G=\mathfrak{A}_{\mathrm{NP}}(\phi_{0},\phi_{1},\phi_{2}).

Equivalently, the exact radiative scalars determine the Maxwell two–form algebraically at each point of the exterior.

Proof.

Fix
p\in\overline{\mathcal{D}}
. The real vector space
\Lambda^{2}T_{p}^{*}\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{KN}}
has dimension six. Relative to the tetrad coframe
(l^{\flat},n^{\flat},m^{\flat},\bar{m}^{\flat})
, a real two–form
G_{p}
is determined by the six real quantities

\Re\phi_{0},\quad\Im\phi_{0},\quad\Re\phi_{1},\quad\Im\phi_{1},\quad\Re\phi_{2},\quad\Im\phi_{2}.

Indeed,

\phi_{0}=G_{p}(l,m),\qquad\phi_{2}=G_{p}(\bar{m},n),

while the reality of
G_{p}
implies that
G_{p}(l,n)
is real and
G_{p}(\bar{m},m)
is purely imaginary, so from

2\phi_{1}=G_{p}(l,n)+G_{p}(\bar{m},m)

one recovers both middle tetrad components separately. Thus the map

G_{p}\longmapsto(\phi_{0},\phi_{1},\phi_{2})

is a real linear isomorphism from the six–dimensional space
\Lambda^{2}T_{p}^{*}\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{KN}}
onto the six real degrees of freedom encoded by
(\phi_{0},\phi_{1},\phi_{2})
. Its inverse depends smoothly on
p
because the principal tetrad is smooth on
\overline{\mathcal{D}}
. This gives the required bundle map
\mathfrak{A}_{\mathrm{NP}}
. Since the tetrad coefficients depend only on
(r,\theta)
, the coefficients of
\mathfrak{A}_{\mathrm{NP}}
are stationary and axisymmetric.
∎

Proposition 4.43

(Step 2 in the proof of Theorem
1.9
: quantitative finite–time reconstruction map)
.

Assume the hypotheses of Theorem
1.8
and fix the scalar short–range radiative reduction furnished by Theorem
1.1
. Let
G
be a smooth charge–free Maxwell field with conserved source, let
u_{G}
be the associated master variable from Proposition
4.1
, and define the exact horizon–regular radiative triple

\displaystyle W_{G}:=(\psi_{+},\phi_{1},\psi_{-}),

\displaystyle\psi_{+}:=\Gamma^{2}\phi_{0},

\displaystyle\psi_{-}:=\bar{\Gamma}^{2}\phi_{2}.

Write
W_{G,0}:=W_{G}|_{\Sigma_{0}}
. Then for every
\tau\geq 0
there is a unique linear finite–time reconstruction operator

\mathscr{R}_{\tau,\mathrm{rad}}:(u_{G},W_{G,0})\longmapsto W[u_{G},W_{G,0}]

with the following properties.

1.

Dynamic reconstruction on the slab.
The field
W[u_{G},W_{G,0}]
is obtained by solving, in triangular order, a closed family of stationary–axisymmetric transport and sphere–elliptic equations with smooth coefficients on
\overline{\mathcal{D}(0,\tau)}
. Every elliptic component lies in the mean–zero sector on each sphere
S_{s,r}
. The output coincides with the genuine exact radiative triple of
G
:

W[u_{G},W_{G,0}]=W_{G}.

2.

Same–order quantitative estimate.
For every integer
k\geq 0
,

\displaystyle\|W_{G}\|_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{rad}}^{(k)}([0,\tau])}^{2}\leq C_{k}\Big(E_{\mathrm{rad}}^{(k)}[W_{G}](0)+\|u_{G}\|_{\mathcal{X}_{M,0}^{(k)}([0,\tau])}^{2}\Big).

(4.149)

The only term on the right not already written in master variables is the initial radiative size
E_{\mathrm{rad}}^{(k)}[W_{G}](0)
; Step 3 of Remark
4.38
is exactly the comparison of that term with the Maxwell Cauchy energy.

3.

Algebraic tensor recovery with no derivative loss.
Define

\mathscr{R}_{\tau,\mathrm{Max}}(u_{G},W_{G,0}):=\mathfrak{A}_{\mathrm{NP}}\big(\Gamma^{-2}\psi_{+},\phi_{1},\bar{\Gamma}^{-2}\psi_{-}\big),

where
\mathfrak{A}_{\mathrm{NP}}
is the bundle map from Lemma
4.42
. Then

\mathscr{R}_{\tau,\mathrm{Max}}(u_{G},W_{G,0})=G\qquad\text{on }\mathcal{D}(0,\tau),

and for every
I\in\mathcal{I}_{k}
,

\displaystyle|Z^{I}G|\leq C_{k}\sum_{|J|_{*}\leq|I|_{*}}\Big(|\Gamma|^{-2}|Z^{J}\psi_{+}|+|Z^{J}\phi_{1}|+|\Gamma|^{-2}|Z^{J}\psi_{-}|\Big).

(4.150)

Hence the finite–time reconstruction from the master solution to the full Maxwell tensor is exact, horizon regular, and uses no derivatives above order
k
.

Proof.

By the finite–time reconstruction clause in Theorem
1.1
, organized in the transport/elliptic form used in Proposition
4.17
, the exact triple
(\phi_{0},\phi_{1},\phi_{2})
and hence
W_{G}=(\psi_{+},\phi_{1},\psi_{-})
obey a closed linear reconstruction system driven by the scalar master solution
u_{G}
. Proposition
3.3
removes the spherewise harmonic mean mode, so every elliptic inversion in that system takes place on the mean–zero sector and is therefore uniquely solvable.

To construct
\mathscr{R}_{\tau,\mathrm{rad}}
, solve the reconstruction system in the triangular order. For a transport component, one solves a linear first–order equation along the integral curves of the uniformly transversal causal vector field appearing in the system, using the prescribed initial trace on
\Sigma_{0}
. For an elliptic component, once the lower components are already known, one solves the corresponding sphere equation on each
S_{s,r}
in the mean–zero class; uniqueness follows from the trivial kernel on that sector. Since every equation in the system is linear, the resulting map
(u_{G},W_{G,0})\mapsto W[u_{G},W_{G,0}]
is linear. The genuine exact radiative triple
W_{G}
satisfies the same reconstruction system with the same initial trace
W_{G,0}
, so uniqueness forces

W[u_{G},W_{G,0}]=W_{G}.

This proves the first assertion.

For the estimate, apply Proposition
4.17
with
U_{M}=u_{G}
and
W_{M}=W_{G}
. This gives

\|W_{G}\|_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{rad}}^{(k)}([0,\tau])}^{2}\leq C_{k}\Big(E_{\mathrm{rad}}^{(k)}[W_{G}](0)+\|u_{G}\|_{\mathcal{Y}_{M}^{(k)}([0,\tau])}^{2}\Big).

By Step 10, one has
\mathcal{E}_{0}^{(k)}=E_{N}^{(k)}
and
\mathcal{M}_{0}^{(k)}
equal to the sum of the redshift, local, and far bulks entering (
4.57
). Hence
\|u_{G}\|_{\mathcal{Y}_{M}^{(k)}([0,\tau])}^{2}
is a sub–sum of the canonical master norm (
4.145
), so Proposition
4.40
implies

\|u_{G}\|_{\mathcal{Y}_{M}^{(k)}([0,\tau])}^{2}\leq C_{k}\,\|u_{G}\|_{\mathcal{X}_{M,0}^{(k)}([0,\tau])}^{2}.

Substituting this into the previous inequality proves (
4.149
).

Finally, Lemma
4.42
gives the algebraic identity

G=\mathfrak{A}_{\mathrm{NP}}\big(\Gamma^{-2}\psi_{+},\phi_{1},\bar{\Gamma}^{-2}\psi_{-}\big).

Because
r\geq r_{+}>0
, the functions
\Gamma^{-1}
and
\bar{\Gamma}^{-1}
are smooth and bounded on
\overline{\mathcal{D}}
. The coefficients of
\mathfrak{A}_{\mathrm{NP}}
are likewise smooth, stationary, and axisymmetric. Therefore
T
and
\Phi
commute with every coefficient, while commuting the Carter operator
\mathcal{O}
through multiplication by any such smooth coefficient produces only lower–order terms. Repeated Leibniz expansion thus yields, for every
I\in\mathcal{I}_{k}
,

Z^{I}\big(fw\big)=\sum_{|J|_{*}\leq|I|_{*}}f_{I,J}\,Z^{J}w

for smooth bounded coefficients
f_{I,J}
depending on finitely many derivatives of
f
. Applying this to each coefficient in the algebraic formula for
G
proves (
4.150
). Since all coefficients are smooth on the closure of the exterior, the reconstruction is horizon regular. This is exactly Step 2 of Remark
4.38
.
∎

Remark 4.44

(After Step 2, what still remains)
.

Propositions
4.40
and
4.43
show that item (1) of Theorem
1.9
and the dynamic part of item (2) are now established: the master norm is canonical, and the finite–time reconstruction operator from the master solution to the exact charge–free Maxwell tensor is explicit, horizon regular, and same–order. The next unresolved step is Step 3, namely the initial comparison estimate (
1.4
). After that one must prove the full spacetime bound (
1.5
), identify a coercive tensorial norm, close the Sobolev argument, and then carry out the scattering steps.

Step 3: initial comparison on
\Sigma_{0}\cup N_{0}^{R}
.

The last quantity left open by Step 2 is the size of the exact radiative trace on the initial hypersurfaces. Since the exact radiative triple
W_{G}
is algebraically equivalent to the full Maxwell tensor and the fixed scalar master variable is obtained from
W_{G}
by a first–order background differential operator, Step 3 is a finite order count. The natural Maxwell initial norm is therefore a weighted
H^{2}
–type norm of
W_{G}
on the spacelike slice together with the corresponding weighted null trace norm on
N_{0}^{R}
.

Proposition 4.45

(Step 3 in the proof of Theorem
1.9
: initial comparison estimate)
.

Assume the hypotheses of Theorem
1.8
and fix the scalar short–range reduction furnished by Theorem
1.1
. Let
G
be a smooth charge–free Maxwell field with conserved source, let
u_{G}
be the associated master variable from Proposition
4.1
, and let

W_{G}=(\psi_{+},\phi_{1},\psi_{-})

be the exact radiative triple from Proposition
4.43
. For a scalar
f
on
\Sigma_{0}
define

\|f\|_{H^{2}(\Sigma_{0})}^{2}:=\sum_{j=0}^{2}\|\nabla_{\Sigma_{0}}^{j}f\|_{L^{2}(\Sigma_{0})}^{2},

and for a scalar
f
on
N_{0}^{R}
define

\displaystyle\|f\|_{\mathfrak{H}_{2,R}^{2}(N_{0}^{R})}^{2}:=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}a,b\geq 0\\
a+b\leq 1\end{subarray}}\int_{N_{0}^{R}}\Big(r^{2}|\partial_{v}^{a+1}\nabla_{S^{2}}^{b}f|^{2}+|\partial_{v}^{a}\nabla_{S^{2}}^{b+1}f|^{2}+|\partial_{v}^{a}\nabla_{S^{2}}^{b}f|^{2}\Big)\,dv\,d\omega.

For the triple
W_{G}
, sum these norms componentwise, and define

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{Max},0}^{(k)}[G](0):=\sum_{|I|_{*}\leq k}\Big(\|Z^{I}W_{G}\|_{H^{2}(\Sigma_{0})}^{2}+\|Z^{I}W_{G}\|_{\mathfrak{H}_{2,R}^{2}(N_{0}^{R})}^{2}\Big).

(4.151)

Then
\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{Max},0}^{(k)}
is a non–degenerate norm on the charge–free Maxwell sector, equivalent to a weighted tensorial
H^{k+2}
–type norm of
G
on
\Sigma_{0}\cup N_{0}^{R}
. Moreover, for every integer
k\geq 0
,

\displaystyle E_{\mathrm{rad}}^{(k)}[W_{G}](0)+\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{C},0}^{(k)}[u_{G}](0)\leq C_{k}\,\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{Max},0}^{(k)}[G](0).

(4.152)

In particular,

\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{C},0}^{(k)}[u_{G}](0)\leq C_{k}\,\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{Max},0}^{(k)}[G](0),

(4.153)

which is exactly the initial comparison clause (
1.4
) for this concrete choice of Maxwell initial norm.

Proof.

The exact definitions of
\phi_{0}
,
\phi_{1}
,
\phi_{2}
, and
\psi_{\pm}
give a smooth stationary–axisymmetric bundle map

\mathfrak{B}_{\mathrm{NP}}:\Lambda^{2}T^{*}\overline{\mathcal{D}}\longrightarrow\overline{\mathcal{D}}\times\mathbb{C}^{3}

such that

W_{G}=\mathfrak{B}_{\mathrm{NP}}(G).

Lemma
4.42
gives the inverse bundle map
\mathfrak{A}_{\mathrm{NP}}
. Consequently
W_{G}
and
G
are algebraically equivalent pointwise. Because the coefficients of both bundle maps are smooth, stationary, and axisymmetric, with only the fixed polynomial weights carried by the
\Gamma^{\pm 2}
factors, commuting with
T
,
\Phi
,
\mathcal{O}
, and with the tangential derivatives
\nabla_{\Sigma_{0}}
,
\partial_{v}
,
\nabla_{S^{2}}
produces only finite linear combinations of the same type with coefficients of the same fixed polynomial class. Therefore the norm (
4.151
) is non–degenerate and equivalent to a weighted tensorial
H^{k+2}
–type norm of
G
on
\Sigma_{0}\cup N_{0}^{R}
.

Since

E_{\mathrm{rad}}^{(k)}[W_{G}](0)=\sum_{|I|_{*}\leq k}\|Z^{I}W_{G}\|_{L^{2}(\Sigma_{0})}^{2},

it is exactly the
j=0
part of the slice term in (
4.151
). Hence

E_{\mathrm{rad}}^{(k)}[W_{G}](0)\leq\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{Max},0}^{(k)}[G](0).

(4.154)

By Theorem
1.1
in the scalar case there is a first–order background differential operator
\mathfrak{D}_{M}
with smooth stationary–axisymmetric coefficients such that

u_{G}=\mathfrak{D}_{M}W_{G}.

Commuting this identity with
Z^{I}
and differentiating tangentially along
\Sigma_{0}
gives, by repeated Leibniz expansion,

\|Z^{I}u_{G}\|_{H^{1}(\Sigma_{0})}\leq C_{k}\sum_{|J|_{*}\leq|I|_{*}}\|Z^{J}W_{G}\|_{H^{2}(\Sigma_{0})}.

Because the multiplier
N
defining the non–degenerate energy is fixed and uniformly timelike on
\Sigma_{0}
, the slice flux
E_{N}^{(k)}[u_{G}](0)
is uniformly equivalent to the sum of these
H^{1}
norms. Therefore

E_{N}^{(k)}[u_{G}](0)\leq C_{k}\,\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{Max},0}^{(k)}[G](0).

(4.155)

Set
\Psi_{I}:=r\,Z^{I}u_{G}
on
N_{0}^{R}
. Since
\mathfrak{D}_{M}
is first order and the chosen short–range reduction is fixed, the coefficients of
r\mathfrak{D}_{M}
together with their first
\partial_{v}
and
\nabla_{S^{2}}
derivatives obey the same fixed far–field growth bounds throughout
N_{0}^{R}
. Hence
\Psi_{I}
,
\partial_{v}\Psi_{I}
, and
\nabla_{S^{2}}\Psi_{I}
are finite linear combinations, with coefficients in that same fixed class, of terms
\partial_{v}^{a}\nabla_{S^{2}}^{b}Z^{J}W_{G}
with
a+b\leq 2
and
|J|_{*}\leq|I|_{*}
. Using the definition (
4.101
) of the weighted null flux, we obtain

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{2,R}^{(k)}[u_{G}](0)\leq C_{k}\sum_{|I|_{*}\leq k}\|Z^{I}W_{G}\|_{\mathfrak{H}_{2,R}^{2}(N_{0}^{R})}^{2}\leq C_{k}\,

(4.156)

\displaystyle\qquad\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{Max},0}^{(k)}[G](0).

(4.157)

Adding (
4.155
) and (
4.156
), and recalling that

\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{C},0}^{(k)}[u_{G}](0)=E_{N}^{(k)}[u_{G}](0)+\mathcal{E}_{2,R}^{(k)}[u_{G}](0),

yields

\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{C},0}^{(k)}[u_{G}](0)\leq C_{k}\,\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{Max},0}^{(k)}[G](0).

Combining this with (
4.154
) proves (
4.152
), and (
4.153
) is its second summand. This is exactly Step 3 of Remark
4.38
.
∎

Remark 4.46

(After Step 3, what still remains)
.

Propositions
4.40
,
4.43
, and
4.45
show that item (1) of Theorem
1.9
and the whole initial–data part of item (2) are now explicit: the master norm is canonical, the finite–time reconstruction operator is fixed, and the initial radiative/master quantities are controlled by a concrete Maxwell norm. The first unresolved step is therefore Step 4, namely the no–loss spacetime reconstruction estimate (
1.5
). After that one must identify a coercive tensorial norm, close the Sobolev argument, and then carry out the scattering steps.

Step 4: the no–loss spacetime reconstruction estimate.

After Step 3 the only finite–time quantity not yet controlled is the full spacetime size of the reconstructed Maxwell field. The right norm is the one that simultaneously records the exact radiative variables and the master solution from which they are recovered. This keeps the derivative count explicit and makes the eventual tensorial coercivity transparent.

Definition 4.47

(Finite–time reconstruction norm on charge–free Maxwell fields)
.

Let
G
be a smooth charge–free Maxwell field, let
u_{G}
be the associated master variable, and let

W_{G}=(\psi_{+},\phi_{1},\psi_{-})

be the exact horizon–regular radiative triple from Proposition
4.43
. For every integer
k\geq 0
and every
\tau\geq 0
define

\displaystyle\|G\|_{\mathcal{X}_{\mathrm{rec},0}^{(k)}([0,\tau])}^{2}:=\|W_{G}\|_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{rad}}^{(k)}([0,\tau])}^{2}

(4.158)

\displaystyle\qquad+\|u_{G}\|_{\mathcal{X}_{M,0}^{(k)}([0,\tau])}^{2}.

(4.159)

Proposition 4.48

(Step 4 in the proof of Theorem
1.9
: no–loss spacetime reconstruction estimate)
.

Assume the hypotheses of Theorem
1.8
and fix the scalar short–range reduction furnished by Theorem
1.1
. Let
G
be a smooth charge–free Maxwell field. Then for every integer
k\geq 0
and every
\tau\geq 0
,

\displaystyle\|G\|_{\mathcal{X}_{\mathrm{rec},0}^{(k)}([0,\tau])}^{2}\leq C_{k}\Big(\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{Max},0}^{(k)}[G](0)+\|u_{G}\|_{\mathcal{X}_{M,0}^{(k)}([0,\tau])}^{2}\Big).

(4.160)

In particular, the reconstruction estimate (
1.5
) holds with
\ell_{\mathrm{rec}}=0
and with
\mathcal{X}_{\mathrm{Max},0}^{(k)}
replaced by
\mathcal{X}_{\mathrm{rec},0}^{(k)}
.

Proof.

By definition (
4.158
),

\displaystyle\|G\|_{\mathcal{X}_{\mathrm{rec},0}^{(k)}([0,\tau])}^{2}=\|W_{G}\|_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{rad}}^{(k)}([0,\tau])}^{2}

\displaystyle\qquad+\|u_{G}\|_{\mathcal{X}_{M,0}^{(k)}([0,\tau])}^{2}.

Proposition
4.43
gives

\|W_{G}\|_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{rad}}^{(k)}([0,\tau])}^{2}\leq C_{k}\Big(E_{\mathrm{rad}}^{(k)}[W_{G}](0)+\|u_{G}\|_{\mathcal{X}_{M,0}^{(k)}([0,\tau])}^{2}\Big).

By (
4.154
),

E_{\mathrm{rad}}^{(k)}[W_{G}](0)\leq\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{Max},0}^{(k)}[G](0).

Substituting this into the previous inequality and adding the explicit master term in (
4.158
) yields (
4.160
). The same derivative order
k
appears on both sides, so the reconstruction is genuinely no–loss. This is exactly Step 4 of Remark
4.38
.
∎

Step 5: choose a coercive tensorial physical–space norm.

The norm from Step 4 is still written in mixed radiative/master variables. We now show that it is in fact equivalent to a genuine tensorial physical–space norm of the Maxwell two–form and may therefore be adopted as the norm
\mathcal{X}_{\mathrm{Max},0}^{(k)}
appearing in Theorem
1.10
.

Proposition 4.49

(Step 5 in the proof of Theorem
1.9
: coercive tensorial physical–space norm)
.

For smooth charge–free Maxwell fields define

\|G\|_{\mathcal{X}_{\mathrm{Max},0}^{(k)}([0,\tau])}:=\|G\|_{\mathcal{X}_{\mathrm{rec},0}^{(k)}([0,\tau])}.

(4.161)

Then the following hold.

1.

Non–degeneracy and tensorial coercivity.
The norm (
4.161
) is non–degenerate on the charge–free Maxwell sector. Moreover it is equivalent to a weighted tensorial
H^{1}
–type physical–space norm of
G
on
\mathcal{D}(0,\tau)
.

2.

Physical–space content.
If one sets

\displaystyle E_{\mathrm{Max}}^{(k)}[G](s)

\displaystyle:=E_{\mathrm{rad}}^{(k)}[W_{G}](s),

(4.162)

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{Max},0}^{(k)}[G](0,\tau)
